Under exact behavior matching, we show that CIA can reward action information that leaves optimal inverse-regression error unchanged, and verify this distinction through a controlled learning example. CIA achieves higher planner-averaged mean success than the base world model and a regression inverse-dynamics baseline across five visual control benchmarks, without modifying the planner.


### arXiv 2611.32772 - RubikSQL-HB: Agentic Text-to-SQL with Knowledge in the Loop

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T15:06:50Z
- authors: Haorui Jia, Zihan Qu, Yundi Dong, Ziyang Mei, Chi Zhang, Zezhi Zheng
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32772

Database agents need more than schema structure: business meanings, stored values and reference queries supply knowledge that becomes relevant at different points in reasoning. We introduce RubikSQL-HB, which exposes these materials through queryable, database-specific knowledge workspaces built on the HeavenBase platform. Its knowledge interface remains available as an agent constructs and investigates an answer. Candidate agents interleave knowledge access with SQL construction and execution; a downstream Judge can reuse the same interfaces to investigate disagreements. A two-stage example interface separates discovering a reference question from expanding its SQL. On a 500-question BIRD Mini-Dev evaluation, the full pipeline reaches 75.2% execution accuracy, against 71.2% for first-candidate selection and 70.4% for execution-majority voting, with a correct candidate available on 79.0% of questions. The raw-knowledge condition, which also regenerates candidates without the built workspace, is 3.0 points lower, and adjudication with tools and revision accounts for most of the gain over selection baselines. A recorded execution probe shows how continued investigation resolves a candidate disagreement. Separate archived runs show semantic revision recovering answers absent from their original candidate pools. A versioned development study further illustrates how recurring corrections become reusable execution checks. The system and results connect knowledge-interface design to agent decision quality: queryable knowledge supports the construction of useful alternatives, and evidence-aware adjudication connects those alternatives to a final answer. Keeping knowledge in the loop lets agents acquire relevant observations as their reasoning develops.


### arXiv 2611.32765 - EviCraft: Can Minecraft Agents Find What They Cannot See?

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T15:03:47Z
- authors: Wenyou Yi, Lantian Zhang, Guanlin Chen, Xingqiu Xu, Haiyang Sun, Minrui Huang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32765

In Minecraft, a common testbed for embodied agents, semantic tools such as mineBlock pick targets from the simulator's loaded world state without a visibility test, so a buried ore the agent has never seen is one call away, and scores cannot show whether the agent searched. We formalize the missing requirement as evidence closure: every decision of an agent system, including those inside its tools, may depend on the world only through facts for which the agent holds evidence. EviCraft is a runtime and benchmark that enforces closure below the agent and makes the admission rule an experimental variable: a perception-grounded regime (G) admits only what is in line of sight or returned by the agent's own actions, and a loaded-state regime (L) reproduces standard tool stacks, with agents, tools, and worlds held fixed. On Survival, the regime effect exceeds the strongest agent's lead: under G, three mid-sized agents reach the diamond in 32%-40% of runs, against 76%-94% under L, and the strongest agent under G scores below each of them under L. In two models, removing only the line-of-sight test closes 93%-96% of the score gap, and loading state only inside tools recovers 70%-98%, so safeguards on observations and queries leave most of the leak open. In open-ended acquisition, the gain from L concentrates in items that depend on buried resources or mobs. Code and benchmark are available at https://github.com/AllenReder/EviCraft


### arXiv 2611.32762 - SGD with Large Learning Rates: General Analysis & Improved Rates

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, math.OC, stat.ML
- published: 2026-11-24T15:01:49Z
- authors: Konstantinos Emmanouilidis, Ziqing Xu, Salma Tarmoun, Lachlan MacDonald, Rene Vidal
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32762

Stochastic Gradient Descent lies at the core of modern machine learning. However its classical analysis typically restricts the stepsize to be below a curvature-dependent stability threshold. Recent developments in deterministic optimization have challenged this paradigm, showing that suitably chosen large stepsizes can not only preserve convergence, but also accelerate Gradient Descent (GD). Whether this phenomenon persists in the presence of stochastic gradient noise has remained an open question (Wu et al., 2024). In this work, we answer this question affirmatively for the cross-entropy loss with linear predictors and two-layer neural networks, establishing an accelerated $\mathcal{O}(T^{-2})$ convergence rate. At the core of our analysis is a fine-grained characterization of the stochastic large-stepsize dynamics. This sharper control of the trajectory further yields tight convergence guarantees within the stable regime, improving existing bounds even in the deterministic setting. Taken together, our results show that the acceleration mechanism induced by large stepsizes survives stochasticity and extends beyond linear models. Numerical experiments demonstrate the benefits of large learning rates in practice.


### arXiv 2611.32755 - ReQOR: Operator-Discrepancy Subspace Repair for Quantization-Conditioned Backdoors in Large Language Models

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-24T15:00:23Z
- authors: Wonjune Seo, Minkyung Park, Saerom Park
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32755

Quantization-Conditioned Backdoors (QCBs) cause large language models (LLMs) to exhibit malicious behavior after quantization while remaining benign at high precision. Existing defenses often rely on security-guided configuration selection or iterative optimization, but do not identify which quantization-induced changes should be repaired. We isolate module-local quantization effects from upstream drift by evaluating the high-precision and quantized versions of each module on identical states from the quantized inference trajectory, and formulate QCB defense as repairing the resulting same-state operator discrepancies. We propose ReQOR, a training-free defense that identifies the dominant directions of these discrepancies on benign calibration data and restores the full-precision computation along them in closed form, yielding a factorized residual adapter. ReQOR uses neither attack information nor gradient-based optimization and supports both full-span and low-rank repair. Experiments across four LLMs, three PGD-based QCB attacks, three malicious behaviors, and three quantization formats show substantial QCB mitigation while largely preserving benign utility. In additional experiments on a non-PGD outlier-injection attack, existing baselines largely fail, whereas ReQOR remains effective. Further analysis shows that dominant operator-discrepancy directions provide more effective and rank-efficient repair than rank-matched random, low-energy, or raw weight-error directions, while low-rank ReQOR reduces deployment memory overhead.


### arXiv 2611.32734 - ASCENT: A Benchmark for Evaluating and Advancing Stepwise Diagnostic Reasoning in Large Language Models on Common Clinical Scenarios

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T14:49:50Z
- authors: Yera Choi, Yeong Hwa Kim, Jaedeok Lee, Taekang Kim, Sangdoo Yun, Seong-Eun Moon
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32734

Large language models (LLMs) excel at medical question answering yet are rarely evaluated on the stepwise diagnostic reasoning that defines real clinical workflows, where impressions are revised as information accumulates. We build Annotated Stepwise Clinical rEasoning for NaturalisTic Diagnosis (ASCENT), a clinician-annotated benchmark and training resource of 3,078 stepwise problems derived from MedQA-USMLE that decomposes each vignette into EMR-aligned steps (Findings, Impression, supporting Rationale), enabling evaluation of intermediate reasoning under incomplete information. Experiments and training with ASCENT revealed insights into how current LLMs handle stepwise diagnostic reasoning. Even strong reasoning models that perform well on MedQA-USMLE leave substantial headroom on ASCENT, and general-purpose frontier models trail further, exposing a persistent gap between fully informed and stepwise diagnosis. Fine-tuning Qwen2.5-7B and 32B on ASCENT yields measurable F1 gains over both pre-trained and HuatuoGPT-o1 CoT-trained baselines, with gains driven primarily by precision. Complementary robustness analyses (counterfactual perturbation, format-vs-content control, judge agreement, and rollout) further show that ASCENT-fine-tuned models rely on the diagnostic content of prior impressions rather than imitating their output format, while error propagation under rollout remains a key challenge for clinical deployment.


### arXiv 2611.32714 - FourStat: A Dual AutoEncoder Leveraging Frequency and Stationarity for Time Series Anomaly Detection

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T14:37:50Z
- authors: Saman Hadian, Ramtin Moslemi, Mohammad Mohammadi, Sarina Heshmati, Siavash Ahmadi, Babak Hossein Khalaj
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32714

While numerous studies have focused on multivariate time series anomaly detection, many existing methods perform poorly or are not directly applicable to univariate settings. We introduce FourStat, a dual-model framework for univariate time series anomaly detection that combines two complementary approaches. The Fourier Autoencoder (FAE) captures global patterns like seasonality through frequency-domain analysis, while the Stationary Autoencoder (SAE) focuses on local structures by enforcing statistical stationarity. We also propose a new evaluation metric called Perfect Point that assesses whether perfect F1-scores can be achieved directly from anomaly scores, eliminating thresholding subjectivity. Results demonstrate that FourStat outperforms existing baselines, particularly on challenging anomaly types, while the new metric provides clearer insights into model performance gaps and advantages.


### arXiv 2611.32687 - Are We Rewarding the Wrong Module? Module-Specific Policy Optimization for Multimodal Reinforcement Learning

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T14:25:49Z
- authors: HuiSeong Cho, Jun Yeong Park, Yu Rang Park
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32687

Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) based on Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) has been widely adopted to enhance the multimodal reasoning of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). However, existing methods share a common limitation of Module-Level Credit Entanglement, in which the vision encoder, projector, and LLM modules are jointly optimized using the same advantage signals even when perceptual correctness and final-answer correctness differ. To address this limitation, we propose Module-Specific Policy Optimization (MSPO), a framework that separates perceptual and outcome-based learning signals across model modules. Specifically, MSPO uses responses to perception checklists comprising auxiliary questions about visual information to derive an advantage for training the vision encoder. From these responses, MSPO computes the Confidence-aware Perception Reward (CaPR), using a perception sign obtained by thresholding checklist accuracy to determine reward direction and posterior confidence from a Beta distribution over latent perception accuracy to scale reward magnitude. We then derive the vision advantage through group-relative normalization of CaPR and scale it using main-task success rates. This advantage guides updates to the vision encoder, while the projector and LLM retain outcome-based GRPO updates. Extensive experiments on seven mathematical and general reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that MSPO outperforms existing RL-tuned models on average at both the 3B and 7B scales.


### arXiv 2611.32686 - CAPTA: Identifying LLMs Behind AI Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CR
- published: 2026-11-24T14:25:39Z
- authors: Kailun Yan, Yue Huang, Sisi Duan
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32686

Large language model (LLM)-based agents access remote services through tools to complete user tasks, often via API calls. In this setting, a remote service may want to identify the backend LLM of an agent to launch model-specific attacks, including prompt injection or selective delivery of manipulated content. However, the service cannot directly query the backend model and must instead collect observable responses through its interactions with the agent, making identification challenging. In this paper, we present Completely Automated Preference Test to tell LLMs Apart (CAPTA), a model identification approach inspired by CAPTCHA (Completely Automated Public Turing test to tell Computers and Humans Apart), which distinguishes humans from automated programs. CAPTA presents multiple-choice questions as verification challenges, using differences in models' selection preferences to identify the backend LLM. It selects questions based on cross-model option-generation diversity and uses LLM-guided search to optimize their option sets. Our evaluation shows that CAPTA achieves 99.0% mean identification accuracy with only 10 questions across 25 LLMs from 10 model families. We further evaluate CAPTA with four widely used agents and seven representative LLMs, achieving 99.7% mean identification accuracy across all 28 agent-LLM combinations with 30 submitted answers.


### arXiv 2611.32663 - Economic World Model

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T14:14:40Z
- authors: Qirui Mi, Yujia Ming, Bo Li, Haifeng Zhang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32663

Can artificial intelligence reproduce the economic dynamics that emerge as households, firms and governments interact and adapt through markets and institutions? To answer this question, we define an economic world model (EWM) as an agent's internal representation of how an economic system works, enabling counterfactual simulation and decision-making under resource constraints. We then propose a general framework for studying EWMs that links three tasks: simulating agent behaviour, learning economic transitions and systematically evaluating the dynamics. As an initial implementation, we learn household policies from microeconomic data and calibrate a structural economic transition model using macroeconomic data, linking individual decisions to aggregate dynamics. Our micro-to-macro evaluation framework compares simulated individual actions and group distributions with observed microdata and assesses macroeconomic dynamics through forecasting and counterfactual experiments. Results show that simulated and observed household consumption distributions overlap by 94.9%, while EWM forecasts closely track real GDP and several other aggregate levels. In government-borrowing counterfactuals, the EWM reproduces the benchmark life-cycle model's short-run transmission from borrowing to consumption. For ex ante planning, the EWM enables an LLM agent to achieve 10.29% higher simulated GDP under the epidemic shock than direct LLM decisions. This initial EWM is only a starting point. We invite economists and AI researchers to build a stronger EWM together. Economists can contribute richer economic models and knowledge, while AI researchers can develop stronger data-driven methods for simulating agent behaviour and learning economic transitions. Together, we aim not only to reproduce real economic dynamics, but also to discover and test new mechanisms that support better decisions and benefit society.


### arXiv 2611.32660 - Bathys: Advancing Deep Search Agents

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T14:12:23Z
- authors: Zixuan Chen, Shengyou Yuan, Nang Yuan, Ming Ma, Zelin Xiao, Lin Zhou
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32660

Deep search is a critical capability for agents tackling complex, open-ended tasks, where solving a problem requires iterative information gathering, query reformulation, and evidence-based verification rather than a single retrieval step. To address these requirements, we present Bathys 9B and 27B deep search agents built around a training framework that spans verifiable question synthesis, post-training, and online reinforcement learning (RL) under context compression. Our high-quality data construction ensures both search depth and reliability: each clue is reformulated as an indirect reference requiring independent retrieval, and retained evidence chains enable verification that the clues jointly determine a unique answer. Curated trajectories underpin a pipeline of supervised fine-tuning (SFT), Kahneman-Tversky Optimization (KTO), and Compaction-Managed Group Sequence Policy Optimization (CM-GSPO): SFT provides a stable foundation for tool use over long horizons and query reformulation; KTO uses binary feedback on trajectories to improve the selection of answers grounded in evidence; CM-GSPO optimizes execution sub-trajectories with length-normalized likelihood ratios on the recorded rollout contexts and reweights sub-trajectory advantages to correct the extra weighting introduced by independently averaging sub-trajectory losses. Bathys 9B and 27B attain 66.51% and 80.49% on BrowseComp, respectively, under an evaluation protocol with trajectory self-verification and conditional retries.


### arXiv 2611.32657 - GeoSurge: Geometry-Grounded Multimodal Reasoning for Auditable Liver Resection Planning

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T14:12:05Z
- authors: Wenjie Deng, Zijian Tu, Xueyin Zhou, Huayi Wang, Zhuoyun Du, Mingyu Chen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32657

Surgical resection planning centers on generating executable resection surfaces from anatomical evidence and operative constraints. Medical multimodal foundation models enable anatomical understanding, and segmentation models predict resection regions, but neither translates these capabilities into explicitly constrained, executable resection surfaces. We present GeoSurge, an anatomy-grounded framework that combines mask-derived metrics with structure-aware point-cloud tokens to generate executable, editable resection surfaces. To make resection geometry learnable and editable, GeoSurge maps actions to parametric geometry through a surface-action grammar. Using this grammar, anatomy-oriented and tumor-centered templates generate candidates, which are filtered by hard constraints and semantic deduplication. Training supervision is then derived from candidate-expert geometric agreement and independently recomputed feasibility metrics. At inference, the reasoner uses patient evidence and surgical requirements to select or revise an action, which an independent geometry engine subsequently materializes and verifies. GeoSurge achieves the best overall scores on DeepTumour Liver and CRLM-QA, improving the constraint satisfaction rate from 73% to 100% on CRLM and from 56% to 99% on GeoSurge-Bench.


### arXiv 2611.32654 - DexWAC: A Unified World-Actor-Critic Model for Dexterous Robot Manipulation

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T14:09:49Z
- authors: Hongjia Liu, Dacheng Zhou, Minghao Fu, Chuanjun Guo, Tianze Wang, Qingtao Liu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32654

Robot demonstrations provide more than actions to imitate: they reveal how actions change the world and how those changes contribute to task success. Recent world-action models (WAMs) extend vision-language-action (VLA) models with the first signal by jointly predicting actions and future observations, yet this predictive ability serves only as an auxiliary loss for policy learning. Meanwhile, most policy improvement methods learn dynamics and values in separate models, increasing training overhead and limiting representation sharing between these complementary objectives. We introduce DexWAC, a unified world-actor-critic model that jointly learns an actor, an action-conditioned world model, and a bootstrapped critic in a single robot pretraining stage. Our key insight is that visual prediction encourages control-relevant features, while value supervision promotes task-relevant representations; together, they shape a representation suited to policy learning. Built on an image-editing backbone, DexWAC predicts, for each action chunk, the visual latent and proprioception at its endpoint together with its reward and continuation, so the world model alone efficiently yields complete imagined transitions without generating intermediate video frames. After adapting the world model and critic on real rollouts, we improve the pretrained actor through either PPO on imagined rollouts or critic-guided best-of-$N$ selection. DexWAC surpasses the large-scale pretrained VLA and WAM baselines in simulation and achieves 75% average success on two real-world tasks, compared with 45% for $π_{0.5}$. Its world model is more accurate and faster than video-based baselines, and ablations confirm that learning dynamics and value together improves the policy.


### arXiv 2611.32649 - ICLWorld: Benchmarking In-Context Learning of Agents from Digital to Physical Worlds

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.RO
- published: 2026-11-24T14:07:17Z
- authors: Qingle Liu, Jieming Li, Pei-lin Li, Shenghan Zheng, Ruize Yang, Sunqi Fan
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32649

In-context learning (ICL) lets agents acquire new rules from context and adapt to user-specific requirements at inference time. The same frontier agents now operate both computers and robots, yet their ICL remains insufficiently assessed: existing benchmarks are largely offline, rely on text or static images, and risk training-data contamination, while robot evaluations are limited to method-level ablations. We present ICLWorld, an online benchmark that evaluates the ICL of agents from digital to physical worlds, covering computer use and robot use, together with UIverse, a unified harness that turns every environment into a user interface. We build 30 environments, record 630 demonstration videos (10+ hours), and design 160 tasks with customized, often counter-intuitive rules that cannot be satisfied without learning from the context. Each task comes with three rule versions, given as text or video, and we test generalization at three levels, from replaying the demonstration to new instances and unseen environments. Across six frontier models, the strongest agents, Claude Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Astra, succeed on 82% and 76% of computer-use runs but only 27% and 26% of robot-use runs, and a no-rule control reduces success to at most 8%. GPT-6 Astra succeeds under all three rule versions on only 38% of computer-use tasks, half its rate under a single rule, and answers 98% of robot rule questions correctly yet completes only 20% of these tasks. Frontier agents learn rules comparably well from video and from text and transfer them across environments, whereas weaker agents rely on text and lose most of their video-based success once the demonstrated situation is resampled. These findings reveal substantial gaps between the digital and the physical world and between identifying a rule and acting on it.


### arXiv 2611.32646 - A Text-to-SQL Benchmark for Official Statistics

- categories: cs.AI, cs.IR
- published: 2026-11-24T14:05:10Z
- authors: Lucas Lageweg, Karen Goes, Tom van den Broek, Frank Pijpers, Paul Groth, Jan-Christoph Kalo
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32646

Official statistics are critical datasets for a wide variety of actors, including policymakers and businesses. For providers of official statistics, improving accessibility, governance, and disclosure of their data is of key importance. This can be improved by providing question-answering systems based on text-to-SQL approaches. However, existing text-to-SQL benchmarks do not resemble this setting. Official statistics are published as open-domain catalogs of thousands of wide tables whose columns and values are codes, and whose meaning is recorded in separate metadata rather than in a relational schema. To address this gap, we introduce LOCuST, a text-to-SQL benchmark that pairs over two thousand English and Dutch tables from Statistics Netherlands with a semantic knowledge graph. The graph describes the tables, measures, and dimension codes through labels, definitions, hierarchies, and units, and is the external knowledge a system needs to find the right tables and ground a question in the right codes. The benchmark includes expert-annotated questions for different query and aggregation types, including raw value retrieval, summations, proportion calculations, and joins. Alongside standard evaluation measures, we introduce an observation F1 metric that measures how close a query is to the ground truth even when the answers are different. We evaluate a variety of state-of-the-art LLMs and find that they struggle to write SQL that answers the questions correctly.


### arXiv 2611.32645 - TeamFlowRL: Online Policy Optimization of Decentralized Flow Policies for Multi-Agent Coordination

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-24T14:04:38Z
- authors: Guowei Zou, Beiwen Zhang, Haitao Wang, Hong Hong, Zhiquan Chen, Hejun Wu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32645

Complex cooperative tasks require coordinated multi-agent behavior. Offline generative policies learn expressive cooperative behaviors from trajectories but remain limited by fixed datasets. Online reinforcement learning can improve them through environment interaction. However, fine-tuning flow policies is difficult because action likelihoods are hard to compute. We propose TeamFlowRL, a two-stage framework that pretrains multi-agent flow policies offline and fine-tunes them online. We formulate team policy optimization over the complete denoising trajectories of decentralized flow policies, avoiding intractable action likelihoods. The resulting objective weights each agent's trajectory ratio with a shared team advantage and clips each ratio separately, aligning local policy updates with the common team return. Across 51 paired configurations in seven simulation groups, TeamFlowRL improves every offline initialization, with a 47.3% average gain in normalized score. It also achieves 33.1% higher average success under exploration noise than Gaussian-policy fine-tuning across 6 bimanual tasks.


### arXiv 2611.32642 - BARE: Body-Aware Reflexive Evasion for Humanoid Robots

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T14:01:29Z
- authors: Zihao Li, Tong Chen, Hua Song, Zitong Ding, Zihao Xu, Ce Hao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32642

Dynamic obstacle avoidance is essential for safely deploying humanoid robots in real-world environments, where rapidly approaching objects require immediate evasive responses. Recent advances in reflexive evasion have focused primarily on quadrupeds, which typically rely on large-scale whole-body maneuvers. In contrast, humanoids can exploit their rich body articulation to evade through small, localized body adjustments. By moving threatened body regions without repositioning the entire body, these responses may require less execution time and less surrounding free space. However, local adjustments can alter mass distribution or support conditions, making it challenging to achieve rapid evasion while maintaining whole-body stability. To this end, we propose BARE (Body-Aware Reflexive Evasion), a humanoid reflexive evasion framework guided by human motion priors that enables rapid local evasion while maintaining whole-body stability. A risk-guided motion adapter is designed to select reference motions from a retargeted human evasive motion library based on the threatened body region and to adjust their temporal phases based on collision urgency, allowing the robot to reach evasive postures before potential contact. Conditioned on the adapted reference and link-level collision risk, a reflexive evasion policy learns through reinforcement learning to coordinate local body adjustments with whole-body stabilization. We validate BARE in simulation and real-world experiments, demonstrating its ability to perform targeted local evasion while maintaining whole-body stability.


### arXiv 2611.32639 - ReFocus: Progress-Guided Adaptive Practice for Long-Horizon Robotic Manipulation Reinforcement Learning

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI, cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-24T14:00:18Z
- authors: Haoyuan Li, Wei Huang, Jiehong Lin, Shengrong Zhang, Chenzhi Liu, Shengchao Zhou
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32639

Online reinforcement learning (RL) enables vision-language-action policies to improve through environmental interaction. However, in long-horizon manipulation, sparse success/failure feedback provides little information about where policies encounter bottlenecks. Moreover, repeated full-task rollouts also leave these bottlenecks weakly analyzed and insufficiently practiced. To this end, we introduce ReFocus, a progress-guided multi-round RL framework that alternates between Global and Local RL to identify task-specific bottlenecks and allocate additional practice to them. Specifically, during Global RL, we decompose each task into a Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) of subtasks. Then a DAG-based Progress Reward is leveraged for both policy training and bottleneck diagnosis. Based on the identified bottlenecks, ReFocus selects Adaptive Practice Windows, defining task segments from an entry stage to a target stage. It allocates additional interaction to these segments during Local RL while updating the entire policy. After that, it evaluates the policy to perform the full task for the next round of RL, forming an iteration between Global and Local RL. Experiments on LIBERO-Long and RoboCasa365 show that ReFocus achieves success rates of 97.8% and 51.3%, respectively, compared with 90.6% and 39.3% for the $π_{0.5}$ baseline. Furthermore, we evaluate a real-world adaptation on three long-horizon tasks. On BreadBaker, ReFocus achieves 95% success, compared with 30% for the $π_{0.5}$. Project page: https://refocus-robotics.github.io.


### arXiv 2611.32630 - MargEmbed-Akan: Bridging Twi Questions and English Knowledge through Cross-Lingual Score Distillation

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T13:52:23Z
- authors: Prince N. Y. Annan, Ali A. A. Aziz, Stephen E. Moore
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32630

Most information sought online by Twi (Akan) speakers is written in English, making cross-lingual retrieval essential. We evaluate eight systems: BM25, five open embedding models, and two proprietary models (gemini-embedding-001 and OpenAI text-embedding-3-large) on two Twi-English tasks. AfriQA contains 1,221 natively written Asante Twi questions that must retrieve their answering English Wikipedia passages. Bible translation retrieval requires a Twi passage to retrieve its English translation from 6,195 candidates. We introduce MargEmbed-Akan, a 560M-parameter open query encoder trained from AfriE5-Large-instruct using cross-lingual score distillation. The frozen base model scores each English training question against its answer passage and seven hard negatives. The student learns to reproduce these scores from both an NLLB-200 Twi translation that passes a round-trip translation check and the original English question, preserving English behaviour. During training, passages are encoded by the frozen base model; at evaluation, MargEmbed-Akan encodes questions and passages. On AfriQA, base AfriE5 and gemini-embedding-001 both achieve 66.6 nDCG@10; EmbeddingGemma 2, Qwen3-VL-Embedding-2B, and OpenAI text-embedding-3-large achieve 56.6, 51.3, and 53.4, respectively. MargEmbed-Akan achieves 71.5, outperforming gemini-embedding-001 by 4.9 points, its base model by 5.0, and OpenAI text-embedding-3-large by 18.1. Its largest gain is on Twi typed without E and O (+7.5 over gemini-embedding-001). English retrieval does not differ significantly from the base model, although both proprietary models score 1.5-2.2 points higher. On Bible translation retrieval, MargEmbed-Akan and gemini-embedding-001 are statistically tied on standard Twi (97.2 vs. 96.5 nDCG@10); MargEmbed-Akan performs better on diacritic-free Twi (92.7 vs. 84.9). No AfriQA data was used for training or model selection.


### arXiv 2611.32623 - REBEAL: Human References Are No Longer the Best Translation, and Metrics Do Not Notice

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T13:48:28Z
- authors: Ahrii Kim, Chanjun Park, Seong-heum Kim
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32623

Reference-based MT evaluation assumes the human reference is correct and better than the systems it scores. Both assumptions now fail, since the human reference ranks first in none of the 2024 WMT MQM campaigns and carries annotated errors in 19% to 87% of its segments. What a metric does with a flawed reference is a question about the metric, and it would have been worth asking before reference quality came into doubt. We introduce REBEAL (Reference Bias EvALuation), a benchmark that probes how MT metrics respond to seven types of controlled reference contamination, drawn from the error categories those campaigns record. Across eight widely used metrics and three language pairs, recent learned metrics (xCOMET, MetricX) prove more robust than their predecessors, but each family fails in its own way. Surface metrics show no length sensitivity, so contamination hides in longer segments, while learned metrics accept references in unrelated languages as nearly correct. The failure they share is named-entity contamination, which no metric detects and which flips up to 8 of 36 system pairs when ranking nine MT systems.


### arXiv 2611.32622 - RoboRecast: Intent-Preserving Demonstration Augmentation via Diagnosis-Driven Optimization

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T13:48:19Z
- authors: Yushi Long, Juzhan Xu, Haibin Huang, Chi Zhang, Yin Chen, Sida Peng
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32622

Collecting diverse real-world manipulation demonstrations is costly, motivating the use of simulation to augment existing demonstrations for robot policy learning. When objects, layouts, or robots change, transferring motions from a source demonstration can produce behavior that is semantically implausible or physically infeasible in the new setting. We introduce RoboRecast, an agentic optimization framework that preserves the interactions required by the source task while jointly optimizing the simulated scene and robot motion to make those interactions executable in the target setting. When an interaction fails, the agent uses simulation feedback to diagnose the cause, revise the relevant scene or motion parameters, and test whether the revision resolves the failure. This flexibility allows RoboRecast to generate synthetic data from both human and robot demonstrations and to adapt them to new layouts, objects, and robot embodiments. When target demonstrations are available, adding RoboRecast data improves policy success on both real-world single-arm and simulated bimanual tasks. Even without demonstrations in the target configuration, RoboRecast adapts source demonstrations to support policy learning with new objects or robot embodiments.


### arXiv 2611.32600 - Gated Concept Adapters: Layer-Wise Concept Bottlenecks for Auditable Text Understanding

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-24T13:36:44Z
- authors: Niloofar Ranjbar, Saeedeh Momtazi, Mohammad Mehdi Homayounpoor
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32600

Human-facing text understanding systems are used to infer affect, moderate harmful content, and support socially sensitive decisions. However, Transformer predictions are difficult to audit because they are produced from high-dimensional hidden states that do not provide a compact, inspectable interface for human analysis or intervention. We introduce Gated Concept Adapters (GCA), a layer-wise concept bottleneck framework that attaches gated, low-dimensional concept-producing readout adapters to selected outputs of pretrained Transformers. Token representations are mapped into concept activations, filtered by learned gates, pooled into layer-level summaries, and exposed to the prediction head as a concept-mediated interface. The dimensions can be inspected through usage statistics, signature n-grams, post-hoc concept names, and targeted concept interventions. We evaluate GCA on fine-grained emotion classification, toxicity detection, topic classification, and a controlled verb-cloze stress test. In the affective and safety-relevant classification settings, GCA learns comparatively low-redundancy and intervention-sensitive concept representations while retaining useful predictive performance. On toxicity detection, it remains close to an unconstrained ModernBERT baseline while enabling concept-level inspection, targeted intervention, and lightweight post-hoc control. On GoEmotions, the end-to-end concept model incurs a macro-F1 cost relative to unconstrained RoBERTa, while a task-initialized frozen variant substantially reduces this gap. On AG News, GCA remains competitive with recent concept-based baselines, and the Qwen verb-cloze experiment clarifies the limits of concept-mediated full-vocabulary prediction. Overall, GCA provides a neural concept interface for auditable text understanding, prioritizing transparency, diagnosis, and intervention over unconstrained accuracy maximization.


### arXiv 2611.32573 - Boiling Down Uncertainty: Deep Generative Scenario Search with Decision-Guided Evolution Strategies for Two-Stage Stochastic Optimization

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, math.OC
- published: 2026-11-24T13:16:43Z
- authors: Atefeh Hemmati Golsefidi, David Pisinger
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32573

One of the most common models for handling uncertainty is two-stage stochastic programming. Choosing the right number of scenarios is a trade-off between the uncertainty that can be represented and computational cost. A recent method, Deep Generative Scenario Search, generates synthetic scenarios and improves them by learning from the decisions they induce. The original approach used a black-box gradient estimate, since decisions are piecewise constant across scenarios. This paper provides the missing analytical foundation by deriving the gradient in closed form from recourse duality and an adjoint solve of the regularized reduced problem. The decision-focused direction, with a heuristic direction as a backup when duals are unavailable, defines the sampling subspace of a guided evolution strategy rather than being used as a direct update. For any guiding subspace, the expected update is a descent direction of the smoothed objective, so an inaccurate guide adds variance while the expected step still descends. For six problem families, both guided estimators outperform the baseline. The decision-focused version reduces the mean gap by three-quarters, and on the two families with the most integer variables the reduced sets beat the exact solver's two-hour incumbent in most runs, using a fifth of the time.


### arXiv 2611.32564 - MXI-MemICL: Towards In-Context Robot Manipulation through Self-Retrieved Interaction Memory

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T13:12:01Z
- authors: Jialong Peng, Shihao Zhou, Feng Yan, Wen Xiao, Haozheng Wang, Wenqi Ren
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32564

In-context learning (ICL) offers robots a way to acquire new skills from a single demonstration without parameter updates. However, existing robotic ICL methods either rely on large-scale pretraining constrained by scarce, costly robot interaction data or require additional annotations yet struggle to generalize across tasks. To bridge this gap, we propose MXI-MemICL, a framework for memory-guided ICL in vision-language-action (VLA) models. By treating demonstrations as retrievable memory, MXI-MemICL enables in-context manipulation without additional large-scale pretraining or specialized annotations. Specifically, a visual-action causal encoder turns actions and their observed effects in demonstrations and completed interactions into reusable memory to guide execution. To retrieve experience suited to upcoming execution, an intention predictor forecasts the next stage's intent and uses it to query memory. The retrieved experience conditions the language backbone through experience-token prefixes and gated cross-attention, guiding action generation with a learned gate that regulates its influence. MXI-MemICL improves success over the baseline by +8.2% and +14.4% on RoboCasa365 and RoboTwin 2.0. Real-robot experiments show a +9.0% gain, with demonstrations adding +5.0% on seen and +16.7% on unseen tasks. The code will be made publicly available.


### arXiv 2611.32533 - Next-Manip: On-Demand Agent-Policy Collaboration for Robotic Manipulation

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T12:58:21Z
- authors: Jiayi Liu, Ke Ye, Jiaming Zhou, Yufei Huang, Haotian Liang, Kun-Yu Lin
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32533

General-purpose robotic manipulation requires task reasoning, failure recovery, and precise control, yet limited manipulation data make these abilities difficult to obtain from a single policy. Recent multimodal agents show promise in task reasoning and recovery but lack reliable fine-grained control and incur high inference cost, whereas learned policies provide efficient fine-grained execution but struggle beyond their learned skills. Although their complementary strengths suggest combining the two, doing so requires repeatedly deciding which executor should act as task demands change. Instead of relying on the agent for each decision, we present Next-Manip, an on-demand agent--policy collaboration framework that decouples executor allocation from costly agent inference. We propose an Agent Invocation Controller (AIC) that allocates control between the policy and the agent through a lightweight classifier of current execution demands. We further introduce Task Dynamics Context (TDC), which packages informative manipulation changes from in-context examples into compact task references to support both agent reasoning and executor allocation. Across two simulation benchmarks and real-world experiments, Next-Manip achieves state-of-the-art performance while reducing agent inference costs.


### arXiv 2611.32529 - ContinualDrive: A Deployment-Anchored, Multi-Axis Benchmark for Continual Updates of Learned Driving Planners

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T12:53:32Z
- authors: Zirui Li, Zhiqi Mao, Yangang Ren, Heye Huang, Haohan Yang, Qihang Huang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32529

Learned driving planners keep being updated after deployment as new logs arrive, yet continual learning evaluations rarely ask whether an update improves on the deployed model, and their verdicts rest on unstated evaluation choices. We present ContinualDrive, a deployment-anchored benchmark for post-deployment continual learning of driving planners. It updates a deployed planner on real-world nuPlan logs one scenario-type group at a time, evaluates the same checkpoints in open and closed loop, makes four measurement axes explicit (evaluation loop, reference checkpoint, temporal aggregation and stream protocol), and anchors a three-valued release verdict to the deployed checkpoint. We study two planners (PlanTF and Diffusion Planner) under full fine-tuning and adapter or LoRA updates, with and without replay or gradient projection, in three task orders. Verdicts can reverse or vanish when one axis changes: open-loop ADE ranks the final PlanTF model above the deployed one in half of its core-suite runs, whereas every closed-loop score ranks it below; backward transfer misses this loss, which on average is already present when each group has just been learned; and regime rankings vary far less across training seeds than across task orders. In the core suite of full and adapter updates, no regime measurably improved on the deployed planner in closed loop; on a panel more than ten times larger every evaluated final checkpoint trails it, and joint training on all groups recovers much of the loss without exceeding its nuPlan closed-loop score. We distill these findings into a reporting contract and will release the code and all checkpoints.


### arXiv 2611.32517 - Wi-Fi Enabled Edge Intelligence Framework for Smart City Traffic Monitoring using Low-Power IoT Cameras

- categories: cs.DC, cs.AI, cs.NI
- published: 2026-11-24T12:44:52Z
- authors: Raphael Walcher, Kurt Horvath, Dragi Kimovski, Stojan Kitanov
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32517

Real-time traffic monitoring in smart cities demands ultra-low latency processing to support time-critical decisions such as incident detection and congestion management. While cloud-based solutions offer robust computation, their inherent latency limits their applicability for such tasks. This work proposes a localized edge AI framework that connects low-power IoT camera sensors to a client, or applies offloading of inference to an NVIDIA Jetson Nano (GPU). Networking is achieved via Wi-Fi, enabling image classification without relying on wide-area infrastructure such as 5G, or wired networks. We evaluate two processing strategies: local inference on camera nodes and GPU-accelerated offloading to the Jetson Nano.We show that local processing is only feasible for lightweight models and low frame rates, whereas offloading enables near-realtime performance even for more complex models. These results demonstrate the viability of cost-effective, Wi-Fi-based edge AI deployments for latency-critical urban monitoring.


### arXiv 2611.32515 - Branch and Consolidate: A Unified Recipe for Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T12:42:16Z
- authors: Zihan Liang, Zikun Qu, Ben Chen, Fan Yang, Wenwu Ou, Min Zhang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32515

Specialists post-trained from a shared checkpoint acquire complementary capabilities, and multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) can consolidate them into a single model. However, we find that existing consolidation methods, from weight merging to MOPD, fall short when specialists diverge substantially from the shared checkpoint, whereas prior studies mostly examine specialists that remain close to it. We present a unified recipe for multimodal MOPD that consolidates such specialists back into the single student they branched from. The recipe specifies initialization, data selection, balanced supervision across teachers, and preservation of existing capabilities for consolidation, together with a targeted refinement stage for capabilities that still lag behind their specialists. In an industrial setting with six specialists, the student closely approaches each specialist in its own domain and surpasses the perception specialist. It recovers 97.0% of their average gain, compared with 79.8% for IM-MOPD. In the fully open five-domain setting with Qwen3-4B, our method recovers 95.7% of the specialists' average gain after consolidation and 97.5% after refinement, compared with 94.1% for IM-MOPD. We further analyze the full pipeline through mechanism ablations and training dynamics, providing practical guidance for diagnosing incomplete consolidation. Code will be released.


### arXiv 2611.32508 - RecurSciBench: Benchmarking Recursive Self-Improvement in Data-Driven Scientific Research

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T12:38:22Z
- authors: Wenxi Xu, Chuan Qin, Zihan Wang, Zixing Chen, Le Zhang, Jingbo Zhou
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32508

Large language model (LLM)-based agents are advancing the automation of data-driven scientific research. Recent advances in recursive self-improvement (RSI) offer the potential for these agents to continuously improve through experience, but evaluating transfer across scientific tasks requires executable, scientifically verifiable tasks and independent assessment of self-improvement gains. We introduce RecurSciBench, a scalable benchmark for evaluating RSI in data-driven scientific research. Its RecurSciPipe framework automatically constructs scientific modeling and data analysis tasks from raw datasets, with execution-based verification and scientific validity checks. RecurSciBench spans four scientific domains, multiple data modalities, and varied scientific objectives. We evaluate four RSI systems by comparing their initial and evolved states on independent tasks after evolution on source tasks, covering within-type, cross-type, and cross-domain transfer. Experiments show that the benefits of self-improvement vary across agent systems and scientific domains. Positive overall gains can coexist with regressions on individual tasks. Experience can transfer across task types and benefit other domains even without measurable gains in the source domain.


### arXiv 2611.32496 - Assessing Robustness to Catastrophic Forgetting in Continual Deep Reinforcement Learning for Cyber-Physical Systems

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T12:27:52Z
- authors: Kim N. Nolle, Ivana Dusparic, Rhodri Cusack, Vinny Cahill
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32496

As the use of reinforcement learning (RL) becomes more widespread in cyber-physical systems, it becomes increasingly important to design RL agents that are able to adapt to changes in their environments in order to maintain safety and reliability. This paper addresses the question of whether different classes of RL algorithm are more robust against forgetting previously learned behaviours, a critical issue in continual learning, in non-stationary environments. We evaluate the robustness of representative examples of three important classes of RL algorithm in a non-stationary autonomous parking environment. These are Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), which is a model-free on-policy algorithm, Soft-Actor-Critic (SAC), which is a model-free off-policy algorithm, and Drama, which is a model-based algorithm. The agents must learn to successfully park in four different scenarios, which are presented successively, with parking spaces oriented at varying angles. We find off-policy algorithms appear to be more robust compared to on-policy algorithms due to their use of replay, while model-based algorithms do not appear to offer any direct advantage over model-free algorithms. These experiments also highlight the additional challenges of finding robust abstractions of the environment, and oversensitivity to hyperparameters.


### arXiv 2611.32483 - CodeWAM: A Code World Action Model for End-to-End Autonomous Driving

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T12:11:29Z
- authors: Zhisheng Chen, Jinpeng Wang, Beicheng Lou, Zhengwei Xie, Hao Wu, Rui Qian
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32483

Driving world models anticipate action consequences through representations of how the world evolves. For autonomous driving, these representations must capture both the surrounding scene and the ego vehicle's motion under a candidate plan. We propose CodeWAM, a driving world model that incorporates executable code as part of its world representation. Code represents ego dynamics by expressing how the vehicle state evolves under plan execution, while a learned visual model captures scene-dependent consequences. Executing the code produces a candidate-conditioned motion sequence, which is evaluated relative to the prior plan and recorded motion history. The resulting execution consequences and learned scene outcomes jointly represent the candidate's future, enabling the planner to assess both what a plan may lead to and how the vehicle transitions into that future. The selected plan becomes part of the next decision's execution context, linking successive choices within the model. CodeWAM achieves 93.6 PDMS on NAVSIM v1 navtest, 92.1 EPDMS on v2 navtest, and 50.6 EPDMS on NAVHARD. Further experiments demonstrate improved pairwise execution consistency and gains on Hydra-MDP and DrivoR without additional training, supporting executable code as a complementary representation for driving world models.


### arXiv 2611.32480 - VISE: Verifier-Independent Skill Self-Evolution with Feedback from Tool Implementations

- categories: cs.AI, cs.SE
- published: 2026-11-24T12:08:40Z
- authors: Yuxuan Liu, Chongyang Du, Yuhao Zhang, Jiahe Guo, Kawai Chung, Wenbin Hu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32480

Agent skills provide reusable workflows that guide LLM agents in using tools. Refining these workflows requires feedback on missing or incorrect instructions, but task-specific verifiers are not always available. We propose VISE (Verifier-Independent Skill Evolution), a scripts-first approach that uses tool implementations to guide skill revision. It extracts tool capabilities, input requirements, usage conditions, and output formats from source code. Using the task request and execution trace, it compares this information with current workflow instructions to identify missing or conflicting steps. It then recommends specific edits to SKILL.md, linking each change to the relevant implementation and usage conditions. We evaluate VISE as an additional revision stage for four evolution methods on SkillsBench and SkillLearnBench. Compared with the corresponding No-verifier conditions, it improves overall average task success by 9.14-13.35 percentage points. The proportion of tasks solved in all three runs increases by 12.36-18.29 points. A case study shows how revised instructions connect tool outputs to later steps. These results support tool implementations as a practical feedback source for skill self-evolution.


### arXiv 2611.32474 - Progressive Reliable Supervision for Black-Box Universal Domain Adaptation

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T12:04:11Z
- authors: Jing Li, Zhe Zhang, Huateng Sun, Yuhui Yang, Wanli Xue, Xu Cheng
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32474

Black-Box Universal Domain Adaptation (B$^2$-UniDA) aims to adapt a target model to an unlabeled target domain under arbitrary label-space relationships, while neither source data nor source-model parameters are accessible during adaptation. Under such restrictive information access, reliable target supervision is unavailable, while predictions from the black-box source model can be unreliable due to source bias and domain shifts. To address this challenge, we propose Progressive Reliable Supervision (PReS), which progressively establishes and refines reliable target supervision by coordinating complementary knowledge from the black-box source model, foundation models, and the evolving target model. PReS first identifies reliable known samples by exploiting semantic consistency under diffusion-based perturbations and cross-model consensus between the black-box source model and CLIP. It then estimates the sample-wise reliability of the two predictors and adaptively integrates their complementary predictions to construct reliable pseudo labels. The resulting supervision is exploited asymmetrically to encourage confident and discriminative predictions for reliable known samples while maintaining uncertain yet locally consistent predictions for potential unknown samples. As adaptation proceeds, the emerging target-domain knowledge is further leveraged to refine both the pseudo labels and the known/unknown sample partition, forming a closed-loop process in which supervision construction and target adaptation mutually reinforce each other. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmark datasets under diverse label-space relationships demonstrate that PReS achieves state-of-the-art or highly competitive performance under the restrictive B$^2$-UniDA setting.


### arXiv 2611.32464 - Gamification in the age of AI: surveying player perceptions of motivation, manipulation, and data protection

- categories: cs.NI, cs.AI, cs.CY
- published: 2026-11-24T11:59:23Z
- authors: Kurt Horvath, Tom Tucek
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32464

Modern games and applications increasingly employ artificial intelligence (AI), user modeling, and gamification to influence user behavior, sustain engagement, and monetize attention. Although these mechanisms can enhance motivation and enjoyment, they also raise concerns about manipulation, personal data usage, and transparency. To examine how players perceive these dynamics, we conducted a survey with 28 adult participants. Respondents expressed strong concerns about the collection and exploitation of personal information in games and gamified applications, while also reporting low awareness and a limited sense of control over how their data is used. They evaluated progression features such as leveling systems and leaderboards as motivating. In contrast, they rated retention-focused mechanics such as daily login rewards and random reward reveals as highly manipulative. Participants reported that these systems can create fear of missing out or resemble gambling. Participants also expressed support for regulatory measures that limit exploitative design and protect vulnerable users. These findings reveal a clear gap between increasingly powerful engagement strategies and user trust, illustrating the need for more responsible and transparent gamification practices.


### arXiv 2611.32463 - Describe, Don't Ask: Caption-Driven On-Policy Distillation for Fine-Grained Visual Perception

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T11:59:22Z
- authors: Haozhan Shen, Liangbo He, Jun Lan, Huijia Zhu, Weiqiang Wang, Tiancheng Zhao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32463

Vision-language models (VLMs) often recognize fine-grained details more accurately in a relevant crop than in the full image. Multimodal on-policy distillation (OPD) methods exploit this by transferring the advantage of a crop-view teacher to a full-image student. However, their supervision relies on region-specific questions curated through an elaborate synthesis pipeline, which brings three weaknesses: (1) every region needs a question--answer pair written exclusively for it, locking training into costly QA curation that is hard to scale to unlabeled images; (2) all rollouts of a region answer the same question, so the teacher supervises only the details it asks about, leaving other visual elements in the region unsupervised; and (3) every question presupposes that its target exists, so the student becomes prone to answering even when the target is absent. In this paper, we study how to turn unlabeled images into broad visual supervision through multi-aspect self-description and privileged-view distillation, and propose CaptionOPD, which replaces tailored questions with a fixed set of image-agnostic caption prompts, ranging from general descriptions to focused captions of parts, colors, counts, text, and layout. The rollouts of a region receive different prompts, so the teacher supervises various aspects of the region. Moreover, because caption prompts mask the model to describe what is visible instead of querying a specific target, they avoid presupposing what the region contains. Using the model itself as the crop-view teacher, CaptionOPD requires no questions, no answers, and no larger model annotations: only images and region boxes. Experiments show that, trained on the same images and regions, CaptionOPD outperforms prior OPD methods across multiple fine-grained and general perception benchmarks.


### arXiv 2611.32439 - See the Answer, Learn to Embed: Learning Universal Multimodal Embeddings from Hindsight

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CV, cs.IR
- published: 2026-11-24T11:39:03Z
- authors: Yoonki Cho, Youngju Na, Taegeun Yang, Jinhwan Seo, Sung-Eui Yoon
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32439

Universal Multimodal Embedding (UME) models map heterogeneous multimodal inputs into a shared embedding space, where pairwise similarity supports diverse downstream tasks. Recent reasoning-driven UME approaches use intermediate reasoning to generate additional context for embedding, but often incur extra computation at inference. In this paper, we explore the matched candidate (i.e., the paired target) as an alternative source of context, asking whether a model can "see the answer" during training and "learn to embed" without it at inference. However, direct conditioning on this privileged context can introduce candidate-specific information unavailable from the query alone or cause the candidate-conditioned query representation to collapse toward the candidate embedding. To address this, we introduce Hindsight-Enhanced Multimodal Embeddings (HEME), which learns query-grounded hindsight representations from such candidate context. HEME first trains a hindsight teacher with a hindsight attention mask, mismatched-candidate conditioning, and query-token alignment to limit excessive reliance on the candidate. It then freezes the teacher and distills its hindsight representation and similarity distribution into a standalone student embedder. At inference, the student independently encodes queries and candidates without candidate conditioning or the hindsight teacher. Experiments on MMEB-V2 and the agent and text tasks of MMEB-V3 demonstrate the effectiveness of HEME and show that it enables more efficient inference than explicit reasoning-driven methods.


### arXiv 2611.32431 - NRG-Twins: Pixel-Level Ground Truth for Rigidity Violations in Generated Videos

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T11:32:50Z
- authors: Chenrui Fan, Qiyang Hu, Luca Scharr, Paolo Favaro
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32431

Video generators now produce realistic clips of scenes and objects, which makes generated video an attractive source of 3D content. Yet generated video is not 3D consistent: static content slowly deforms or slides, 3D reconstruction degrades, and the errors are too subtle to label by hand. To make generators 3D consistent, or to build rewards and guidance that push them there, we need data that say where and by how much a generated video breaks rigidity, and these data must come from generated video itself. We obtain per-pixel ground-truth labels from real-generated twins: a real clip paired with its re-creation by a keyframe-conditioned video generator. After inserting real keyframes so that the twin camera roughly follows the real one, we measure per pixel, in degrees, how far the twin departs from a rigid scene seen along its own camera path. We release Non-Rigidly Generated Twins (NRG-Twins), 871 twins, the code that builds them for new generators, and a benchmark with an official test split. A detector trained on these data, the Non-Rigidly Generated Pixel Detector (NRG-PD), predicts the per-pixel angle from RGB alone. It separates inconsistent from consistent pixels with AUROC 0.85, against 0.40-0.74 for seven existing consistency and motion-segmentation methods, and its predictions improve 3DGS of video that contains generated content.


### arXiv 2611.32415 - Within-task hazards of AI agents: unidentified by time-horizon curves, identified by repeated runs

- categories: stat.ME, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T11:18:43Z
- authors: Ruiqi Zhao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32415

The time horizon of an AI agent is read off a curve: the probability that the agent completes a task, plotted against the time a human needs for it. Several recent analyses fit survival models to this curve and conclude that agents' hazard of failure declines as a task goes on. The curve cannot support that conclusion. Every log-logistic horizon curve with slope $β\le1$ is a mixture of constant-hazard curves, and in a proportional-hazards frailty model the same curve is produced by every within-task Weibull shape $k\geβ$. If runs of a task are conditionally independent, repeated runs restore identification within this model: the probability that two runs both succeed is the single-run curve shifted by $1/k$ on the $\log_2$ time axis. Without the Weibull form, the horizontal distance between these two curves still bears on the direction of the hazard: if the average within-task hazard is non-increasing, the distance is at least one, and so is the population limit of a two-step estimator when the cross-section is log-logistic. We decompose the bias of that estimator into a violation of the shift and a first-step misspecification term, and examine five mechanisms that can break the shift. On METR's public run-level data, under stated assumptions that the data cannot verify, no model's 95% interval for $1/k$ lies entirely above one, and for the five frontier models in the TH1.0 release every interval from runs in different batches lies below one. Under the same assumptions, taken interval by interval and given a log-logistic cross-section, these intervals reject a non-increasing average hazard for the five models, whereas in TH1.1 the result depends on how human times were obtained. Whether $k>1$ reflects a hazard that rises within a task or tasks whose difficulty human time measures only loosely needs experiments that observe the same task at several lengths.


### arXiv 2611.32406 - BEACON: Learning Observation-Conditioned Starting Points for Guided Conditional Flow Matching in Microscopy

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T11:08:44Z
- authors: Anirban Ray, Florian Jug
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32406

Fluorescence microscopy drives much of modern life science, but accessible imaging setups often yield images degraded by out-of-focus light, limited resolution, and strong signal-dependent noise. Computational restoration can recover high-quality images from such data, enabling cheaper and faster imaging at lower light dose, provided biologically meaningful structures are preserved. Guided conditional flow matching has recently shown strong results for microscopy dehazing and computational super-resolution by learning distributions of plausible restorations. However, it pairs each clean target with an independent standard Gaussian sample, so the starting point of every training path does not leverage the degraded observation. This arbitrary noise--target coupling can produce conflicting velocity targets and makes the conditional velocity field harder to learn. We present BEACON, a $BE$st-of-$K$ $A$daptive learned coupling for guided $CON$ditional flow matching, which learns an observation-conditioned base distribution. During training, a lightweight conditional noise model proposes $K$ starting points for each degraded input, and the candidate with the lowest flow-matching loss supervises both networks, while a KL term keeps the proposal close to the Gaussian prior. At inference, a single draw from the learned proposal is transported along the velocity field, requiring neither candidate selection nor the target. We evaluate BEACON on ten datasets spanning microscopy dehazing and computational super-resolution against 13 baseline families. BEACON achieves the best median perception--distortion rank for every pairing of a fidelity metric (PSNR, MicroMS-SSIM) with a perceptual metric (LPIPS, FID). Code and pretrained models will be released under a permissive license.


### arXiv 2611.32405 - CPU-Eval: Evaluating Large Language Models for CPU Hardware Code Generation

- categories: cs.AR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T11:07:23Z
- authors: Yuisei Iwata, Yutaka Masuda, Tohru Ishihara
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32405

Benchmarks such as VerilogEval have supported rapid advances in hardware description language (HDL) generation by large language models (LLMs). However, recent HDL-generation systems achieve pass rates above 90% on VerilogEval, leaving limited headroom for revealing further advances. To provide a challenging target for further progress on a practically important hardware-development task, we introduce CPU-Eval, a benchmark of 339 HDL-generation problems constructed from six CPUs. Drawn from practical CPU designs, including families fabricated in silicon, the problems span CPUs from small embedded cores to Linux-capable out-of-order cores and cover diverse CPU functions written in SystemVerilog, Chisel, and SpinalHDL. In each problem, an LLM performs the real CPU-development task of generating HDL. Across seven state-of-the-art LLMs, mean pass@1 is 45.14 percentage points lower on CPU-Eval than on VerilogEval v2 Spec-to-RTL, exposing capability differences obscured by VerilogEval's high pass rates. CPU-Eval reveals that pass@1 is lower for submodules with more source-code lines, which motivates fine-grained modular design for LLM-aided hardware generation. We also show that pass@1 is lower for CPUs written in less widely used HDLs, motivating designers to verify that an LLM is sufficiently proficient at generating code in the HDL they use before deployment.


### arXiv 2611.32400 - Verify then Steer: Progress Hallucination-Aware Recovery for Continuous Vision-and-Language Navigation

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T11:05:44Z
- authors: Jiewen Hou, Guanxiang Qi, Meina Kan, Shiguang Shan, Xilin Chen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32400

Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) requires an agent to follow language instructions to reach a target in an unseen 3D environment. However, existing methods still struggle to recover from route deviations at test time. We investigate this limitation through the lens of instruction progress, and trace it to a phenomenon we term progress hallucination: agents misinterpret off-route observations as evidence of task progress, causing their internal instruction progress to advance beyond the actual task stage and hindering recovery based on earlier sub-instructions. To address this failure mode, we propose PHAR (Progress Hallucination-Aware Recovery), a test-time recovery framework following a verify-then-steer paradigm. The verifier examines residual-stream representations of the Vision-Language Model (VLM) to identify mismatch of cross-modal evidence along the trajectories, and uses split conformal calibration to control false alarms. When an alarm is raised, the steering module uses a contextual bandit to adaptively select the rewind extent of instruction progress for online recovery. Through extensive experiments on R2R-CE and RxR-CE, our framework enhances the reliability of multiple state-of-the-art vision-language navigation methods at test time.


### arXiv 2611.32399 - Not All Pairs Align: Caption-Guided Audio-Visual Contrastive Learning

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T11:05:39Z
- authors: Omar Emara, Nan Lu, Dima Damen, Hazel Doughty
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32399

We address the problem of learning audio-visual representations from co-occurring modalities. Multimodal contrastive learning typically assumes that paired observations provide shared views of the same underlying information. While this may be true for datasets collected in constrained environments, it does not always hold. For instance, co-occurring audio and visual signals can differ substantially in their content due to off-screen sounds. Nevertheless, standard contrastive objectives treat all paired samples as equally aligned. In this work, we propose Caption Guided Alignment Aware Loss (CGAAL), a model-agnostic objective that leverages modality-specific captions to estimate semantic agreement between audio and video. CGAAL uses these agreement scores to weight the contribution of each positive pair. A warm-up period and gradual thresholding strategy progressively introduce this alignment-aware training signal. We demonstrate the generality of CGAAL by applying it to three contrastive baselines, consistently improving their performance on audio-visual retrieval.


### arXiv 2611.32390 - Evolve to Learn: Gradient-aligned experience evolution for model self-improvement

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T10:54:17Z
- authors: Haoyuan Li, Gangyi Zhang, Dong Wang, Yang Liu, Daoxuan Liu, Jiancan Wu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32390

Self-evolving language models can continuously acquire useful experience through test-time interaction, reflection, and prompt optimization, yet turning such experience into model itself to ensure self improvement remains challenging. On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) provides a natural mechanism for internalizing test-time information into model parameters, yet it largely assumes that the gener- ated experience is worth learning. We show that this assumption does not always hold: experiences that improve test-time performance may provide limited or even harmful training signals. To better characterize which experience should be inter- nalized, we systematically analyze diverse mutated experiences across multiple tasks and reflection models, and find that gradient-based alignment is a more reli- able indicator of downstream distillation gains than immediate test-time improve- ment. Based on this finding, we propose E2L, a self-evolution framework that iteratively generates, evaluates, and distills experiences according to their esti- mated training utility. E2L closes the loop between experience generation and parameter learning, enabling agents to autonomously produce experience suitable for self-improvement.


### arXiv 2611.32384 - Can AI Design a Better nnU-Net? A Hypothesis-Driven Architecture Discovery Loop from Failures to Design Rules

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-24T10:49:50Z
- authors: Songtao Jiang, Yuan Wang, Fucheng Niu, Hualiang Wang, Jian Wu, Zuozhu Liu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32384

Neural architecture search and LLM-driven evolution often reduce evaluation to a scalar fitness score, discarding failures that could explain why designs succeed or fail. We present a hypothesis-driven architecture discovery loop in which LLM agents diagnose structured error patterns, formulate falsifiable hypotheses, test counterfactual controls under a frozen nnU-Net pipeline, and distill validated mechanisms into executable trigger-to-action rules. Across eight search waves, the loop identifies two principles for 3D medical image segmentation. First, reallocating computation from high-resolution decoders to the mid-encoder preserves thin-anatomy boundaries while reducing cost. Second, support-confined region contrast addresses errors between nested anatomical compartments, with anatomy-dependent triggers selecting when the rule applies. These principles produce LeanNeXt (0.78 MACs and 0.92 training-step time relative to nnU-Net; +1.63 Dice points on four-fold KiTS23 cross-validation) and trigger-routed LeanNeXt+. The latter improves all 15 evaluated CT/MRI settings over nnU-Net and exceeds the 3.3 larger ResEnc-M on 12 of 15 benchmarks at 0.30 its parameter count. Relative to random sampling, one-shot LLM generation, and score-selected agent search, the loop shows how failure analysis can yield reusable design rules across modalities.


### arXiv 2611.32381 - Does Research-Paper Context Improve LLM-Generated Quadruped Navigation? An Exploratory Simulation Study

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI, cs.SE
- published: 2026-11-24T10:46:28Z
- authors: Barnabas Juhasz, Rolf Pfister
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32381

Large language models can write competent robot navigation code, but not only does the research on the topic advance, a model may also, due to the volume of findings, fail to recall relevant findings and apply them to the task at hand. In this work, we test whether providing particular research papers as context improves the LLM-written navigation code for the Go2 quadruped, with the LLM (Claude Opus 5) and locomotion policy frozen. Specifically, we compare the navigation controllers the LLM writes when provided with and without particular papers across three settings: behavior trees, navigators edited from a shared base, and controllers written from scratch. When a maze's shortest route is blocked mid-episode, 2 of 8 controllers written with the context of an incremental-replanning paper complete all four trips under Isaac Sim physics, against 7 of 8 without it (two-sided Fisher's exact test, p = 0.041). This difference appears only under physics: in an idealized kinematic simulator, the controllers written with and without the paper are indistinguishable. We hypothesize that an LLM provided research papers as context tends to overweight both their content and their goals -- in this case, the efficiency of incremental replanning -- negatively affecting the fulfillment of the main task and leading to less robust code.


### arXiv 2611.32374 - Validating Multi-Model AI-Generated Acceptance Criteria in Requirements Engineering

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T10:41:13Z
- authors: Mateen Ahmed Abbasi, Petri Ihantola, Tommi Mikkonen, Niko Mäkitalo
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32374

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in software engineering to generate artifacts such as requirements, user stories, acceptance criteria, documentation, and code. Their use in requirements engineering raises important questions about correctness, traceability, and hallucination, especially because flawed specifications can affect later design, implementation, and verification activities. While much recent work on AI-assisted software engineering has focused on code generation and code-level validation, the validation of AI-generated specifications has received less attention. This paper addresses this gap by presenting a validation-oriented approach for AI-generated acceptance criteria. The approach combines retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), multi-model generation, and an explicitly structured human-in-the-loop workflow. We describe a working prototype that allows users to prepare or enter user stories, generate acceptance criteria using several LLMs, optionally augment generation with retrieved requirements-related examples, compare outputs side by side, inspect retrieved context, and select, edit, or regenerate candidate criteria before explicitly submitting a reviewer-finalized version. The system also records model outputs, user interventions, retrieval settings, and final criteria as part of a logged decision process. Rather than claiming that generation accuracy is already improved, the contribution of this work is to operationalize validation as an explicit, inspectable, and accountable process for AI-generated requirements artifacts.


### arXiv 2611.32365 - The Control-Flow Tax: Specialized Decision Routing for LLM Agent Workflows

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T10:36:06Z
