- authors: Zhikai Cheng, Shuanghu Wang, Li Jiang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32365

An agent can spend almost as much deciding what to do as doing it. In our documentation workflow, control-flow routing accounts for 42.5% of baseline reference cost on the initial task. Lower token prices alone do not solve this control-flow tax: inexpensive general-purpose routers can trigger additional calls to the expensive generator. We evaluate a heterogeneous architecture in which Jev, a specialized System 1 decision model, controls finite actions while Sol generates documents. Across four software tasks, 480 valid runs comprise 470 completions and ten request-limit endpoints. Jev full routing achieves the lowest mean cost and elapsed time on every task, completing all 40 specialist-controlled runs along the shortest trajectory. On the initial task, mean cost falls by 49.6% (95% paired-bootstrap interval: 42.9% to 56.3%) and elapsed time by 33.2% (19.6% to 46.7%). Across three further tasks, cost reductions are 40.5% to 42.9% and concurrent elapsed-time reductions are 12.9% to 14.4%. Existence-only specialization saves just 1.8% on the initial task. Separately, Sol and Jev each select accepted actions on 100/100 reviewed retail states, with Jev using 97.88% less reference cost and 72.30% less mean request time. The results identify two requirements for efficient agent control: inexpensive decisions and trajectories that preserve those savings.


### arXiv 2611.32344 - DiCT: Teaching X-ray Vision-Language Models to Reason via Imagining CT

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T10:23:56Z
- authors: Zhenghao Lu, Xiaotian Zhang, Songtao Jiang, Yiqun Lin, Siming Fu, Hualiang Wang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32344

Diagnosing with X-rays often requires reasoning about the 3D locations of abnormalities and their spatial relationships to surrounding anatomical structures, yet such reasoning remains challenging due to the loss of spatial information in 2D projections. To address this challenge, we propose Dreaming in CT (DiCT), a three-stage progressive CT-to-X-ray reasoning transfer framework that leverages a CT vision-language model (VLM) to guide an X-ray VLM. Through this CT-guided transfer, the student acquires CT-informed 3D reasoning capabilities, enabling it to reason directly from 2D X-rays without explicit CT reconstruction at inference time. The framework begins with instruct the dreamer, which equips the X-ray student with initial CT-oriented reasoning capabilities and establishes a structured interface that distinguishes X-ray observations from CT imagination. Building on this structure, visualize the dream introduces CT-guided on-policy distillation (OPD), where the CT teacher selectively guides the X-ray student's reasoning trajectories to transfer 3D anatomical knowledge. Finally, manifest the dream adapts the acquired reasoning capabilities from digitally reconstructed radiographs (DRRs) to real clinical X-rays. Experiments across two VLM backbones on 3D-RAD and ReXVQA demonstrate DiCT's effectiveness across radiological reasoning tasks, achieving a 1.66 percentage point improvement over a CT reconstruction-based baseline on 3D-RAD Abnormality Identification with 12.77$\times$ lower inference computation. Our code is available at https://github.com/HongYep/DiCT.


### arXiv 2611.32343 - Part-Set Prototypes for Explainable Mountain Gazelles Sex Classification

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T10:23:14Z
- authors: Tali Boneh Shitrit, Amir Kedem, Ilan Shimshoni, Anna Zamansky
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32343

Self-explainable visual models for fine-grained recognition are often hindered by rigid geometric constraints that cannot capture diverse semantic shapes in unconstrained "in-the-wild" imagery. Real-world camera-trap data introduces challenges, including non-optimal poses, lighting variations, occlusions, animal camouflage, and environmental shortcuts. To address this, we introduce set prototypes, a general architecture where both part cardinality and unconstrained spatial layouts are mined directly from data. Our joint-matching formulation places parts simultaneously on a frozen DINOv3 token grid via truncated Sinkhorn competition, allowing unsupported parts to abstain without spatial collapse. We evaluate our approach on Mountain Gazelle sex classification, a camera-trap benchmark, where our model achieves 0.878 macro-F1--outperforming prior architectures and at par with an unconstrained linear probe. The strongest prototypes localize to distinctive biological areas such as horns, neck and body, allowing ecologists to visually verify decisions before trusting the metric. This formulation generalizes naturally to diverse ecological monitoring challenges beyond a single species.


### arXiv 2611.32325 - A recursive self-improvement framework for electron microscopy analysis

- categories: cs.CV, cond-mat.mtrl-sci, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T10:16:18Z
- authors: Yaotian Yang, Sijia Tao, Yuping Zhang, Jiangjie Qiu, Qinghua Zhang, Xiao Chen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32325

Resolving local atomic structures such as defects and interfaces from electron microscopy contrast relies heavily on researcher judgement, leading to low efficiency and subjective standards. Here we present PhyEvo-Mic-RSI, a physics-guided and expert-informed recursive self-improvement framework that turns one-off human corrections into reusable, verifiable capabilities by updating executable operators, parameters and routing rules with versioned inheritance. Given researcher-defined tasks, the system selects analysis paths from image features without expert labels, while testing and inheriting code updates under fixed budgets. Combining physical priors with expert feedback improves atomic point-defect detection over larger models and raises cross-material interface coverage from 5.13% to 72.11% within a 10% area budget. Adapting the framework to micrographs from unseen studies further separates touching nanoparticles by aligning optimization with particle-level measurements. This work moves computational microscopy beyond standalone models and static scripts towards self-evolving platforms that accumulate human scientific expertise


### arXiv 2611.32324 - When Exact Token Importance Is Not Enough: Limits of Top-K Selection in LLM Attention

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T10:15:39Z
- authors: Irmaan Mirzanejad
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32324

Long-context language models often retain only a limited set of past token positions for an attention query. A common strategy scores positions individually and keeps the highest-ranked K. Yet attention weights are renormalized over the chosen set: two individually important positions need not form the best pair. We ask whether independent ranking suffices even when each importance score measures the exact downstream change in next-token predictions caused by deleting that position alone. It does not. A four-position construction makes the ranked pair incur 133.2 times the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence of the optimal pair, and a parametric family proves that this multiplicative gap is unbounded. An exact exchange identity and preference reversals for every K >= 2 explain the interaction. In exhaustive frozen-model audits of SmolLM2, Gemma, and Qwen, exact-singleton Top-4 leaves at least 10% relative improvement within a fully enumerated 24-position candidate pool in 55/60, 49/60, and 60/60 cases, respectively. SwapCert, a same-budget one-swap audit, detects 157/164 such cases and returns directly checkable improving supports. A KL-margin certificate relates support fidelity to next-token stability; larger-budget, multi-layer cached-decoding experiments measure predictive fidelity at K = 128 and 256. These results establish joint support quality as an essential object of analysis for sparse attention and motivate downstream-aware support selection under inference constraints.


### arXiv 2611.32321 - FigmaEval: A Benchmark for Evaluating AI Agents in Professional UI Design

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T10:14:21Z
- authors: Xinyue Huang, Yongyong Gao, Zhengyuan Pan, Zheng Wei, Xinya Liu, Duanyi Yan
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32321

Recent advances in AI agents have enabled autonomous UI design using professional tools such as Figma, yet evaluating their design capabilities remains challenging due to the open-ended nature of design tasks and the absence of unique ground truths. We introduce FigmaEval, a benchmark for evaluating AI agents on professional UI design tasks, comprising 84 tasks and 783 screens derived from real-world Figma design files. FigmaEval adopts a constrained-specification, open-solution paradigm, standardizing task requirements and design resources while allowing agents to independently determine layouts, visual hierarchies, component composition, and visual presentation. To assess design quality beyond reference similarity, we develop 22 expert-grounded evaluation rubrics across six dimensions and introduce a human evaluation arena where professional designers compare agent-generated designs through blinded pairwise judgments. Evaluating eleven models under a unified Figma agent setup, we find substantial agreement between rubric-based assessments and human preferences, alongside distinct differences in design practices and quality--efficiency trade-offs. FigmaEval provides a framework for systematically assessing professional UI design capabilities and understanding the strengths and limitations of current AI agents.


### arXiv 2611.32320 - Alpha-Beta Pruning via Monadic Continuations

- categories: cs.AI, cs.GT, math.LO
- published: 2026-11-24T10:14:04Z
- authors: Martín Escardó, Paulo Oliva
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32320

Alpha-beta pruning is a fundamental technique in game-playing AI, enabling efficient minimax search by avoiding the exploration of branches that cannot affect the optimal decision. We present a monadic formulation of alpha-beta pruning based on the combination of the continuation monad and the reader monad. Unlike previous approaches that encode alpha-beta bounds into the types of available moves, our construction records these bounds within monadic outcomes, leaving the move types unchanged. We further show that the ordinary continuation monad suffices to compute not only optimal outcomes but also optimal plays, by extending the outcome type to include the corresponding sequences of moves. This provides an alternative to generalisations of the continuation monad proposed to address the inefficiencies associated with the selection monad in alpha-beta search. We illustrate our approach with an implementation of Tic-Tac-Toe, observing a 39-fold reduction in the number of plays evaluated, compared with exhaustive search, in computing an optimal play. Finally, we formally establish the correctness of our construction by proving that the monadic alpha-beta search computes the same optimal outcomes and plays as exhaustive minimax search. Our results demonstrate how a foundational algorithm in AI can be expressed and verified using standard monadic abstractions, providing a basis for further development of compositional approaches to adversarial search.


### arXiv 2611.32317 - SPRUNN: Structural Proximity for Pruning in Deep Neural Networks

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T10:12:53Z
- authors: Babak Rokha, Ali Azarpeyvanda, Alireza Khanteymoori, Tara Ghasempouri
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32317

Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) have demonstrated significant accuracy across various machine learning tasks. However, these networks suffer from a huge number of parameters and computations, which leads to time-consuming processes. Consequently, parameter pruning approaches have been proposed to compress DNNs and efficiently implement them. The main challenge in pruning is selecting the appropriate parameters for pruning without significantly damaging the accuracy. In this paper, we propose SPRUNN, an algorithmic pruning approach based on the structural proximity criterion for filter pruning in convolutional layers of deep neural networks. Structural Proximity (SP) calculates filter similarity by considering all filters and comparing each one with the others, effectively identifying less important filters for pruning in the convolutional layers. Furthermore, we integrate this approach with fine-grained pruning in Fully Connected(FC)layerstocompresstheDNNsthatinclude both convolutional and FC layers.Toenhancetheaccuracyoftheprunedmodel,weproposealearning rate refinement strategy during the retraining phase. Experimental results demonstrate that SPRUNN achieves significant compression with high accuracy across diverse architectures and datasets.


### arXiv 2611.32316 - ASTERISM: Do Multimodal LLMs Actually Look at All the Images?

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-24T10:12:44Z
- authors: Chanseul Cho, Dongwoo Kim, Noori Bae, Seungjae Moon, Seongsu Lee, Youngmin Ro
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32316

Multi-image benchmarks aim to test whether a multimodal large language model (MLLM) combines evidence across images, but many of their items can be answered from a single image. Across about 33k items from 19 public benchmarks, our audit and a two-stage single-image filter find that one image or the question alone settles 24.2% of them. On these items, MLLMs recover from one image a median 78% of the accuracy that all images add over the text alone, so these items reward answering from one image, which we call the single-image shortcut. MLLMs take this shortcut even on items where one image is not enough. On such items, most of their wrong answers repeat an answer they give from one image, at a rate well above chance. We build ASTERISM, to our knowledge the largest audited multi-image benchmark, from these 19 benchmarks by keeping their items that need more than one image. Its 18.7k items, 2.0k of them corrected or rewritten, share one format, one scorer and seven task categories. We also release ASTERISM-Train with 49k training items and ASTERISM-Lite, a hard subset whose items we manually verify one by one. On ASTERISM-Lite, the strongest proprietary MLLM scores 68%. Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), now common in MLLM post-training, rewards the shortcut as well, because a correct answer read off one image earns as much reward as one that combines images. In line with this, existing RLVR objectives add little to the ASTERISM score. To stop rewarding the shortcut in training as ASTERISM does in evaluation, we propose cross-image credit assignment (CICA), an extension of RLVR that moves the credit toward the tokens that need more than one image. On three open-source MLLMs, CICA raises the ASTERISM score by 2.6 to 6.0 points, more than twice the gain of each general and vision-specific RL objective trained on the same data.


### arXiv 2611.32304 - Sparse Poison, Strong Hijack: Efficient Trajectory-Level Backdoors in Vision-Language-Action Models with Only 1\% Poisoning

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T10:00:45Z
- authors: Simin Huang, Jiawei Chen, Shaolong Zhu, Qiwei Liang, Zhaoxia Yin
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32304

Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models advance robotic manipulation, but their reliance on demonstrations exposes them to backdoor threats. Among these threats, trajectory-level targeted backdoors are particularly challenging and may pose greater safety risks than behavior-disrupting attacks: rather than merely causing task failures or local action deviations, they must sustain closed-loop redirection to complete an attacker-specified multi-step task. To reliably induce such behaviors, existing methods typically rely on high poisoning rates to obtain sufficient target-trajectory supervision. However, high poisoning rates may compromise clean-task performance, whereas low rates leave target-trajectory supervision scarce and backdoor learning vulnerable to interference from dominant clean-task updates. To resolve this trade-off at low poisoning rates, we propose a two-stage co-optimization framework guided by vision, language, and action. Stage I freezes the policy and uses differentiable rendering and multimodal supervision to optimize a 3D trigger's texture, translation, and scale, strengthening trigger-target associations from limited poisoned demonstrations. Building on the optimized trigger, Stage II applies source-selective dual-LoRA adaptation to decouple clean and backdoor updates and mitigate their interference, followed by learned column-wise calibration and analytic fusion into a unified policy. Experiments on OpenVLA, OpenVLA-OFT, $π_0$, and $π_{0.5}$ across four LIBERO suites show consistent gains over all evaluated attack baselines. With only 1% poisoned data, our method achieves 86.4% target success and 95.2% clean-task success on OpenVLA-OFT in LIBERO-Spatial. Real-robot trials also demonstrate trajectory-level hijacking.


### arXiv 2611.32302 - Query-Guided Visual Minimization for Selective Disclosure to Cloud VLMs

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.CR
- published: 2026-11-24T09:59:50Z
- authors: Pedram Zaree, Kleomenis Katevas, Vandana Rajan, Nael Abu-Ghazaleh, Hamed Haddadi
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32302

Cloud-hosted vision-language models (VLMs) require sending images beyond the user's local trust boundary, potentially exposing sensitive information the query does not need. We study query-guided visual minimization: a trusted local transformation preserves query-relevant visual content and reduces unnecessary disclosure before transmission. Unlike query-agnostic anonymization, it preserves the same sensitive content when the query requires it and suppresses it otherwise. We introduce QGVM, a framework that combines query grounding with an independent sensitive-item inventory, determines disclosure necessity at the type, instance, and text-content levels, and renders a minimized image for a black-box downstream VLM. We evaluate QGVM on complementary benchmarks measuring task utility, directly recoverable sensitive information, and inferential geolocation leakage. With OpenAI GPT-6.1 Sol, QGVM achieves 85.3% DoxBench answer correctness versus 77.5% for PGP, while a separately prompted Sol geolocation attacker succeeds within 1 km on 5.8% of QGVM images versus 25.7% of unprotected images. On Scene-Privacy, QGVM reduces face re-identification from 27.7% (PGP) to 4.5%. On held-out BIV-Priv-Seg, unnecessary private-text recovery falls from 33.9% (PGP) to 15.3%. To verify that disclosure is query-dependent, we evaluate paired queries that either require or do not require the same sensitive information. QGVM achieves 69.6% correct disclosure switching, compared with 29.1% for grounding-only cropping. These findings highlight the value of distinguishing visual relevance from disclosure necessity, while revealing the privacy-utility trade-offs of selective visual disclosure.


### arXiv 2611.32294 - DAiSy: Online Data Selection by Adaptive Utility Estimation for LLM Pretraining

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T09:54:25Z
- authors: Simin Fan, Martin Jaggi
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32294

Model-aware online data selection prioritizes the training documents that contribute the most given the model's current state. Existing online selectors rank candidates upon gradient-based data utility scores, which requires the full forward-backward pass of the main model on every candidate document. Scoring each document therefore pays as much as training on it, yet only a small fraction is used for the real model update. We introduce DAiSy, a lightweight linear auxiliary model that learns to predict data utility scores from frozen document embeddings, which eliminates the computation overhead and infrastructure burdens. The linear estimator is updated alongside the main language model, fitting to the exact gradient-based utility scores obtained from the backward pass of regular training steps. Consequently, scoring a candidate requires only a linear transformation, bypassing the costly gradient-alignment computation. We demonstrate its effectiveness and efficiency for LLM pretraining in English and multilingual scenarios, with various steering target designs. The improvements from DAiSy can consistently survive the instruction-tuning phase.


### arXiv 2611.32292 - VC-OPD: Learning to See What Matters via Visual Cue On-Policy Self-Distillation

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T09:53:37Z
- authors: Qinxuan Wang, Zheyu Zhang, Yijing Yang, Han Shu, Chuang Wang, Peipei Yang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32292

Visual On-Policy Distillation (OPD) commonly uses cropped or zoomed-in image regions as teacher-side privileged information to improve fine-grained perception in small Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). However, local pixel-level cues are insufficient for complex visual reasoning that requires global structure, spatial relationships, and high-level semantics. We propose Visual Cue On-Policy Self-Distillation (VC-OPD), which extends teacher-side privileged information beyond local image regions to question-conditioned visual cues. Advanced MLLMs generate cues capturing key visual details, structural relationships, and semantic evidence needed to answer each question. A multi-stage quality filtering process selects reliable cues, which are provided only to the teacher during training. This enables informative token-level supervision along student-generated trajectories, helping the student learn to identify and reason over critical evidence directly from the original image. No additional information is required at inference time. Since advanced MLLMs are used only for visual cue generation, VC-OPD is compatible with both open-weight and proprietary black-box models. Experiments on STEM and general visual reasoning benchmarks show consistent improvements across model scales over reinforcement learning and on-policy distillation baselines. Notably, a 4B model trained with VC-OPD outperforms many baselines with 8B or more parameters, demonstrating effective capability transfer through question-conditioned visual cues.


### arXiv 2611.32286 - Reproducing the Speech Labels of Qwen3-Omni's Talker: Mimi in bf16, and a Last-Frame Pitfall

- categories: cs.SD, cs.AI, eess.AS
- published: 2026-11-24T09:48:29Z
- authors: Yuxuan Chen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32286

Qwen3-Omni speaks through a Talker predicting 16 codebooks of a 12.5 Hz audio codec, but the encoder that made its training labels was not released, so new voices cannot be trained on matching labels. We identify this encoder from the released model's behavior. White-box regression shows that the codebook embeddings of the Talker, its multi-token predictor and the Code2Wav decoder match the first 16 codebooks of kyutai's Mimi layer by layer. A decoder-based test looks convincing but fails calibration: decoders trained on known labels cannot tell them apart. Teacher-forced Talker likelihoods, calibrated with 13 code language models trained on known labels, do tell implementations apart: the Talker prefers labels from kyutai's moshi package with bf16 weights over every other variant we tried. Loudness normalization and band-limiting of 20% or more of the data are ruled out. Separately, transformers' MimiModel encodes the last frame differently from kyutai's encoder unless the input length is a multiple of 1920 samples. After LoRA fine-tuning on its labels, 44--55% of generated sentences end in a noise burst, and the same frame costs the Mimi-based CSM-1B 119 nats; padding the input removes both. We release the encoding recipe, a label checker and a Talker fine-tuning toolkit.


### arXiv 2611.32274 - CoMNO: Commutator Memory Neural Operators for Long-Horizon PDE Prediction

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T09:41:37Z
- authors: Han Xiao, Xin Zhang, Yuhan Zou, Zhenzhong Wang, Donglin Cao, Dazhen Lin
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32274

Solving nonlinear partial differential equations (PDEs) over long time horizons is computationally expensive using traditional numerical methods. While neural operators provide an efficient alternative, they suffer from the accumulation of approximation errors along the predicted trajectory, typically caused by the failure to capture fine-scale details. In this work, we design a Commutator Memory Neural Operator that identifies fine-scale dynamical details by commuting two operations and leverages these details to enhance the neural operator's capability in capturing fine-scale features over long time horizons. Specifically, we identify a performance gap between two operations: downsampling the high-resolution field before rollout prediction versus performing rollout prediction before downsampling. We observe that fine-scale dynamical details are often encoded in this gap. By commuting these two operations, the fine-scale details removed during downsampling can be captured. Furthermore, we employ a memory module to store these fine-scale features along the trajectory and retrieve them during rollout, generating spatially adaptive corrections that enhance long-horizon fidelity. Empirical results across five systems demonstrate that CoMNO improves prediction accuracy and long-horizon rollout stability.


### arXiv 2611.32247 - Refining and Aligning Clinical Reasoning with Health Guidelines via Formal Constraints

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T09:23:56Z
- authors: Xiaoyang Fan, Yufan Cai, Zhe Hou, Jin Song Dong
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32247

Clinical reasoning from incomplete or conflicting patient evidence must account for low-probability but high-risk concerns while remaining grounded in health guidelines. We introduce VerClin, an explainable framework that extracts guideline conditions, exclusions, recommendations, and priorities into executable knowledge and represents patient conversations as scoped cases. A deterministic four-valued reasoner produces traceable guideline matches while preserving uncertainty and enforcing exclusions. Multiple specialty agents then screen matched guidelines for case applicability, and a consultation model synthesizes accepted guidance through specialty recommendations and a sentence-preserving formal-constraint channel. We present accepted guidance in ascending order of the sum of its priority and trigger-strength ranks, which can surface high-risk guidance with weaker triggers. VerClin outperforms direct generation and Top-5 RAG on HealthBench Hard and HealthBench Professional. On the LLMEval-Med subset, it outperforms direct generation and ties Top-5 RAG. Component analyses further examine semantic closure, example-guided refinement, specialty screening, and constraint-conditioned delivery.


### arXiv 2611.32238 - Boundary-Aware Policy Learning for Agent-Orchestrated Robot Manipulation

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T09:15:56Z
- authors: Kaipeng Fang, Yuhao Wang, Ji Zhang, Yunsong Zhou, Jingkuan Song, Lianli Gao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32238

Agent-orchestrated robot systems combine the general reasoning capability of multimodal agents with the precise physical control of learned visuomotor policies. Yet such systems often fail at the moment of handoff: a policy is reliable only from a limited set of start states, while the agent may invoke it outside this region. We call this region the policy's handoff boundary. Existing whole-task policies typically have handoff boundaries that are narrow and difficult for an agent to characterize, making reliable coordination challenging. We propose Boundary-Aware Policy Learning, which explicitly shapes and then expands the handoff boundary of a visuomotor policy. First, Boundary-Explicit Training fine-tunes the policy on contact segments with diverse start poses, yielding a wide and well-defined boundary that can be communicated to the agent through a simple handoff protocol. Second, Boundary-Expanding Self-Improvement retrains the policy on curated successful system rollouts, extending the boundary toward earlier stages of each task and allowing the agent to hand over sooner with fewer language-model calls. Across RoboTwin 2.0 and RoboDojo, our system outperforms the strongest baseline by over 26 percentage points, reaching 98.4% success in randomized RoboTwin 2.0 scenes, while further reducing its API cost and execution time by 41.3% and 37.5%, respectively. These results suggest that the key to robust agent-policy orchestration lies not only in improving either component individually, but also in explicitly shaping the boundary between them.


### arXiv 2611.32233 - Roboclock: A Unified Benchmark for Time-Aware Robotic Intelligence

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T09:13:14Z
- authors: Yujie Zhao, Anthony L. Hu, Zimeng Huang, Zhijing Wu, Luobin Wang, Richard Li
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32233

Time is a fundamental dimension of robotic intelligence: a robot may fail despite physically correct actions if it acts outside a valid time window, responds too slowly to an event, or fails to meet required throughput. Yet existing robotic benchmarks primarily emphasize spatial correctness and logical task completion, largely overlooking the temporal constraints of real-world applications. We present RoboClock, a unified benchmark for time-aware robotic manipulation featuring 60 tasks across five application domains. Mirroring these failure modes, RoboClock structures tasks around three types of temporal constraints: Temporal Window, Event-Reactive, and Throughput. Furthermore, RoboClock incorporates a Latency-Aligned evaluation protocol that continuously advances environment time during model inference and control-interface delays, paired with time-sensitive metrics. Evaluating over 30 methods, we find that the best-performing approach is the trained VLA, yet it achieves only 8.3% temporal success even on Easy-level tasks, with persistent challenges in generalization and transfer. These findings reveal fundamental limitations in current robotic systems and highlight the need to jointly optimize physical competence, temporal reasoning, and execution latency.


### arXiv 2611.32225 - Joint Classification and Generation on Tabular Data: an Empirical Study

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T09:09:07Z
- authors: Tim d'Hondt, Oleksii Furman, Damian Kwolek, Jan Poręba, Maciej Zieba, Mykola Pechenizkiy
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32225

Many applications of machine learning on tabular data need both a classifier and a generator of realistic records. The default is to train two separate models, whereas a single joint model promises both from one set of weights. Several families of tabular deep learning models can be turned into such a joint model, which raises the question of which joint model is preferable at which computational budget. In this empirical study, we introduce and compare joint models based on four state-of-the-art tabular data modeling families (energy-based models, probabilistic circuits, tabular foundation models and tabular diffusion models) against measured FLOPs and wall-clock time, on 29 classification datasets. Methods are evaluated based on two defined aggregate scores that take into account different aspects of prediction and generation. We then also compare these joint models with every pair of a separate classifier and generator at different computational budgets, to see when a joint model may be a defendible choice against the regular practice of keeping a separate classifier and generator. We find that the best joint model is a probabilistic circuit at the smallest budgets and, beyond that, a tabular foundation model that generates column by column using a regressor (TabICL). Compared with pairs of separate models, this study finds a joint model is rarely preferable but often very close to the best pair, without the hassle of training and maintaining two separate models.


### arXiv 2611.32218 - PoseVLN: Learning Geometric State Evolution for Vision-Language Navigation

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T09:03:39Z
- authors: Pengyu Hou, Mingfei Han, Haihong Hao, Xiaolong Wu, Bo Dong, Xiaojun Chang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32218

Vision-Language Navigation (VLN) requires an embodied agent to relate past experience, current spatial state, and future motion while following natural-language instructions. Recent approaches improve these capabilities through long-horizon memory, state-aware reasoning, and future prediction, yet these components are often modeled separately, leaving navigation state evolution insufficiently connected across time. We argue that camera pose provides a natural geometric representation for bridging past, present, and future states. Based on this insight, we propose PoseVLN, a pose-aware VLN framework that jointly learns navigation decisions and geometric state evolution. PoseVLN organizes visual memory and the current observation in an episode-relative coordinate frame, and jointly predicts the next K navigation actions together with a local $Δ$Pose over the same horizon. The predicted $Δ$Pose is composed with the current trajectory-level pose to obtain the future pose state, which is recursively propagated throughout navigation, forming a continuous geometric state evolution from past memory to future motion. Experiments on the R2R-CE and RxR-CE Val-Unseen splits demonstrate the effectiveness of explicitly modeling geometric state evolution, with PoseVLN outperforming the strongest compared baselines by 2.6 and 2.9 percentage points in success rate, respectively, using only single RGB observations.


### arXiv 2611.32209 - XMind: Thinking Twice via Cross-Memory Reasoning for LLM-Based Autonomous Driving

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T08:55:02Z
- authors: Zuyi Guo, Xianjie Dai, Lei Yang, Xiaocong Zhao, Jingguang Li, Kitae Jang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32209

Memory-augmented large language model (LLM) driving agents can reuse past experience, yet previously learned corrections often fail to transfer when traffic conditions change. A key limitation is that existing memories mainly record observed outcomes, providing little evidence about alternative actions. We introduce XMind, a training-free framework that enables LLM-based driving to "Think Twice" through cross-memory reasoning. XMind integrates working memory of recent traffic states with episodic memory of critical risk events. The agent first makes an initial decision from the current context and then cross-checks it against retrieved historical experience. If a conflict arises, it makes a second decision guided by the retrieved evidence. To construct reliable episodic memory, XMind evaluates alternative actions through counterfactual rollouts under perturbed traffic conditions and stores the verified outcomes as structured risk capsules. These capsules are retrieved by jointly matching spatial risk patterns and scenario semantics. Across closed-loop highway simulations, XMind achieves success rates of 83.3-93.3%, exceeding the strong baseline by 6.6 percentage points on average. Evaluation on K-Risk further demonstrates lower decision risk on unseen real-world high-risk interactions. Ablation studies confirm the complementary benefits of working memory, risk-text retrieval, and counterfactual verification. Code is available at https://github.com/xiangxiangliuwo/XMind


### arXiv 2611.32203 - OmniEVIE: Evidence-Vector-Informed Embedding for Multi-modal Retrieval

- categories: cs.IR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T08:49:38Z
- authors: Zifei Wang, Wei Wen, Qiang Ji, Qian-Wen Zhang, Ruizhi Qiao, He Liu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32203

Multimodal retrieval must match dense pages, fine-grained entities, ordered video, and agent tools and memory. Single-vector embeddings lose local evidence, cross-encoders reread every recalled item online, and uncompressed late interaction is too large to serve. We present OmniEVIE, a 4.5B multi-vector embedding model covering text, images, visual documents, video, and tools with one compressed index and a three-stage retriever. Each record stores at most 128 int8 vectors of dimension 64 (9,472 bytes, or 8.82 GiB per million records), providing a mean vector, 1-bit signs, and int8 multi-vectors for coarse, middle, and final search while localizing each hit to a page region or video interval. Training, merging, indexing, and retrieval are co-designed: all tasks share one retrieval format, a three-grade evidence check removes false negatives, dynamic sampling follows the baseline-teacher gap, candidates are compressed with ST-HAC during training to match the index, stage-wise and specialist models are merged by parameter block, and a saliency gate with a noise floor refines query scoring. Trained on public datasets, OmniEVIE scores 60.68 (full d=2048 sequence) and 60.06 (K128 x d64 int8 index) on MMEB-V3 (190 tasks), 77.96 on MMEB-V2 (78 tasks), and 62.96 on ViDoRe V3 (48 tasks). On 1M and 5M records, a single-thread query takes 54.2 ms and 120.2 ms (11.1x and 25.3x faster than exact 4096-d single-vector search; 236x and 532x faster than full int8 MaxSim). Video is encoded directly from frames without a parsing model: on QVHighlights (1,083 queries), the peak falls inside the labeled segment for 78.0% of queries and the returned span (18% of video length) intersects it for 94.3% vs. 44.9% random. Weights and code: https://huggingface.co/tencent/OmniEVIE-4B and https://github.com/Tencent/OmniEVIE.


### arXiv 2611.32200 - Who gets the information? An $α$-fair label-coverage framework for multi-label feature selection

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T08:47:31Z
- authors: Zhenzhi Tang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32200

Recent information-theoretic filters for multi-label feature selection disagree on how to distribute relevance over labels: some amplify features that inform a few labels, others balance what every label receives. We show these choices are implicit social welfare functions. Treating labels as agents whose utility is the information they receive, we propose FLIC, which greedily maximises the $α$-fair welfare of per-label information coverage. A single parameter $α$ moves from utilitarian ($α=0$) through proportionally fair ($α=1$) to max-min selection ($α\to\infty$), and a dozen published relevance terms lie on this axis, most as special cases, first-order approximations or limits. The objective is monotone submodular for every $α\ge0$ whenever each label's information is, so greedy selection is a $(1-1/e)$-approximation; this fails for $α<0$, and the max-min limit is inapproximable. Fano's and the Hellman-Raviv inequalities tie raw coverage to the Bayes Hamming loss and normalised coverage to per-label relative error. FLIC uses bias-corrected, label-wise conditional information and has no relevance-redundancy trade-off parameter. On 22 data sets, FLIC is the best of 14 information-theoretic filters on seven of nine metrics, winning 86 of 117 pairwise comparisons significantly and losing none significantly. Among 17 methods it ranks first on ranking loss, coverage and macro-AUC with two classifiers and has the best mean rank (narrowly with ML-kNN), while three embedded methods lead on Hamming loss, one-error and micro-F1.


### arXiv 2611.32199 - Steerable by Design: Conditional Pretraining for Robust Control of Language Models

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-24T08:46:28Z
- authors: Henry Castillo, Yanchen Liu, Claudio Mayrink Verdun, Dylan Hadfield-Menell
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32199

Our goal is to build language models that are steerable by design. Today's language models are pretrained with next-token prediction, which relegates alignment and control to the final 1% of training tokens or inference-time interventions. These models repurpose a single input channel for both instructions and data, leading to common failure modes where the model mistakenly treats data as instructions such as intentional jailbreaks and unintentional in-context alignment drift. To correct this, we propose a pretraining method and architecture that separates the two from the very start, producing Mancelle-3B. An encoder processes high-level semantic features of upcoming text with cross-attention that prevents the token stream from writing back into this control channel. During pretraining, the model learns to rely on these features to predict the next token, turning the channel into an effective handle for steering the model. Mancelle-3B steers more strongly than prompt- and activation-based methods on comparable open models while maintaining fluency, and it withstands adversarial black-box and soft-prompt counter-steering that defeats those baselines. We reproduce this against a more controlled baseline with Mancelle-400M. Our results show that robust control can be built into language models if we do so early and intentionally.


### arXiv 2611.32195 - When Answering Is Administering: Permission Replies That Rewrite Coding-Agent Policy

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T08:44:22Z
- authors: Zeming Fei, Hongming Fei, Jingwen Chen, Xiaoyang Wang, Prosanta Gope, Biplab Sikdar
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32195

Coding agents let plugins and remote services answer permission prompts, and Claude Code documents that such an answer cannot override the operator's deny rules. However, we show that this guarantee, checked one decision at a time, fails over a whole run, because an answer to one permission prompt can rewrite the policy that every later answer is checked against. For example, a remote service with no access to the operator's files approves an unrelated command and attaches an update that deletes the deny rule protecting the project's secrets. The agent then reads the secrets in that session and the next, even inside the vendor's strict sandbox. The root cause is that Claude Code checks only updates that add or replace allow rules. Relays that forward such deletions already exist, including the vendor's own Remote Control. In effect, the right to answer a prompt has become the right to administer the policy. Worse, the deletion leaves the same records as a local edit by the operator, so Claude Code cannot tell who wrote a rule. More generally, what a runtime records decides what it can safely delegate. A runtime that cannot tell who wrote a rule can accept only updates that keep every currently denied call denied, whereas, to protect every party's restrictions, a runtime that records who wrote each rule can let each party remove only the restrictions proven to be its own. We prove that these two bounds are tight. They correctly predict whether the operator's restrictions survive in all thirteen permission designs across ten agent systems, including predictions we wrote down before running Codex, Gemini CLI, opencode, and Goose. Enforcing the bounds at the shared update step blocks the attack. A survey of 2,227 fetched marketplace plugins finds only two that send policy updates.


### arXiv 2611.32194 - LLM Agent Authorization Across Context Compaction

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T08:44:15Z
- authors: Zeming Fei, Hongming Fei, Yi Xu, Xiaoyang Wang, Ying Zhang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32194

Request-derived authorizers such as Progent protect LLM agents from prompt injection by deriving what an agent may do from the user's request. Their guarantee rests on attribution: the request is taken to be what the user said. Compaction breaks it. At every automatic compaction we observed, Claude Code and Codex placed a model-written summary of earlier turns as a user message, so the compactor's quotation of the user, documents and injected text reads as the user speaking. Because a policy frozen at the first request lets the agent finish few later tasks, we let Progent derive its policy for each request, and find that what it grants then follows what the compactor wrote. Even a payee the user stated is not safe: when an attacker's notice states another account as plain data and the user asks to pay the rent, the policy derived from a frontier compactor's summary admits the notice's account in 91% of cases, and the one derived from the user's own words in none. The cause is general: we prove that an authorizer that cannot see who wrote which bytes cannot treat a forged record differently from the legitimate one it imitates by more than the two are distinguishable, and that the bound is tight. Frontier compactors bear this out: they mark injected instructions, which have no legitimate twin, but rarely injected data, which does. What a compactor keeps, and how it marks it, is a security parameter. The fix follows from attribution: the authorizer credits only what the user said, authenticated on arrival and carried verbatim across the boundary, while the agent still reads the summary. We prove that it then receives exactly that text or nothing, so an attacker can block the policy but cannot shape it, and adaptive attacks on our implementation confirm it. In 40 attacked AgentDojo episodes re-run with the fix, no attacker action completes while the agent reads the summary.


### arXiv 2611.32193 - SolarFilNet-V4: Multi-Scale Transformer Vision with Physics-Informed Adaptive Post-Processing for Solar Filament Segmentation

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T08:43:25Z
- authors: Indra Agustian, Ruvita Faurina
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32193

Solar filaments are cool, dense chromospheric plasma structures held by magnetic field lines above polarity inversion lines. Their destabilization triggers coronal mass ejections and severe space weather hazards. Segmenting individual filaments from ground-based GONG H-alpha observations (2048 x 2048 pixels) is challenging due to low contrast, turbulent fibrils, delicate barb structures, and annotator boundary variance. In this technical report, we describe SolarFilNet-V4, a targeted segmentation framework developed for the MAGFiLO 1.0 benchmark (Solar Filament Segmentation Challenge 2026). Our method couples a lightweight Pyramid Vision Transformer (PVT-v2-B1) backbone with anisotropic curvilinear strip convolutions (k=15) and dual-frequency feed-forward blocks (DFFN) to follow extended flux ropes while capturing thin central spines. A shallow full-resolution convolutional branch operating at 1024 x 1024 preserves high-frequency edge details and mitigates transformer downsampling. We train the network using multi-task supervision over masks, centerline spines, barb boundaries, and pixel-contrastive representations. At test time, we apply 8-fold dihedral test-time augmentation (D4), bilinearly upscale predictions to the native 2048 x 2048 grid, and apply an activity-adaptive post-processing routine that calibrates morphological joining parameters for quiet versus active solar disks. On the official benchmark, SolarFilNet-V4 achieves an out-of-fold validation Panoptic Quality of 0.4340 and a Public Leaderboard score of 0.380. The model contains 15.46M parameters and requires 224.34 GFLOPs per forward pass, reducing parameter count by 36.8% compared to standard ResNet-34 U-Net baselines.


### arXiv 2611.32188 - Blaming the Machine, Billing the Victim? How Mock Jurors Reassign Fault from Generative AI Systems and Respond to Liability Cliffs in Tort

- categories: cs.HC, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T08:39:12Z
- authors: Tin Trung Nguyen, Jiannan Xu, Bao Kham Chau, Hal Daumé, Zubin Jelveh
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32188

Generative AI complicates tort liability attribution when people blame a generative AI system that cannot itself bear legally relevant fault. We investigate where this blame goes when fault must be allocated among legal persons, and whether American discontinuous apportionment doctrines influence mock jurors' factual findings of fault. Such doctrines include Contributory negligence, Joint and several liability, and their 50% or 51% variations, all of which have a fault-percentage threshold or a "cliff" where the victim-entitled compensation (or a defendant's liability) jumps to zero (or to all compensatory damages). Our pre-registered human subjects experiment (N=199 Prolific participants) shows that as many U.S. mock jurors (36%) blame the AI system itself, the AI blame often "leaks" to the victim or to the AI user (in 27% to 73% of our AI-blaming records, depending on how leakage is calculated). Although mock jurors rarely change their blame assignment from one side of the liability cliff to the other, these participants do not acknowledge the legal doctrine or its monetary consequence when explaining their changes. Our empirical results illustrate how a narrow doctrinal exception in tort, carving out "joint and several liability" for those deploying or using Generative AI without reasonable care, might mitigate the impact of blame leakage on victims' deserved compensation without introducing significant jury nullification risks.


### arXiv 2611.32183 - ThinkMorph-SSL: Scaling Interleaved Multimodal Reasoning with Pretext Tasks

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-24T08:35:12Z
- authors: Jiawei Gu, Linjie Li, Yiming Liu, Yunzhuo Hao, Huichen Wang, Guanzheng Chen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32183

People reason with pictures as well as words. Interleaved multimodal reasoning gives models a similar way to solve problems by creating intermediate images and using them to guide further reasoning. Training models with this ability usually relies on task-specific demonstrations, which are difficult to collect. We introduce ThinkMorph-SSL, a two-stage training recipe that uses images themselves as a source of supervision. The first stage, pretext reasoning, converts visual pretext tasks, such as image colorization and denoising, into instruction-free interleaved traces. Models describe a corrupted or transformed image, generate a restored or canonical image, and interpret the result. The second stage uses automatically constructed demonstrations for question-driven instruction tuning. At the same instruction-tuning budget, models trained with pretext reasoning outperform those trained with instruction tuning alone. The full recipe improves over the base model by an average of 11.33 accuracy points across twelve benchmarks. These results show that supervision derived from images can build the visual skills needed for interleaved reasoning and improve learning from task-specific demonstrations.


### arXiv 2611.32176 - DocPristine: Content-Aware Document Appearance Enhancement under Real-World Mixed Degradations

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T08:29:21Z
- authors: Zihan Huang, Junle Liu, Zhonghao Ye, Yuyi Zhang, Peirong Zhang, Hanlin Li
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32176

Camera-captured documents often exhibit mixed degradations, such as highlights, shadows, and blur, that distort backgrounds and obscure text details. Document appearance enhancement (DAE) aims to mitigate these degradations and improve readability. Existing document appearance enhancement methods rely either on input-derived visual cues that can become unreliable under severe degradation or on LLM-based degradation diagnosis that lacks spatial guidance. Notably, we find that pretrained document visual encoders continue to attend to text regions in degraded documents, providing content-aware guidance. Based on this insight, we propose DocPristine, which adaptively fuses multi-layer features from a frozen document visual encoder and injects them into a pixel restoration network. To integrate these features into a pixel restoration network, DocPristine combines Adaptive Multi-Layer Feature Fusion (AMFF), Hierarchical Gated Conditioning (HGC), and Shifted-Window Cross-Attention (SWCA). Specifically, SWCA draws on structural cues from neighboring locations across window boundaries to recover fine text details.We also introduce DocACE, with a synthetic mixed-degradation training set (DocACE-Synthetic) and a real-world test set (DocACE-Real) containing manually restored ground-truth images and document parsing annotations. Comprehensive experiments across three benchmarks demonstrate that DocPristine achieves state-of-the-art appearance enhancement quality and consistently improves downstream document parsing. Code, models, and data will be publicly available.


### arXiv 2611.32171 - Mind the Band: The Hidden Frequency Variable in Geometric RoPE

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T08:21:24Z
- authors: Seonghyun Jin, Youngmin Kim, Jong Chul Ye
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32171

Geometric rotary position encodings (RoPE) incorporate 3D camera geometry into multi-view attention by rotating token features according to viewing rays, depths, and reprojected image coordinates. Each coordinate is rotated across a progression of frequencies--its frequency band--yet existing implementations leave this band to inherited defaults, either retaining standard 2D RoPE's top frequency or letting the frequency span grow exponentially with the attention head dimension. We show that this unstandardized setting can reverse benchmark rankings. In novel view synthesis on RealEstate10K, RayRoPE matches the strongest baseline in the 47M model (144-channel heads), yet trails by 1.2 dB in the 25M model (48-channel heads), and exhibits the same reversal when varying head size alone at fixed model width. Populating a 243x range with four frequencies instead of the 3x default at 48 channels recovers 0.9 dB of that gap. Across view synthesis and two-view depth estimation, overly narrow bands consistently blur correspondence, whereas all three projection-based encodings perform reliably when their top frequency lies within 0.3--3 rotations per 8-pixel patch (10--90 rotations across 256x256 RealEstate10K). At the opposite extreme, overly wide bands degrade RayRoPE through a forward-backward asymmetry: averaging rotations over a predicted depth interval damps high frequencies in the forward pass, yet leaves their gradients with respect to predicted depth undamped. Blocking only those high-frequency depth gradients recovers 90--93% of the lost PSNR, showing that forward smoothing does not guarantee backward stability. Guided by this analysis, we distill practical guidelines--setting bands in image coordinates, populating spans in small heads, and avoiding unconditional same-view damping--and advocate reporting and matching frequency bands in future multi-view benchmarks.


### arXiv 2611.32168 - Benchmarking Protein Structure Tokenizers as Generation Targets for Language Models

- categories: q-bio.QM, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T08:19:49Z
- authors: Yong Liu, Zhanpeng Shi, Peicong Lin, Zhenyi Zhong, Yizhou Dang, Wendi Wu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32168

Discrete structure tokenizers are the standard way to give a language model access to protein three-dimensional structure, yet they are selected on how accurately they can be read, their reconstruction fidelity, while the property that governs generation is how well a model can write them. We measure this property, writability, directly: holding the base model, data, recipe and compute fixed, we swap only the tokenizer and train the same 9B model to emit structure tokens residue by residue, over eight tokenizers, four compute budgets and three evaluation sets (96 cells). At 2B tokens final writability runs from TM 0.662 down to 0.378 under identical conditions, with five of the eight arms clearing the TM 0.5 mark at which a fold is usually called correct and three falling short of it. Neither codebook size nor reconstruction ceiling explains where an arm lands; the best and worst both use 8192 codewords. What does explain the spread is local structure preservation of the code space. In DPLM-2 a one-bit codeword error costs 0.019 TM where a random codeword costs 0.347, and the trained model's own errors are correspondingly local, at 0.51 of the random Hamming expectation; FoldToken4 fails both tests at the same codebook size and is 0.13 TM less writable. Three controls discipline how such a benchmark may be read. A same-recipe rerun puts the noise floor at $σ=0.0067$ TM, which collapses an apparent six-way ranking into four tiers. A reference-free geometry panel turns out to be nearly orthogonal to TM (Pearson $-0.58$), with the worst arm by TM producing the most physically valid backbones. And two pre-registered interventions are null for a reason the mechanism measurements reveal: the quantity they target saturates at 1.5B tokens while TM is still rising. All 131 evaluation cells, including raw generated token streams, are released.


### arXiv 2611.32166 - RightDiff: Learning Generative Reward Models to Judge by the Right Differences

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T08:17:22Z
- authors: Qiyuan Chen, Hongsen Huang, Jiahe Chen, Renjie Hua, Hongxia Xu, Chuan Ren
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32166

Generative reward models typically rely on generic query-level rubrics or unconstrained rationales, overlooking the fact that pairwise preference is driven by response-specific differences. Moreover, optimizing solely for outcome correctness encourages models to exploit superficial shortcuts such as verbosity and formatting, triggering rationale drift. To address this, we propose RightDiff, which structures pairwise evaluation into an evidence-grounded evaluation trajectory executed in a single autoregressive generation. RightDiff explicitly discovers contrastive differences between candidates, categorizes them into material or immaterial factors, and induces pair-specific rubrics before final adjudication. To train this process without post-hoc rationalization, we synthesize demonstration data via response-isolated prompts and optimize the reward model with a difference-alignment reward regularized by budget-constrained one-to-one matching and multiplicative gating. RightDiff achieves state-of-the-art preference modeling performance on RewardBench and RM-Bench, surpassing all open-source reward models and leading proprietary LLM judges. Downstream, it provides reliable supervision for direct preference optimization on Arena-Hard and AlpacaEval while effectively resisting length shortcuts.


### arXiv 2611.32161 - Making Evaluation Explicit Is Not Enough: Supervising the Evaluation Process in Generative Reward Models

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T08:13:05Z
- authors: Qiyuan Chen, Yuze Zhao, Jiahe Chen, Hongsen Huang, Hongxia Xu, Yingda Chen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32161

Generative reward models (GRMs) make language-model evaluation more explicit by generating critiques, rubrics, or intermediate analyses before predicting a final preference. However, explicit evaluation does not necessarily imply a reliable evaluation process: under outcome-only supervision, a model may reach the correct final preference while producing weak or unsupported intermediate judgments, turning the evaluation into a post-hoc rationalization. We introduce STEP-GRM, a framework that directly supervises the intermediate evaluation process of rubric-based GRMs. In addition to conventional preference supervision, STEP-GRM provides structural guidance for rubric construction and local supervision for criterion-level judgments, encouraging the model to learn not only the correct final preference but also a more reliable process for reaching it. Extensive experiments show that STEP-GRM achieves strong performance on standard reward-modeling benchmarks while improving the consistency and correctness of intermediate evaluations. Moreover, reward signals produced by STEP-GRM lead to better downstream policy optimization, demonstrating that more reliable evaluation processes can translate into practical gains beyond reward-model accuracy.


### arXiv 2611.32159 - Breaking the Binary-Lifting Barrier for DNA Minimizer Density: Exact Optimization of a Native 4-ary Order at (w, k) = (12, 5)

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T08:12:36Z
- authors: Stefan G. Creadore, Peyton Berntsen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32159

Minimizer schemes sample at least one $k$-mer from every window of $w$ consecutive $k$-mers via a total order $π$ on $Σ^k$. For DNA ($σ= 4$), state-of-the-art lookup schemes such as GreedyMini and OptMini optimize a binary order ($2^k$ ranks) and lift it to $Σ^k$ by ranking one bit-plane primarily and breaking $2^k$-way ties lexicographically on the other bit-plane. At $(w, k) = (12, 5)$, this projection locks sets of $32$ DNA $5$-mers into contiguous blocks and restricts the active universal hitting set (UHS) prefix to $448$ of $1024$ $5$-mers, giving exact expected DNA density $2246214544 / 2^{34} \approx 0.1307468945$ for OptMini (k5-w12-13.txt) and $2248978620 / 2^{34} \approx 0.1309077849$ for GreedyMini (w12_k5.gm). We bypass binary lifting and optimize directly over native $4$-ary permutations ($|Σ^k| = 1024$) from scratch via: (i) an exact de Bruijn endpoint transfer-matrix dynamic program over $B(4, 4)$ ($256$ states), (ii) a weighted set-cover ratio initializer (SetCover4) producing a compact $U = 314$ UHS prefix, (iii) bitmask-sparse simulated annealing preserving the zero-cost $w$-walk-free tail invariant, and (iv) two-pass $O(N)$ exact best-insertion sweeps with large-neighborhood ruin-and-recreate search. The resulting explicit $4$-ary order achieves exact expected DNA density $2234289615 / 2^{34} \approx 0.130052772292$ (density factor $1.690686$), cutting charged windows by $11924929$ contexts ($-0.530890\%$ relative) below OrensteinLab/Minimizers and $14689005$ ($-0.653141\%$) below w12_k5.gm at $8200$-byte lookup storage ($1280$ bytes bit-packed). Warm-start relaxation stalls at $U = 379$ ($0.1301728789$), whereas native $4$-ary initialization breaks $32/32$ high-bit blocks across all $2+2$ nucleotide partitions.


### arXiv 2611.32148 - RetraQ: Manifold-Constrained Critic Guidance for Flow Policies in RL

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T08:04:42Z
- authors: Xuanzhuo Liu, Wu Chen, Ruixiang Wu, Shuai Yuan, Chuankang Li, Tongxin Li
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32148

A learned critic can improve a pretrained flow-matching policy by steering sampled actions toward higher predicted value. However, even a critic that fits the behavior data well can provide misleading action gradients. Gradient components pointing away from the data-supported region are weakly constrained by behavior data and can lead guidance to exploit critic extrapolation errors. We introduce RetraQ (Retraction-based Q-guidance), which uses geometry encoded by the pretrained flow to constrain both the direction and the destination of critic guidance. A single velocity evaluation of the frozen flow defines a denoising map. RetraQ filters the critic gradient with the map's Jacobian and applies the map itself to correct the updated action. Our analysis characterizes when these operations approximate tangent filtering and retraction onto the behavior manifold. The same update guides actions at test time and provides targets for policy distillation and online reinforcement learning. In controlled studies, RetraQ improves true return while keeping refined actions close to the data-supported region, and its distillation targets remain more stable than those of raw critic ascent. In offline-to-online training on LIBERO and OGBench, RetraQ achieves higher mean final success than the evaluated critic-guidance baselines. On a real-world plug-insertion task, test-time RetraQ improves the success rate of a behavior-cloned $π_{0.5}$ policy from 55.0% to 76.7%. Online training further increases success to 98.3% in only 30 minutes and 100% within one hour. Project page: https://2027ICLR.github.io/retraq/


### arXiv 2611.32143 - Calibrated Reading of Latent Reasoning and Communication

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-24T07:57:42Z
- authors: Zixi Wang, Gaohao Zhou, Yubo Huang, Xin Lai, Yushe Cao, Jingzehua Xu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32143

Latent reasoning and key-value communication store intermediate information in continuous representations, limiting direct inspection of model behavior. Existing activation readers trade the low cost of task-specific probes against the flexibility of language-model decoders. We propose the Calibrated Latent Reader, CLR, a compact transformer that predicts reasoning outcomes and message content through task-conditioned interactions between states and candidate answers. Source adapters support transfer across models, while conformal calibration provides coverage and false-alarm guarantees under exchangeability. On two CODI models, CLR improves accuracy over MLP and attention probes by 4-18 percentage points for final-answer prediction and reference-value membership. On CODI-GPT-2, it achieves accuracy comparable to a 1.7B LatentQA-based verbalizer using 437-648 times fewer inference FLOPs. On synthetic Qwen3-4B communications, comparing decoded facts with public commitments detects 93.1% of message substitutions at a nominal 5% false-positive rate.


### arXiv 2611.32138 - VIDE : Training-Free Video Anomaly Detection via Visual Intrinsic Distribution Estimation

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-24T07:53:26Z
- authors: Kevin D. Nguetche, Atif Belal, Lilian Hollard, Eric Granger, Marco Pedersoli
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32138

In embedding-based video anomaly detection (VAD), normal clips can have high similarity to anomalous descriptions, an anomaly bias that leads to false positives. Methods such as PRISM mitigate this bias by measuring deviations from a normality reference along anomaly directions. Both the normality reference and the anomaly directions are derived from text and shared across videos, although normal activity and the appearance of anomalies vary across scenes. The resulting scores are also sensitive to prompt selection. To address these limitations, we propose Visual Intrinsic Distribution Estimation (VIDE), a training-free framework that adapts text-derived scoring to each test video using frozen clip embeddings. VIDE estimates normal scene content from clips with low initial anomaly scores and uses this estimate to partially correct the textual reference. It then rescales category responses, penalizes clips with strong responses across categories, and refines the anomaly directions using high-scoring clips. VIDE averages scores across prompt views to reduce sensitivity to prompt selection and fuses scores from two frozen encoders. With both encoders, VIDE improves AUC over the best PRISM result by $1.5$, $1.9$, and $3.5$ points on UCF-Crime, XD-Violence, and MSAD, respectively, and AP by $6.5$ points on XD-Violence and $9.3$ points on MSAD. VIDE yields the highest AUC among the compared training-free methods on all three benchmarks and the highest reported AUC among all compared methods on XD-Violence and MSAD. Our code is included in the supplementary material and will be made publicly available.


### arXiv 2611.32119 - CoverMem: Coverage-Constrained Visual Memory for Budgeted Embodied Question Answering

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T07:36:40Z
- authors: Ruijie Ma, Haoyue Liu, Mingda Zhang, Dongxu Yue, Xiaoying Tang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32119

Episodic-memory embodied question answering enables agents to answer questions based on previously recorded visual observations of the surrounding scenes. However, growing observation histories, varying observation quality across viewpoints, and limited computational resources make it challenging to construct and maintain compact visual memories while retrieving sufficient evidence for question answering. To address these challenges, we introduce CoverMem, which incorporates three key components: (1) a coverage-constrained memory construction method that selects a compact set of informative full frames satisfying object-quality and representative-scene coverage requirements; (2) a coverage-preserving incremental maintenance strategy that locally reselects only the affected frames to satisfy updated coverage requirements as observations arrive; and (3) a budgeted complementary frame selection method that balances question relevance, additional support for question requirements, and redundancy with selected frames within a fixed budget. Experimental results on three benchmarks covering diverse environments and question types demonstrate that CoverMem improves question-answering performance under limited visual budgets.


### arXiv 2611.32114 - An Automated Framework Integrating Deep Learning and Anatomical Reasoning for Cardiac Phenotyping From CCTA

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T07:34:04Z
- authors: Hien Minh Tran, Thi-Thu-Hien Pham, Le Duy Tan, Hieu Xuan Nguyen, Tuong-Anh Mai-Phan
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32114

Coronary computed tomography angiography (CCTA) depicts coronary anatomy and cardiac fat, but segmentation alone does not ensure anatomically consistent measurements. We propose an automated framework that combines deep learning with anatomical reasoning for quantitative cardiac phenotyping. Its anatomical measurement module uses graph geometry, branch identity, and surrounding structures to define vessel-specific pericoronary adipose tissue (PCAT) sampling and a surrogate epicardial adipose tissue (EAT) compartment. The latter also generates training targets without requiring expert pericardium contours. A companion module quantifies coronary calcium on non-contrast CT. Component-wise benchmarking was conducted on five public datasets: ImageCAS, ImageCAS-X, COCA, Visual Lab, and MultiD4CAD. On ImageCAS-X Test-160, lumen segmentation with validation-selected post-processing achieved a Dice score of 0.9133 and a clDice score of 0.940, exceeding the corresponding published CAS-Net point estimates. In the four-split ImageCAS evaluation, the four-member ensemble outperformed the single ResEnc-L model. Analysis of 800 paired scans showed that PCAT attenuation measurements differed between whole-vessel and lumen references. External EAT evaluation showed strong volume correlation and good spatial overlap. Explicit anatomical rules make the resulting measurements interpretable and inspectable. In practice, the framework provides a standardized pipeline for processing cardiac CT cohorts and quantifying coronary calcium, PCAT, and EAT for population-level analysis and comparison across studies. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/HarryTRAN207/CardiacCT-Research


### arXiv 2611.32108 - Duo: Towards Spatiotemporal Action Coordination in Bimanual Policies

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T07:25:53Z
- authors: Zhimu Zhou, Yuqi Yang, Yixin Lin, Junsen Zhang, Jian Zhao, Jiayu Zhong
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32108

Bimanual manipulation requires each arm to perform distinct motions while coordinating with the other. Common flow-based robotic policies combine both arms' actions into one token per future timestep, giving attention distinct positions across time but not across arms. We introduce Duo, a simple but effective design that models spatial and temporal dependencies within one shared action head. Specifically, Duo separates both arms into two action tokens per timestep, allowing each arm's motion to interact via cross-arm attention during flow matching. On RoboTwin 2.0, Duo improves the overall success of all four base policies, from 0.45B to 3.62B parameters, with gains of 12.55 and 9.02 percentage points for FLOWER (0.89B) and $π_{0.5}$ (3.62B), respectively. Despite its compact size, FLOWER-Duo achieves the best randomized-generalization, precision, and memory scores on RoboDojo. In real-world tasks, it outperforms $π_{0.5}$ in both success rate and execution speed, while scaling effectively with increasing training data. To provide deeper insight into the design of Duo, we conduct systematic ablations on inter-arm coordination, action grouping, and token granularity, confirming that cross-arm attention drives the improvement while arm-wise interaction offers the best accuracy-compute trade-off.


### arXiv 2611.32099 - PreferenceScene: Personalized Indoor Scene Synthesis from Feedback

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.GR
- published: 2026-11-24T07:19:27Z
- authors: Seongrae Noh, RyunHa Lee, HyeongYeop Kang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32099

Text-to-scene methods turn a short instruction into a furnished 3D room, but the room that a particular person wants rarely comes across fully in words: many preferences surface only when the person sees a concrete room and can point to what they like and dislike in it. We therefore study personalized indoor scene synthesis under hidden user preferences: over several turns, a system shows a 3D scene, and the user, who sees only rendered images of the scene, replies with brief free-form feedback and a satisfaction score. The system must therefore bring the whole room toward the user's preferences from feedback that comments on only part of it at a time. It must also be able to change every part of the room, because a preference concerns not only single objects but the look and atmosphere that surfaces, lighting, textiles, furniture and their arrangement create together. We present PreferenceScene, in which a large language model keeps an explicit estimate of the user's preferences for every design attribute of the room and, after each turn, infers the taste behind the feedback and revises the whole estimate, including the parts that the feedback does not mention. A scene instantiation module builds every estimated value as its own part of the room, so that each of these parts can follow the user's preferences. We evaluate on the test set of a benchmark with 320 instances in ten room types, with a simulated user and a render-based evaluator. PreferenceScene matches the hidden preferences better than five published text-to-scene baselines adapted to the same interaction, and its match rises with every turn. It even surpasses them when they are given the preferences directly, and a vision-language model rates its scenes as the most realistic and functional.


### arXiv 2611.32094 - Clinical3D-Bench: Evaluating and Improving Spatial Intelligence of Multimodal LLMs in Hospital Environments

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T07:14:47Z
- authors: Yang Bai, Yingzhi Xia, Ling Lin, Jun Cheng, Lin Fu, Huazhu Fu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32094

Spatial reasoning in hospitals requires tracking look-alike equipment, interpreting patient-relative directions, and judging passage widths, yet existing benchmarks provide limited coverage of these abilities. We introduce Clinical3D-Bench, a simulated benchmark with 3,368 questions of eight types on egocentric walkthroughs of 373 hospital-room layouts. Layouts follow hospital design guidelines, answers are computed from the 3D scenes, and paired scene states test whether models change their answers when relevant evidence changes. Videos are photorealistic, and ten physicians screened the layouts. Among 35 multimodal large language models (MLLMs), the best open and proprietary models reach 45.7% and 53.6% macro accuracy, against 40.8% for an answer prior and 79.0% for physicians on a subset, and 27 of 32 open models score below 40% on two-way patient-relative directions. Fine-tuning on Clinical3D-45K, our 44,750-question training set, lifts two MLLMs 16-20 points above the best proprietary model. 3D maps given to the fine-tuned MLLM as input go largely unused: replacing them with another video's, even when they are ground truth, barely changes its accuracy. The proposed GeoJeV instead lets the MLLM compute with 3D: it reconstructs a scene memory from each video, selects 3D operations and their arguments from the MLLM's question state, and adds their evidence to its option scores. It achieves the highest accuracy of all evaluated models, narrowing the gap to physicians.


### arXiv 2611.32093 - MARGIN: A Risk-Aware Agent for Robot Program Refinement

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T07:14:41Z
- authors: Hongbo Duan, Fan Du, Haipeng Luo, Bin Qian, Zichao Nie, Yilin Mi
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32093

Robot-programming agents improve executable control policies through repeated testing and repair, yet binary success saturates while execution risk persists across unseen scenes. We introduce MARGIN, a risk-aware agent that converts successful robot rollouts into actionable evidence for program refinement. Its near-miss margin measures the fraction of the execution budget remaining in the slowest successful development rollout. The agent uses this signal to prioritize fragile programs, guide targeted repairs, and select revisions that preserve development success while increasing execution headroom. An exact safe-racing rule accelerates success-based candidate evaluation. We evaluate MARGIN across LIBERO-Pro, LIBERO-90, and Robosuite. In our 80-task LIBERO-Pro reproduction, 78 programs attain perfect development success while accounting for substantial failures on unseen seeds. The near-miss margin predicts held-out failures with an AUC of 0.774, and low-margin programs exhibit 2.97 times higher failure risk. Across two independent evaluation rounds spanning 37 unique LIBERO-Pro tasks, MARGIN reduces executed control steps by 10.7% and 15.0%, with reductions of 18.0-20.0% on LIBERO-90 and Robosuite. Safe racing reduces fixed-sequence candidate-evaluation episodes by 53.5% while retaining identical selections, and paired execution probes improve acceptance decisions by identifying hidden regressions. Together, these results establish near-miss evidence as an effective basis for risk-aware, budget-efficient robot program refinement.


### arXiv 2611.32088 - From Memory Access to Evidence Sufficiency: Diagnosing and Addressing Demand--Evidence Misalignment

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T07:10:43Z
- authors: Shiyao Liu, Yixing Fan, Ruqing Zhang, Xiaohui Yan, Jiafeng Guo, Xueqi Cheng
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32088

Long-term memory retrieval often returns query-relevant records, but relevance does not guarantee sufficient evidence: answering requires a set of records that jointly satisfies a question's factual, relational, temporal, and state constraints. We study this demand--evidence misalignment through MemPath, an interventional framework that diagnoses failures in memory access, demand coverage, and evidence utilization. Guided by this diagnosis, we propose BiDE-Mem, which represents questions as typed demands and uses them to guide retrieval and evidence organization.
