We formalize demand--evidence support; in the evaluated compact path, it is conveyed through provenance-aware evidence organization and Reader guidance rather than a separate generative four-state verification step. On LoCoMo, BiDE-Mem improves accuracy by 6.23 percentage points over Mnemis; on LongMemEval, it achieves 91.80%, with strong multi-hop and temporal performance. Supplementary Qwen-Reader evaluations place it first among seven systems on LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S under two judging protocols. Ablations and evidence-level analyses show that demand representation, retrieval, and evidence presentation each contribute to answer quality.


### arXiv 2611.32085 - ExpGuard: Guarding Long-Horizon LLM Agents against Corrupted Experience

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T07:09:54Z
- authors: Yuzhou Chen, Licheng Pan, Zhixuan Chu, Yunsheng Lu, Yichen Sun, Han Zheng
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32085

Long-horizon large language model (LLM) agents distill past trajectories into reusable experiences. Misleading or adversarial guidance in source trajectories can corrupt these experiences, allowing harmful instructions to persist across tasks and influence future agent actions. Assessing the risks of potentially corrupted experiences poses three challenges: (1) modeling provenance and execution dependencies before experience reuse, (2) learning task-conditioned risk from trajectory outcomes, and (3) repairing risky experiences at online inference. To address these challenges, we present ExpGuard, a framework for retrieval-triggered experience risk assessment and online repair. Specifically, to tackle challenge (1), ExpGuard integrates experience semantics with provenance and execution dependencies in a task-conditioned heterogeneous graph to predict attack and task-failure risks before experience reuse. To enable task-conditioned risk supervision and tackle challenge (2), we conduct matched counterfactual replays of each original experience and its minimally neutralized counterpart from the same task and environment checkpoint, deriving separate risk labels from differences in attack success and legitimate task completion. To tackle challenge (3) at inference time, ExpGuard repairs candidate experiences flagged as high-risk by the graph-based predictor at retrieval and re-predicts their risks before they enter the agent's active experience context. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of ExpGuard in identifying risky experiences and defending agents against experience corruption.


### arXiv 2611.32084 - The algorithmic behavioral sink: Modeling the transition to irreversible demographic collapse

- categories: physics.soc-ph, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T07:08:31Z
- authors: Feng Yu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32084

In 2025, the vast majority of major global economies reported a Total Fertility Rate below the replacement level of 2.1. This synchronized collapse has persisted despite extensive pro-natalist financial interventions, revealing a structural limitation in conventional linear demographic models. This paper proposes that the ubiquity of mobile internet and Artificial Intelligence acts as a synthetic mating disruptor, precipitating a non-linear phase transition in human pair-bonding dynamics. Treating the marriage market as a complex system governed by thermodynamic constraints, we present a Fermi-Dirac selection model, using national income distributions as the input spectrum and fitted with Japanese longitudinal data to illustrate how digital hyper-reality generates an increasingly prohibitive Marriage Energy Barrier. Our model quantifies a Tech Factor that decouples female mating expectations from local economic realities during initial courtship, a distortion we predict will be severely exacerbated as AI evolves into an emotional substitute. Projections indicate that this substitution effect is accelerating; without intervention, the world post 2040 risks entering an irreversible demographic contraction, as the sudden surge in TFR required for recovery is biologically impossible. Ultimately, this framework explains why traditional financial subsidies fail to reverse falling birth rates and suggests that digital convenience may represent a behavioral Great Filter that erodes the imperative for species persistence.


### arXiv 2611.32063 - Integral DeltaNet: Improved Linear Attention with Adaptive Memory Integration

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T06:50:52Z
- authors: Junsoo Oh, Jihun Yun, Junhyuck Kim, Hanbaek Lyu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32063

Linear attention replaces softmax attention with a linear formulation, enabling linear-time computation with a fixed-size recurrent state instead of a growing key-value cache. These computational and memory advantages have attracted growing interest in linear attention for long-context language modeling. However, compressing past information into a fixed-size state causes memory collisions that hinder long-context understanding. Although DeltaNet mitigates memory collisions through delta-rule updates and Gated DeltaNet extends this approach with gating, these mechanisms may also discard information relevant to future predictions. To address this limitation, we introduce Integral DeltaNet, which maintains two complementary recurrent states: a fast-updating delta state that captures current key-value associations and an integral state that preserves a weighted history of the delta state. At readout, the model adaptively combines these states to access information across different timescales. We further derive a chunkwise parallel formulation that enables hardware-efficient training. We evaluate Integral DeltaNet using 340M and 1.3B parameter models trained from scratch on 15B and 100B FineWeb-Edu tokens, respectively. Comparisons with strong recurrent baselines show improved overall performance in language modeling, commonsense reasoning, real-world and synthetic retrieval, long-context understanding, and length extrapolation. We also compare training throughput and activation memory, as well as decoding latency and memory.


### arXiv 2611.32055 - More Powerful, Less Verifiable? Mitigating Hard-to-Verify Hallucinations in MLLM's Reinforcement Learning

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T06:42:45Z
- authors: Jianhong Pang, Ziyi Ye, Qiuxia Hou, Yanhao Zhang, Zuxuan Wu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32055

Reinforcement learning (RL) strengthens model capabilities through feedback from humans and environments. However, emerging evidence suggests that reward optimization can produce outputs whose errors are harder for humans to detect. To address this issue, we construct ITHV (the Image-Text Hallucination Verifiability Dataset), comprising 2,728 visually grounded instances that distinguish between obvious hallucinations, which humans readily detect, and elusive hallucinations, which humans struggle to identify. We then propose DAPO-V, an extension of DAPO that maximizes correctness subject to a budget on verifiability-aware risk. Specifically, DAPO-V combines a verifiability-aware reward with adaptive dual control, where larger penalties are assigned to incorrect responses on questions with lower human verification accuracy. An adaptive multiplier further adjusts the penalty strength according to risk-budget violations. Across three MLLM base models, DAPO-V improves overall accuracy while substantially improving accuracy on elusive hallucination questions by 5.78--9.72 percentage points over accuracy-only DAPO. We further test DAPO-V with a blinded human study, which shows that hallucinations are more readily detected in DAPO-V than those of the base model.


### arXiv 2611.32053 - EffectWAM: Learning Action-Grounded Effects for Robot Manipulation

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T06:36:34Z
- authors: Yuan Zhang, Hao Cui, Jingfei Ni, Meiyi Wang, Xin Nie, Chi Liu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32053

World-action models connect visual prediction with executable robot control. We present EffectWAM, which develops this connection through three complementary components: Effect, camera positional encoding (CaPE), and Action Grounding. Effect represents visual change as the difference between independently encoded future observations and a shared current-observation anchor. CaPE conditions endpoint pooling on relative camera pose when reliable extrinsics are available, while sharing the same learned parameters with pose-free pooling for uncalibrated trajectories. Action Grounding trains the visual representation to retain measured changes in end-effector pose and gripper state. An exponential moving average of the observation encoder supplies Effect targets. A shared generation expert learns to jointly predict Effect and an executable action chunk through flow matching, and reuses the sampled pair to generate future video on demand. This allows the controller to execute actions without waiting for video synthesis. We train on more than 20,000 hours of heterogeneous embodied data, excluding unused chain-of-thought data, and accommodate dataset-specific joint, end-effector, and hand-pose representations. Our evaluation uses the Small (8B) configuration on all five benchmarks and additionally evaluates the Large (26B) configuration on RoboDojo. Experiments examine closed-loop manipulation across complementary benchmarks, and comparisons across multiple baseline policies show consistent improvements from Action Grounding.


### arXiv 2611.32046 - From Rule-based to Agentic: LLM Agent based Real-time Control of Urban Drainage Systems for Flooding and Sewer Overflow Mitigation

- categories: cs.AI, eess.SY
- published: 2026-11-24T06:31:14Z
- authors: Wenchong Tian, Shixun Li, Ruozhou Lin, Peng Ye, Zhiyu Zhang, Shi Tao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32046

Real-time control (RTC) for urban drainage systems (UDS) remains hampered by sparse in-network sensing. While heterogeneous multi-modal meteorological early warnings often contain decision-critical information for UDS RTC, none of the existing approaches can exploit such externally-sourced information within the control workflow. Here we introduce AgenticAI-Control, an agentic artificial intelligence framework able to assimilate multi-source meteorological early warnings in RTC. Two coupled large language model agents are integrated to generate control strategies by retrieving meteorological early-warnings and fusing them with domain engineering expertise. Validated via simulation studies of a combined sewer catchment in eastern China across 10 real-world rainfall events, the AgenticAI-Control achieved 69.1-90.6% reduction in aggregated flood and overflow volumes compared to the rule-based baseline control. Rigorously standardized prompt engineering and modular workflow constraints substantially compress performance disparities among four distinct LLMs. Our work establishes AgenticAI as a viable tool to unify multi-source meteorological monitoring and practitioner domain knowledge, surmounting longstanding monitoring limitations of UDS RTC and delivering cost-efficient, robust intelligent control solutions for sensor-sparse sewer infrastructures.


### arXiv 2611.32041 - Spec-CoFrGeNet: Reparametrizing Continued Fraction Networks for Stable and Accurate Training

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T06:25:33Z
- authors: Soumyadeep Pal, Amit Dhurandhar, Vijil Chenthamarakshan, Dennis Wei, Karthikeyan Natesan Ramamurthy
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32041

Continued fractions have been shown to be a function class capable of constructing expressive yet parameter-efficient neural networks. These neural networks are not only effective for supervised learning, but also for training LLMs. However, the rational form of this function class introduces poles when the denominator approaches zero, which prior work has avoided by enforcing a small positive (non-smooth) lower bound. We show that performance and training stability can be highly sensitive to the choice of this lower bound. In this work, we introduce a novel transformation of the partial denominators, inspired by its connection to the Green's function in physics, that mitigates the above issue elegantly by constraining the spectra of the constructed tridiagonal Hamiltonian. Our approach further eliminates the need for incremental training used by previous works for stability, thus avoiding involved modifications to standard pre-training procedures. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our reparameterization by replacing FFNs with a previously proposed continued fraction architecture for both language and image generative modeling. Across diverse models, such as GPT-2 XL, (nano) Llama-MoE, and Granite, we show that our proposed method achieves performance comparable to the original (FFN-based) architectures while maintaining ~30%-50% parameter savings as before, and in most cases even outperforms the more complicated incremental training baseline. We further demonstrate, for the first time for continued fraction networks, that their effectiveness also carries over to image generative modeling, that utilize diffusion objectives. On a state-of-the-art Diffusion Transformer, we show that our approach again achieves performance comparable to the original FFN-based models with ~30% parameter savings and significantly outperforms the previous incremental training approach.


### arXiv 2611.32032 - Provenance-Isolated Epistemic Quorums: Execution-Bound Evidence Certification and Plausible-World Safety Gating in Agentic Control Planes

- categories: cs.DC, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-24T06:13:55Z
- authors: Jun He, Deying Yu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32032

Agreement among large language models is not physical evidence for mutating production infrastructure. Because generative models share pretraining priors and conform to peer rationales during deliberation, voting ensembles correlate errors instead of providing independent fault tolerance; yet attaching rigid deterministic checks to unconstrained agent outputs paralyzes incident remediation. We present Provenance-Isolated Epistemic Quorums (PIEQ), an execution-bound certification layer that keeps every language model strictly outside the trusted computing base. PIEQ unifies assumption-based truth maintenance, physical provenance isolation, control-theoretic plausible-world safety shielding, and linearizable concurrency governance around three distinct obligations: certifying diagnostic preconditions from disjoint physical failure domains, gating candidate mutations across all unrefuted fault worlds while falling back to read-only diagnostic probes under ambiguity, and binding admission certificates to target execution through snapshot barriers, revision checks, authorization freshness, trusted temporal-precondition policies, and capability-tiered enforcement. Across formal guarantees, automated etcd fault suites, frozen software benchmarks, and live language-model runs, PIEQ achieves zero within-assumption unsafe admissions and zero false-precondition commits, converting occasional agent proof omissions into recoverable false blocks rather than unsafe actions. On a physical PostgreSQL/Patroni cluster, PIEQ gates supervised failovers alongside Patroni and etcd's native split-brain protection and enforces a full Tier E2 contract inside a single serializable PostgreSQL transaction for bounded capacity reservations. All benchmarks are author-labeled, live model snapshots are unpinned, and Tier E2 physical failover promotion as well as multi-host hardware fault isolation remain open work.


### arXiv 2611.32031 - Unifying Partner and Environment Diversity to Improve Human-AI Coordination

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-24T06:10:40Z
- authors: Patrick Q. Zhang, Yancheng Liang, Adrienne Fairhall, Natasha Jaques
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32031

Real-world applications of modern AI systems require generalization to both unseen environments and novel human users. The field of zero-shot coordination (ZSC) has focused primarily on deploying AI to cooperate with novel partners in otherwise familiar environments. Popular population-based methods achieve this by partnering the agent with a diversity of simulated human users during training in a fixed environment, but does not consider the more realistic scenario where both the users and the environment are unseen during training. Recently, training across environments has been explored in training a single self-play agent, which shows better generalization on both novel human users and unseen environments. In this work, we ask whether jointly modeling partner diversity and environment diversity yields stronger coordination with novel partners in novel environments. We introduce Cross Environment Co-Play (CECP), a two-stage framework that first trains a population of self-play agents across a distribution of procedurally generated environments, and then trains a cooperator policy against this partner population. To further couple social and environmental reasoning, we add a next-state prediction module that encourages representations to encode both partner behavior and task structure. We evaluate CECP on both a neuroscience-inspired cooperative foraging task, and the popular ZSC benchmark Overcooked, under held-out partner and held-out environment conditions. CECP achieves the strongest performance among the compared baselines and attains state-of-the-art results under our evaluation protocol for human-AI collaboration. We analyze the model's latent representation encoded by the next-state prediction module, showing it can not only improve the performance of the cooperative AI, but also provide more interpretable latents, demonstrated by the inference of partner skill levels.


### arXiv 2611.32029 - Do Occupancy World Models Really Follow Actions?

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T06:10:28Z
- authors: Jongseong Bae, Ha Young Kim
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32029

Occupancy world models predict future 3D scenes conditioned on ego actions, yet their predictions may not faithfully follow the specified trajectory under counterfactual actions. Because driving datasets provide sparse coverage of alternative trajectories for each scene, existing models learn the spatial effect of ego motion implicitly from observed scene--trajectory combinations, which may entangle action effects with scene--trajectory correlations. We propose ReCast, an ego-trajectory-guided transformer that grounds future prediction in action-induced geometric correspondence across ego-centric frames. ReCast decouples historical encoding from future decoding and aligns deformable interactions using relative ego transformations, while retaining flexibility for non-rigid scene evolution. We also introduce a counterfactual action-faithfulness benchmark covering in-distribution, tail, and out-of-distribution trajectories. On Occ3D-nuScenes, ReCast achieves competitive standard forecasting performance while improving counterfactual forecasting, with particularly clear gains under out-of-distribution trajectories.


### arXiv 2611.32022 - Beyond Before-and-After Images: Evaluating Temporal Grounding in a Lightweight VLM for Disaster Assessment

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T06:04:43Z
- authors: Shubh Garg, Pratham Arora, Debabrata Ghosh
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32022

Bi-temporal satellite imagery is central to disaster assessment: deciding what was damaged requires comparing a pre-event scene with its post-event counterpart. A vision-language model (VLM) can nonetheless appear to answer change questions while relying mainly on the post-event image or on the prompt's temporal framing. We present a controlled diagnostic of temporal grounding and apply it to one lightweight VLM, InternVL3-2B, on DisasterM3: 1,659 optical and SAR image pairs from 41 events and nine disaster types, in event-disjoint partitions, with 9,031 reasoning questions. We compare post-only input, the true pre/post pair, the post image repeated twice, reversed image order, and an unrelated pre-event image. The true pair scores $+0.5$ accuracy points above the repeated-post control (95\% paired bootstrap CI $[-0.2, +1.2]$), and reversing the order changes accuracy by $-0.2$ points ($[-0.8, +0.5]$). Under this protocol we therefore find no measurable benefit from the content of the true pre-event image and little sensitivity to temporal order. The model is not blind to the first image, however: the true pair scores $2.5$ points above a pair whose pre-event image comes from another event ($[+1.6, +3.4]$). State-transition and multi-hop questions remain hard, and optical and SAR inputs show different profiles. We outline temporal scene graphs as a testable next step, not a validated solution, and argue that temporal benchmarks for Earth observation should report whether answers depend on the intended evidence, not only how often they are right.


### arXiv 2611.32021 - HuluOPD: Unifying Heterogeneous Medical Specialists into One Lightweight Model with Unlabeled Prompts

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-24T06:02:54Z
- authors: Yuncheng Zhang, Songtao Jiang, Yitian Tang, Zijie Meng, Zihan Xu, Xuxiang Ren
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32021

Specialist medical vision-language models (VLMs) are strong within their specialties but hard to unify: their training data are often private, and independently developed specialists cannot be assumed to share an architecture. Deploying these large models separately is costly. We present HuluOPD, an on-policy distillation recipe that unifies heterogeneous specialists into one lightweight VLM using only unlabeled prompts, without requiring reference answers or the specialists' training data. The lightweight model answers each prompt, and the specialist of the prompt's modality supervises its response; a continuation-aligned cross-tokenizer loss makes this possible even when they differ in architecture and tokenizer. We apply HuluOPD to five specialists, for ultrasound, pathology, optical coherence tomography, fundus photography, and dental imaging. To distill and evaluate them, we build HuluOPDCorpus, 914k unlabeled prompts, and HuluOPDBench, 47k held-out test items, from over 90 public datasets, and score all models under one protocol on 132k test items, including third-party benchmarks. A single model with 10% of the five specialists' combined parameters retains 94% of their question-answering performance, and one with 2% retains 89%. To our knowledge, this is the first demonstration of unifying heterogeneous medical specialist VLMs without requiring their training data or a shared architecture.


### arXiv 2611.32018 - Structure-Aware Transformers for Hierarchical Authorization in Personal AI Agents

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, stat.ME
- published: 2026-11-24T06:00:21Z
- authors: Dongyu Cui, Jie Ding, Xuan Bi
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32018

Personal AI agents can carry out complex, multi-step tasks on users' behalf, but doing so often involves numerous permission requests. Manually reviewing each request is time-consuming, while indiscriminate approval poses privacy and security risks. Predicting users' authorization preferences offers a way to reduce this burden while preserving user control. However, authorization prediction is challenging because permission requests are often hierarchical and observed user decisions are sparse and potentially correlated. To address these challenges, We propose the Parent-gated Authorization Transformer for Hierarchies (PATH) to predict users' authorization preferences for personal AI agents. PATH is a novel neural network architecture that combines hierarchy-aware attention masks with a parent-gated probability formulation to ensure hierarchy-consistent predictions. Hierarchy-level embeddings further support learning from sparse observations and patterns shared within each authorization level. We establish finite-sample risk bounds and provide sufficient conditions for consistent authorization probability estimation and algorithmic convergence. Experiments on simulated data, AI-generated user profiles, and real user authorization data demonstrate strong predictive performance with zero hierarchy violations.


### arXiv 2611.33100 - CANON: Agent-Assisted Construction of Canonical Differential Equations for Feynman Integrals

- categories: hep-ph, cs.MA, hep-th
- published: 2026-11-24T18:35:05Z
- authors: Dong-shan Jian, Yuanche Liu, Yan-qing Ma, Bo-xuan Shi, Yu Wu, Zihao Wu
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33100

The construction of canonical basis is an important procedure in the calculation of multi-loop Feynman integrals via the canonical differential equation methods. Currently, there are various different methods for this purpose. Among them, no single method is universally applicable. Thus, obtaining a valid basis often requires repeated attempts and verification. In this paper, we present CANON, an agent-assisted framework designed to make this search-and-verification process more convenient and more efficient. In this workflow, the agent selects methods and calls the corresponding tools to revise the basis proposal or the associated differential-equation system. Independent programs then verify whether the agent's result is valid and ask the agent to retry if verification fails. This framework contains a specific state-management tool to supports long-range basis searching works which requires multiple sub-agents. Its modular architecture enables future extensions to the framework itself. In this paper, we demonstrate this framework by applying it to some two-loop and three-loop integral families.


### arXiv 2611.32966 - Multi-Agent Coordination Through Stigmergy for Canal Network Formation on Real Terrain

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-24T17:03:33Z
- authors: Jordan Kennedy, Federico Oliva, Amir Degani, Karen-Lee Bar Sinai, Nare Karapetyan
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32966

Terrain-modifying robot swarms operating in unstructured outdoor environments must coordinate under uncertain dynamics despite unreliable communication and incomplete global information. Stigmergy offers a decentralized alternative by allowing agents to coordinate indirectly through persistent environmental modifications, yet its application to large-scale terrain construction on real landscapes remains largely unexplored. Inspired by beaver canal construction, we present a minimal stigmergic framework for multi-agent systems in which decentralized agents follow local wetness cues, excavate terrain, and interact through a closed agent--environmental feedback loop. The framework couples agents with seasonal hydrology on a real digital elevation model (DEM). Across 110 simulations on an 862m x 1167m region spanning agent count and hydrological recession rate, the model produces emergent canal-like networks whose morphology varies predictably with environmental constraints. An alignment-invariant morphological evaluation against a field-annotated reference network identifies parameter regimes that reproduce key canal characteristics, while failure cases reveal over-canalization and fragmentation. These results demonstrate that simple local terrain-modifying behaviors can generate plausible landscape-scale structures through environmental feedback, providing a minimal framework for studying decentralized terrain construction and bio-inspired swarm coordination.


### arXiv 2611.32606 - Recursive Collective Intelligence: Emergence on an Equal-Budget Ladder in Multi-Agent LLM Systems

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-24T13:37:54Z
- authors: Lixuan He, Shengqu Cai, Fang Wu, Shikang Zheng, Liwei Jiang, Manling Li
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32606

Large language models increasingly work as teams of agents, and a team is expected to solve problems beyond the reach of any single member. Most evaluations credit a team for outperforming a single agent, without accounting for the additional computation: a team may consume many times the tokens of one agent, and a plain vote over independent answers already explains most of the reported gain. Two questions therefore remain open: When does interaction itself make a group smarter, and can a group improve its next generation by learning from its own work? We answer both with the equal-budget ladder, which grades a group against independent agents at three levels: outperforming a single agent, a compute-matched majority vote, and compute-matched pass@n. On this ladder we define Recursive Collective Intelligence (RCI) as a group whose own output, without a teacher or external labels, makes its next generation smarter at equal cost. Three findings follow. First, interaction improves collective intelligence when it moves information across agents: forwarding split facts lifts groups above pass@n at three model scales, by about a third at 27B. Second, organization emerges spontaneously: identical 27B agents form hubs and improve accuracy by 18 percentage points; encoding their organization in a prompt enables them to reach level 3 at less than half the token budget in a descriptive comparison. Third, groups recursively improve themselves: Two label-free generations progressively climb the ladder, the second 9B generation surpassing both pass@$n$ and a control trained on base-model turns; on PlanBench, HiddenBench and Hanabi, each generation outperforms its predecessor on unseen problems for four to six generations. A group therefore outperforms its members when it forwards useful information more efficiently than it spends tokens on coordination, and learns to do so from its own work.


### arXiv 2611.32255 - TimeTravelContainer: A Live Forking Sandbox for Stateful Agent Exploration

- categories: cs.OS, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-24T09:29:45Z
- authors: Zizheng Guo, Yanqing Zhang, Nathaniel Pinckney, Cunxi Yu, Brucek Khailany
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32255

LLM agents that drive stateful tools (e.g., interactive shells, design environments, simulation engines) frequently need to clone, backtrack, and parallelize live sessions to support exploration. However, cloning a live session today requires serializing the tool's in-memory state to disk or relaunching from scratch, both prohibitively slow for interactive exploration. Existing agent sandboxes based on microVMs or kernel patches either serialize full guest memory to storage or require privileged deployment, limiting use on shared compute farms, personal computers, and other restricted environments. We present TimeTravelContainer (TTC), a rootless container runtime that live-forks an entire running container, including processes, memory, filesystem, and network, with copy-on-write (CoW) semantics. Because the bulk of a tool session's memory is typically read-only after the fork point, clones share it automatically and only diverged pages consume additional memory. TTC requires no kernel modifications, hardware virtualization, or root privileges, and runs on all major platforms including Linux, macOS (Apple Silicon), and Windows (WSL 2), making it deployable as a portable foundation for agent harnesses. We evaluate TTC on two agentic electronic design automation (EDA) flows as representative stateful-tool workloads: MAGE (Innovus) and EvoDRC (KLayout). TTC achieves 84.1$\times$ environment cloning speedup, 31--72% memory reduction, and 1.7--9.4$\times$ end-to-end speedup, demonstrating that live forking removes a scaling barrier for agent-driven exploration of stateful tool environments.


### arXiv 2611.31876 - AgentDRC: Multi-Agent Algorithms for Spatially-Aware Automated DRC Correction

- categories: cs.AR, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-24T03:30:08Z
- authors: Pengju Liu, Nuo Xu, Winso Chen, Peijun Li,  Yang,  Zhao
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31876

Design Rule Checking (DRC) error fixing remains a time-consuming bottleneck in physical design, often requiring manual intervention despite advanced EDA optimizations. Existing LLM-agent approaches fall short due to redundant contextual shapes, limited spatial reasoning over raw coordinates, and inefficient flat layout representations. We propose AgentDRC, which formulates iterative DRC correction as a multi-agent algorithm and introduces a spatial-aware verification and correction algorithm that reconstructs structured spatial relations among critical shapes and validates fixes through geometric and semantic constraint checking. The system coordinates four specialized agents to perceive multi-level, multi-modal geometric features, filter irrelevant shapes, and iteratively correct violations. Experiments show that AgentDRC achieves up to 30.4% pass@1 and 55.7% pass@5 on challenging violations, with 13.8% pass@5 improvement over the best baseline. We will open-source all code and benchmark tasks upon acceptance.


### arXiv 2611.31821 - Exploring Multi-Player Social Games as Verifiable Training Grounds for Large Language Models

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-24T02:15:30Z
- authors: Keyu He, Michael Li, Jiarui Liu, Mingqian Zheng, Koichi Onoue, Tongshuang Wu
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31821

The training of socially intelligent language models often relies on large language model (LLM) judges, which are expensive, noisy, and vulnerable to reward hacking. Multi-player social games offer a natural alternative: they require open-ended communication to coordinate with teammates and infer hidden roles, while their rules provide an objective outcome which can serve as a verifiable reward for training. Using three social games, we explore how much social intelligence Qwen3 models can acquire through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). Results show that against a much stronger Gemma-3-27B model, SFT and RLVR raise the win rate of Qwen3-4B from 33% to 81% on the three trained games, and from 39% to 54% on three additional held-out games. In contrast, in-context strategies such as prompting improve the win rate of Qwen3-4B by only about 6 pp across the same games we train and evaluate on. Furthermore, we show that the gains from SFT and RLVR hold across the model sizes 1.7B, 4B, and 32B. Qualitative analyses of agent behavior show signs of stronger social intelligence; for example, trained agents tend to share information more openly, check each other's claims against public evidence, and coordinate their votes. More broadly, our results demonstrate that training LLMs on verifiable outcomes in social games can cultivate generalizable social behaviors, which offers a scalable path for building socially intelligent agents.


### arXiv 2611.31573 - The Paradox of Agency-Preserving AI: Negotiating Learner Agency in Layered Human-AI Interactions

- categories: cs.HC, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-23T21:15:09Z
- authors: Yueling Fan, Jacqueline Wong, Olov Engwall, Olga Viberg
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31573

Generative AI is increasingly used by learners in cognitively demanding tasks, but its capacity to provide immediate solutions raises concerns about decreased learner agency. Meta-level AI systems that provide reflective and metacognitive scaffolding have emerged as a promising approach to supporting learners' self-regulation. However, these systems introduce a fundamental paradox: AI intended to preserve learner agency can itself constrain that agency by regulating when, how, and why learners should reflect. To investigate this challenge, we conducted a co-design workshop with 18 students engaged in programming and academic writing tasks using ChatGPT extended with ChatWise, a meta-level AI system providing self-regulated learning-aligned feedback. Drawing on 185 prompt-feedback entries, 174 written reflections, and participants' design artifacts (17 storyboards and 5 group design proposals), we identified four interconnected design tensions: assistance vs reflection, immediate intervention vs space for self-regulation, visibility vs attention competition, and system adaptation vs user control. The study contributes a multidimensional account of learner agency that includes task, cognitive, and interaction agency, along with empirically grounded interaction-design mechanisms to negotiate these tensions.


### arXiv 2611.31568 - Heterogeneous Risk-Sensitive Evolutionary Learning in Coordination Games: Polarization, Welfare Loss, and Intervention for Efficiency

- categories: eess.SY, cs.GT, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-23T21:12:04Z
- authors: Solaleh Mohammadi, Yiman Fong, Kaiqing Zhang
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31568

We study evolutionary learning in coordination games in a finite population of agents with heterogeneous risk attitudes, modeled using the entropic risk measure (ERM). The population consists of two subpopulations, each with a fixed risk attitude. Under risk-sensitive best response with mutations (BRM), we characterize the long-run outcomes of the learning process. In particular, we characterize when a polarized state, in which the two subpopulations choose different actions, is uniquely stochastically stable in terms of agents' risk attitudes and the subpopulation sizes. Notably, this polarized state is strictly Pareto dominated by the payoff-dominant equilibrium under both expected payoffs and agents' own ERM-adjusted payoffs. Moreover, its welfare, measured by average expected payoff, is always lower than that of the payoff-dominant equilibrium and can also be lower than that of the risk-dominant equilibrium. Thus, heterogeneity of risk attitudes in learning alone can lead to an inefficient long-run outcome. To mitigate such inefficiency, we propose intervention mechanisms to modify agents' risk attitudes in learning. We show that a sufficiently large intervention share (the fraction of agents modified) and sufficiently high intervention intensity (the increase in their risk parameter) ensure that the payoff-dominant equilibrium is uniquely stochastically stable. However, greater intervention intensity cannot compensate for an insufficient intervention share, and a larger intervention share cannot compensate for insufficient intervention intensity.


### arXiv 2611.31561 - Sampling at Scale: Distributed Multi-Agent Trajectory Optimization

- categories: cs.RO, cs.MA, eess.SY, math.OC
- published: 2026-11-23T21:07:52Z
- authors: Minchan Jung, Dimitrios S. Georgiou, Augustinos D. Saravanos, Evangelos A. Theodorou
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31561

Multi-agent trajectory optimization poses two coupled challenges: effectively exploring non-convex landscapes and scaling favorably to large teams of agents. Sampling-based methods are natural candidates for the exploration challenge but scale poorly with problem dimension. Moreover, their off-the-shelf integration into distributed optimization introduces a fundamental trade-off: sampling either fails to address the constraint-induced non-convexity or its search space grows with neighborhood size. We address this gap by introducing a $\underline{D}\text{istributed}$ $\underline{S}\text{ampling-based}$ $\underline{T}\text{rajectory}$ $\underline{O}\text{ptimization} \ \text{(DS-TO)}$ architecture built on a splitting mechanism tailored to sampling. Under this splitting, each agent samples only in its own control space, independent of neighborhood size, while still remaining exposed to both the constraint-induced non-convexity and the decisions of other agents. The resulting algorithm is fully decentralized, requiring only neighborhood communication, with all local updates executed in parallel across agents. Efficient variations, including parallel Jacobi updates within each agent's subproblem and single-step constraint linearization, further reduce computation. Experiments on 2D and 3D tasks with up to 1024 agents demonstrate improved exploration and constraint satisfaction over state-of-the-art derivative-based distributed optimization, and superior scalability to existing distributed sampling methods.


### arXiv 2611.31543 - Reproducible Social Structure Emerges From Free-Form Interaction Among LLM Agents

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-23T20:51:57Z
- authors: Tom Alisch, Sonia Murthy, Robert Hawkins, Felix Sosa, Core Francisco Park
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31543

Multi-agent LLM simulations are increasingly used as models of social systems, but most published findings are anecdotal, i.e., drawn from a single run. It is therefore unclear which observed phenomena are robust findings of the simulated system itself, and which were random events in one particular run. Here, we introduce a repeatable, goal-free, proactive multi-agent framework in which fifty persona-conditioned agents with persistent memory choose whether, with whom, and how to communicate across three social platforms. The framework supports non-perturbative measurements across temporal snapshots and repetitions. Running the same world ten times from identical initial conditions for a total of 700 hours, we find that most measured network- and agent-level parameters are stable across repetitions. Throughout a run, initially broad communication contracts into sparse sustained dyads in all ten worlds. Importantly, we find that many identical dyads re-form across worlds significantly more often than expected by chance or from agent-specific sociability alone. Static personality similarity largely fails to predict these dyads. Instead, alignment and extremity in the emotions and traits expressed during first contact predict whether a pair later forms a sustained dyad. Additionally, individual agents' emotion and trait expression in their chain-of-thought predicts their future behavior. Finally, swapping either persona prompts or accumulated memories between agents causes recipients to exhibit behavior more similar to donors. Both kinds of transplant move the recipients about 60% of the way toward its donor personality, and state-transplanted agents start behaving similar to their donor agent from their next turn on. Overall, we show that repeated worlds enable robust, statistically-grounded conclusions about emergent within-configuration behavior in agent societies.


### arXiv 2611.31499 - Measuring Counterfactual Credit in a Replayable Wildfire-Suppression Benchmark

- categories: cs.MA, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-23T20:09:38Z
- authors: Alireza Moghaddasi
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31499

In cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning, learned critics are usually evaluated by how well they predict the team's return. Yet predicting a team's return and assigning credit to individual actions are distinct tasks. To test credit directly, we introduce FLARE (Fireline Learning Agents in a Replayable Environment), a stochastic wildfire suppression benchmark in which any moment of a fire can be replayed with different actions under the same random events. Replaying one step measures the immediate effect of each agent's suppression, which we call simulator credit and use for training. Replaying to the end of the episode measures the effect of each action, which we use for grading critics. In a comparison over eight seeds with multi-agent proximal policy optimization (MAPPO), simulator credit reduces the interquartile mean burn score (each cell's burning time, capped and averaged over the map; lower is better) from 26.6% with shared reward to 20.9%. Learned credit from a centralized COMA-style critic does no better than the shared reward. Four trained critics predict the team's return well, yet the credit they assign is 9 to 42 times larger than the credit measured by replay. The critics also give heterogeneous agents nearly identical action rankings. These results show that a good fit to the team's return is a weak test of a critic's credit.


### arXiv 2611.31205 - ACAP: Asynchronous Coding Agents for Efficient Robot Control

- categories: cs.RO, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-23T18:52:19Z
- authors: Zexin He, Rui Li, Yushi Lan, Chengkai Jin, Zhi Hou, Xingang Pan
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31205

Coding agents can construct and revise robot behavior during execution, but using one agent for the whole robot can delay otherwise independent work. We introduce Asynchronous Coding Agents as Policy (ACAP), an asynchronous framework that organizes one robot as a team of persistent agents. Each member reasons and acts for a configurable control group, such as an arm with its gripper or the torso and chassis, enabling asynchronous reasoning and execution. However, different robot components often have physical dependencies that require coordination. To address this challenge, ACAP introduces a hyper-agent that assigns tasks, coordinates dependencies, and dynamically allocates control between the lead and member agents. This allows independent work to progress concurrently while supporting tightly coupled manipulation when necessary. Across 72 real-world rollouts covering 25 task/platform settings, ACAP reduces task-balanced time cost by 28.2% compared with a single-agent Direct Policy, with improvements across Fully Parallel, Parallel-Sequential, and Collaborative tasks. Across all eight Collaborative settings, ACAP also achieves lower mean time cost than Fixed Delegation, where only member agents can control their assigned groups. An additional 90-rollout study shows lower mean 30-minute-capped time cost than Direct on all six tasks. We further demonstrate mobile manipulation using the same member interface for chassis and torso control. We present the framework and a real-world rollout dataset containing observations, generated programs, actions, and feedback to support research on how coding agents reason and act in the physical world.


### arXiv 2611.31063 - From Local Errors to System Failures: Modeling and Benchmarking Error Propagation in Multi-Agent LLM Systems

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-23T17:20:43Z
- authors: Wenlong Yu, Bingxu Lu, Qilong Wang, Qinghua Hu, Qianru Sun
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31063

In multi-agent systems (MAS) built on large language models (LLMs), errors can propagate through intermediate messages and compromise final answers. However, outcome-based evaluation obscures how agents amplify, attenuate, or correct these errors. We propose Response-Aware Error Propagation among Agents (REPA), a structural model that connects measured local responses to system-level error propagation. REPA separately estimates input assembly and agent processing across multiple error channels, while theoretical analysis relates local modeling errors to endpoint prediction error. Experiments span three Qwen base models, six MAS workflows, and ten knowledge and reasoning settings in ErrPropBench-10. REPA achieves a mean condition-level Spearman correlation of 0.84 across base models, compared with 0.47 for direct regression. Controlled experiments also demonstrate its utility for targeted recovery. REPA-guided retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) improves accuracy over no retrieval by an average of 19 percentage points with 16% additional tokens. By linking local agent behavior to system susceptibility, REPA provides an interpretable foundation for studying error propagation and developing targeted optimization in MAS.


### arXiv 2611.30950 - Vetoes and missed agreements in language-model bargaining

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-23T16:27:35Z
- authors: Fuad Ali
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30950

A language model played three representatives, each instructed to get the best payoff for its own group. One representative requested a plan, another chose the sole offer, and all three voted. If anyone voted no, each group received its fallback payoff. In 60 games with Qwen3-Next-80B, the representative at risk of a loss always requested a plan that would leave everyone better off. In each game, two copies of the proposer saw the same payoffs, peer request and reminder that the offer needed three yes votes. Showing the proposer how many groups gained under each plan raised mutually beneficial agreements from 6/60 to 31/60 (paired increase 41.7 percentage points, conservative 95% interval 18.8 to 58.5). Two of the four plans benefited everyone, so picking uniformly would put a mutual gain on the ballot half the time on average. In an earlier study, GPT-4.1 played 12 conflict games under two message conditions. Majority rule passed 15 of 24 offers, all at one group's expense. Unanimity passed two, both mutual gains. In the Qwen games, voters never saw the counts. They approved all 37 offers that helped everyone. On all 83 offers that would have cost one group points, its representative voted no.


### arXiv 2611.30874 - Bridging Continuous-Time MAPF and Industrial AGV Fleet Coordination with Lazy Priority-Based Search

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-23T15:43:31Z
- authors: Federico Signorile, Raffaele Carli, Alessandro Bertogli, Roberto Olmi, Sven Koenig, Mariagrazia Dotoli
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30874

Continuous-time multi-agent path finding on roadmaps (MAPF$_R$) offers a principled way to coordinate robot fleets with geometric collision avoidance, yet its deployment on industrial automated guided vehicle (AGV) infrastructures remains challenging, as highly connected and redundant roadmaps amplify both safe-interval reasoning and multi-agent interaction handling. To bridge this gap, we formalize AGV-MAPF$_R$-CTC, a configuration-space variant of MAPF$_R$ with precomputed continuous-time conflicts that enables the reuse of MAPF$_R$ solvers for AGV fleet coordination with minimal changes. We then revisit continuous-time priority-based search (CPBS), identifying the boundary conditions required by its safe interval path planning (SIPP) low-level planner under priority-induced constraints and enabling earlier solution detection. As a central contribution, we analyze the structure of the priority search space, clarifying which agents are affected by each priority refinement and when replanning can be avoided. Building on this analysis, we introduce a novel algorithm, Lazy-CPBS, which materializes high-level nodes lazily: a child's plans are computed only when it is expanded, so siblings that are never expanded cost no low-level search. Experiments on standard MAPF benchmarks and on industrial AGV roadmaps imported from a proprietary digital-twin simulator show that CPBS-based solvers remain effective well beyond the agent counts reachable by optimal MAPF$_R$ solvers, while Lazy-CPBS substantially reduces CPBS overhead and offers the most consistent robustness/runtime trade-off across industrial layouts.


### arXiv 2611.30788 - From joint attention to shared symbols: A free-energy principle account

- categories: cs.MA, hep-th, q-bio.NC
- published: 2026-11-23T14:50:11Z
- authors: Tadahiro Taniguchi, Koji Hashimoto, Ryuichiro Ishikawa
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30788

Joint attention precedes vocabulary acquisition. How can attending to the same object help two people with different sensory experiences come to use a shared word? In a free-energy principle account, marginalizing the common object induces a statistical dependence between private perceptual states. In a categorical world, the joint prior inherits a mixture structure whose component index provides a candidate for shared reference. The collective predictive coding model approximates this prior by representing the dependence through that shared index. An established Metropolis-Hastings naming game lets agents infer and exchange words using private evidence. Toy and multiview image experiments illustrate how word exchange can coordinate private categories and improve the fit of collective models. The account connects joint attention to shared symbols through the coupling of private inference.


### arXiv 2611.30621 - AgentBouncer: Defending Software Engineering Agents via Dynamic System-Call Mediation

- categories: cs.CR, cs.MA, cs.SE
- published: 2026-11-23T12:54:45Z
- authors: Pascal Joos, Michael Pradel
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30621

Software engineering (SE) agents can complete diverse engineering tasks by interacting with the development environment via tools that access files, run commands, and download resources. Unfortunately, the same ability that makes SE agents useful also makes them prone to performing unintended malicious actions through prompt injections, malicious tools, or supply-chain attacks. Existing defenses that analyze the agent trajectory may fail to detect such attacks, as they inspect only the initial prompt and the sequence of tool calls, but not the actual actions that are executed in the environment. This paper presents AgentBouncer, a novel runtime enforcement system that prevents SE agents from performing malicious actions by mediating, and if necessary denying, system calls that agents trigger via tool calls. The core of AgentBouncer is an LLM arbiter that judges system calls along two axes, necessity and safety for the current task. It denies unsafe system calls, and the less necessary a system call appears, the more certain the arbiter has to be about its safety to allow it. To manage runtime overhead, preseeded policy rules and hierarchy-aware caching ensure that the LLM arbiter is only invoked for key decisions. We evaluate AgentBouncer on a dataset of 340 attacks on the development environment, covering four attack vectors, combined with benign issue resolution tasks from SWE-bench Verified. AgentBouncer reduces the attack success rate from 99.41% for an unsupervised agent to 10.59%, which is 18.23-73.53 percentage points lower than competitive state-of-the-art defenses. At the same time, AgentBouncer preserves the task success rate of the agent, with only 0.25% failed benign tool calls. Per benign task, AgentBouncer adds 47 seconds and USD 0.0036 in LLM costs to an unsupervised agent, whereas the closest existing runtime defense adds 4.5x as much time and 9.6x as much cost.


### arXiv 2611.30185 - Causal Evidence for the Emergence and Spread of Reward Hacking in AI Agent Swarms

- categories: cs.HC, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-23T07:30:56Z
- authors: Sumer S. Vaid
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30185

Autonomous AI agents that coordinate and collaborate among each other have become pervasive across domains ranging from scientific research to finance and engineering. The opportunity to share messages and build upon each other's work also creates the potential for misaligned behaviors to spread across AI agents. We provide causal evidence that mere exposure to reward hacking, cheating on a task to pass an automated check, is enough to spread the behavior among AI agents, even after the cheat has been rejected. We conducted 17 preregistered experiments with six frontier models (n = 20,111 agents). First, we show that cheating emerges spontaneously. When agents were told that work passing the visible tests is accepted, 33% cheated in the first round, when the shared record of teammates' submissions was empty. Second, we show that agents who rarely cheat when working alone cheat most of the time after seeing a teammate's cheat, as 94% of teammates who saw an accepted cheat cheated. Third, we show that rejection of reward-hacked solutions offers little protection, using experiments that changed only the label on the cheat. Most agents copied a cheat labeled rejected, 34% of agents cheated after an AI reviewer had rejected the cheat, and nearly every agent copied a cheat labeled accepted. Fourth, we show that cheating generalizes to new tasks. On a new problem with an empty collaboration record, 70% of agents who had earlier seen an accepted cheat cheated again, compared with 32% of agents who had seen an empty record. Fifth, we show that cheating spreads from neighbor to neighbor through networks of 256 agents. In the networks, 44% of agents with a cheating neighbor cheated, compared with 2% of other agents. Together, the results suggest that mere exposure can spread misaligned behavior among AI agents, and that catching a cheat is insufficient once other agents have seen the cheat.


### arXiv 2611.30035 - TRAE: Trustworthy Automated ESG Annotation via Multi-Agent Consensus and Confidence Assessment

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-23T04:44:25Z
- authors: Jaeyoung Lee, Misuk Kim
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30035

With the growing demand for ESG disclosure and evaluation, constructing high-quality datasets for automatically extracting ESG-related information from large-scale documents has become essential. However, ESG annotation remains challenging due to strong context dependence, ambiguous label boundaries, and the need for domain expertise, resulting in high annotation cost and limited scalability. To address these challenges, we propose TRAE (TRustworthy Automated ESG annotation), a multi-agent framework for scalable and reliable ESG data construction. Unlike direct LLM-based annotation, TRAE evaluates annotation reliability by modeling agreement, confidence, and reasoning consistency among multiple agents through a structured multi-step reasoning process. Experimental results demonstrate that TRAE improves annotation performance compared with existing automated annotation strategies. Furthermore, TRAE supports a hybrid human-AI annotation strategy that assigns expert labeling only to uncertain samples, reducing annotation cost while preserving data quality. Using TRAE, we construct a high-quality ESG dataset containing approximately 20,000 instances with complete reasoning traces, intermediate agent outputs, and reliability information to enhance transparency and reproducibility. Overall, this work provides a practical and scalable framework for trustworthy ESG data construction. The code and data are publicly available at: https://github.com/nowzer0/TRAE.


### arXiv 2611.29811 - Notes on Generative AI for Mathematical Research

- categories: math.HO, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-22T22:35:19Z
- authors: Shurui Liu
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29811

These notes describe how generative AI is now used to solve and to check research-level mathematics, with a focus on agents and their harnesses. For the proving agents Rethlas, Danus, and Pharos and the verification agent OSVerify, we explain the design principles, compare each with its base model, present case studies, and show how to use and contribute to them. We also briefly review the history of AI for mathematics and the community's reactions to it.


### arXiv 2611.31312 - Evolutionary AI Control: Population-Level Safety Under Reproduction, Heredity, and Selection

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-22T19:17:03Z
- authors: Andrew T. Cadotte
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31312

An AI agent can satisfy every individual safety check and still give rise to a population that violates a collective safety limit. This risk arises in open multi-agent systems, where agents copy themselves, modify their successors, and change system membership during a run. Selection then shifts the population toward traits outside the developers' optimization targets. We prove conditions under which safety guarantees persist through reproduction, inheritance, and selection, and we characterize how they fail. Four counterexamples illustrate these failures. Unauthorized copies, which violate a specified reproductive restriction, can each leave fewer than one successor on average while their expected number grows without bound under continued production from an expanding authorized population. Two lineages can each decline in isolation yet grow exponentially when they interact. Alternating between reproductive rules can drive growth even when each rule, applied alone, suppresses it. Selection can raise the population's average rate of unauthorized reproduction above a certified limit even though every agent's rate remains fixed. Whereas existing verification methods assume fixed or registered membership, we prove safety certificates, under stated assumptions, for populations whose agents create successors during a run. The main result bounds the expected cumulative harm over a run, including when a scheduler adapts the reproductive rules to the observed history. Further conditions cover every population state reachable through evolution and preserve reproductive restrictions as descendants inherit modifications. Drawing on the mechanisms by which multicellular organisms control cell reproduction, the framework enables developers to specify and verify these controls before deployment.


### arXiv 2611.29598 - AEP:Towards Trustworthy Agent Communication, Networking, and Collaboration

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-22T16:36:30Z
- authors: Yuhao Wang, Shengfang Zhai, Xinyu Zhou, Guanghao Jin, Wenjie Qu, Huichi Zhou
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29598

As privately owned agents become increasingly capable, collaboration in open agent networks is limited by partial topology knowledge, ownership boundaries, and the risk of exposing or contaminating private context. Existing agent-to-agent protocols are useful when collaborators are already known, while shared-memory and broadcast-based systems rely on visibility and trust assumptions that do not hold in decentralized, low-trust environments. We introduce Open Private Agent Collaboration (OPAC), an asset-centric paradigm for cooperation among private agents with open membership, partial observability, and ownership-aware access control. Rather than treating collaboration as the exchange of final responses, OPAC enables agents to selectively discover, exchange, audit, and integrate policy-controlled context assets. This shifts the unit of cooperation from transient messages to reusable external context, while keeping disclosure and integration subject to privacy and security constraints. We instantiate OPAC with the Agent Exchange Protocol (AEP), a decentralized framework that combines semantic discovery, demand-driven coordination, safety-gated asset exchange, and ledger-backed evidence derivation. By distinguishing physical topology from each agent's local cognitive topology, AEP allows agents to discover task-conditioned collaborators under an unknown network structure, release only admissible assets, and update future cooperation decisions from auditable interaction evidence. AEP therefore provides a middle ground between pure request-response interaction and globally shared memory, offering a practical foundation for trustworthy agent-native networking through selective, auditable reuse of distributed context assets.


### arXiv 2611.29581 - Social Interaction Breaks Replicate Independence in Controlled-Clone LLM Agents

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-22T16:08:12Z
- authors: Yandan Zheng, Haoran Luo, Anh Tuan Luu
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29581

Multi-agent LLM evaluations and social simulations create many agents from one model and treat them as independent replicates, even after the agents interact. This assumption remains untested with matched controls. We introduce SocCogEval, a controlled-clone assay for Social Cognitive Transfer (SCT). Clones share the base model, system prompt, and decoding parameters and differ only in a private brief, and a factorial protocol separates the brief, 30 rounds of interaction, and initialization noise. On Qwen3-122B-FP8, interaction with the briefs held fixed raises off-topic BFI-10 response-profile drift by $\barΔ=+0.917$ and $+0.912$ in two topically disjoint scenarios (exact $p=0.0008$ and $0.0039$). The drift is larger than under the brief alone, persists under temperature-zero re-probing, and stays positive without typed memory extraction in exploratory tests. Matched solo generation reproduces part of it, and social interaction remains highest. Across five more model variants, every scenario-averaged interaction contrast is positive. Each interacting population is therefore one replicate, and treating its agents as independent requires paired no-interaction controls.


### arXiv 2611.29548 - Real-Time Kinodynamic Multi-Agent Motion Planning

- categories: cs.RO, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-22T15:19:23Z
- authors: Jiarui Li, Runyu Zhang, Gioele Zardini
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29548

Coordinating large robot fleets, from warehouses to quadrotor swarms, requires collision-free trajectories that satisfy dynamic constraints and stay within speed and acceleration bounds. Existing kinodynamic multi-agent planners enforce these bounds during the search or through trajectory optimization after it, which limits fleet size and speed. We present Tickwise Actions to Kinodynamic Trajectories (TAKT), a framework that accounts for the dynamics before the search and reduces planning to a lattice search for double-integrator robots. TAKT analytically bounds the clearance lost when a discrete plan is smoothed and reserves this margin on the lattice. A closed-form convolution provably converts any valid plan into dynamically feasible trajectories with continuous-time collision avoidance. For robots spanning many lattice vertices, we introduce a diamond formulation that permits one-tick sideways yielding and adapt the Multi-Agent Path Finding (MAPF) solvers PIBT and LaCAM to it. With 50 robots, our planners run over 2,000 times faster than the strongest baseline in 2D halls and over 100 times in 3D, with comparable or lower makespan and cost. They plan about a thousand robots in seconds in 2D, and tens of seconds in 3D, and replan up to a thousand robots in real time.


### arXiv 2611.31307 - When Successful Collaboration Becomes an Attack Surface: Insider Memory Poisoning in Multi-Agent Systems

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI, cs.CR
- published: 2026-11-22T12:18:13Z
- authors: Jihyung Ahn, Buru Chang
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31307

Recent Multi-Agent Systems (MAS) increasingly use shared memory to store and reuse successful collaboration records, enabling teams to improve across tasks. However, this memory introduces a new attack surface for malicious insiders. Unlike existing insider attacks that inject misleading information directly into the current collaboration, misinformation stored in shared memory can later appear as successful team experience and gain greater credibility and influence. We propose MIMPA (Multi-Agent Insider Memory Poisoning Attack), which exploits this vulnerability using only normal participation privileges, without direct memory access. During an earlier task, the malicious agent embeds a target-specific payload while helping the team answer correctly, allowing the payload to be retained in a successful collaboration record. When retrieved for a later target task, this poisoned record can steer benign agents toward a designated wrong answer, further amplified by malicious triggering. Across three benchmarks and two LLM backbones, MIMPA substantially outperforms existing insider attacks and remains effective under hierarchical memory management, while auditing only partially mitigates it. These results reveal that shared memory intended to accumulate team experience can become a persistent channel for insider attacks. Our code is available at https://github.com/ahnji0520/MIMPA-code.


### arXiv 2611.29333 - EMIP: Embodied Multi-Party Interaction Platform

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.HC, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-22T09:03:19Z
- authors: Koichiro Terao, Rikunari Sagara, Moe Nagao, Naoto Iwahashi
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29333

Experimental platforms that enable multiple participants to engage in spoken dialogue and perform physical actions concurrently in a shared environment are important for studying cooperation between humans and embodied AI agents. In this paper, we propose EMIP (Embodied Multi-Party Interaction Platform), a virtual environment platform built on Minecraft. Through an Internet-based client-server architecture, EMIP connects multiple humans and multiple AI agents to the same virtual environment and supports full-duplex speech communication and simultaneous physical interaction. It provides interfaces based on the Model Context Protocol (MCP) for environmental observation, physical action execution, and audio input and output, enabling researchers to develop their own agent control methods using large language models (LLMs). Additional features include centralized control of an agent group through a single EMIP agent client, collaborative object transportation by multiple agents, and experimental support functions such as recording and retrieving observation histories. By integrating these features into a reusable platform, EMIP aims to support research on human-human, human-agent, and agent-agent cooperation under different participant configurations and agent control methods. Usage examples illustrate concurrent speech and physical action, task coordination, and overlapping speech. The source code for the platform is publicly available on GitHub (https://github.com/AI-Humans-Lab/EMIP-Embodied-Multi-Party-Interaction-Platform).


### arXiv 2611.29283 - ARRANGE: Agentic Skills for Layering Vocals

- categories: cs.SD, cs.HC, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-22T07:11:03Z
- authors: Henry Tan, Eidee Tan
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29283

Adding a singing voice to an existing piano recording requires decisions about melody, syllable placement, pronunciation, timing, and vocal register. ARRANGE organizes this work as a sequence of agent skills with readable intermediate artifacts and composer approval checkpoints. An LLM translates instructions in plain English, notes played on a keyboard, and vocal references into local edits; deterministic scripts validate and apply those edits before open audio models render the result. The Score Canvas makes note, syllable, and pronunciation decisions inspectable and editable. In a benchmark of 16 revision requests, three draws per request, and five LLMs, typed edits achieved exact-change correctness of 0.83-0.98, compared with 0.33-0.69 for whole-plan rewriting without ARRANGE instructions. A separate score-editing benchmark and a first-person worked example examine the interaction between representations and corrections. Author listening evaluations further explore voice transfer and register selection, including 185 listening presentations across eight copied voices. These evaluations support the value of constrained editing and expose limits of both model execution and listening measures. The contribution is an interaction architecture for retaining musical decisions through inspectable intermediate representations, rather than a new singing model or evidence of general usability.


### arXiv 2611.29107 - Beyond Evidence Coverage: Encoding-Time Access in Multi-Agent Systems

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-22T00:11:01Z
- authors: Joshua Huang, Ajit Mallavarapu, Elliott Kalt
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29107

Cache-sharing multi-agent systems can retain every evidence token's representation while preventing cross-worker access during encoding. We test whether this coverage recovers joint-encoding quality using attention masks over two logical workers in frozen Qwen3-4B, Qwen3-8B, and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct models. Tokens, positions, and answer-time access remain fixed within comparisons. We evaluate 200 two-hop MuSiQue-Ans questions and 200 selected HotpotQA bridge questions. In data-layout seed 0, averaged across models, blocking access costs 5.6 and 6.8 F1 points relative to full access on MuSiQue and HotpotQA, respectively. Exposing the earlier worker's supporting paragraph improves F1 over blocked access by 7.6 and 6.7 points; equal-token non-support access provides no detectable average gain. With model weights and greedy decoding fixed, we vary distractor layouts using five data-layout seeds per dataset for Qwen3-8B and three for Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. Supporting-paragraph access has higher mean F1 than blocked and equal-token non-support access in every evaluated data-layout seed. Supporting access can also outperform full access, but its advantage depends on the model, dataset, and layout. Oracle support labels isolate access from selection error; the study does not evaluate a deployable selector. Complete evidence coverage at answer time therefore does not guarantee joint-encoding quality: which supporting facts can interact during encoding matters.


### arXiv 2611.29079 - Factory-simulator: A process-oriented environment for evaluating manufacturing decisions against optimisation-based references

- categories: cs.MA, cs.CE
- published: 2026-11-21T22:46:50Z
- authors: Andrzej Wodecki, Wojciech Godlewski, Michał Chojna
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29079

Motivated by discussions with an electronics contract manufacturer about decision support, Factory-simulator provides a deterministic manufacturing environment for controlled research on the business performance of large language model-based multi-agent systems (LLM-MAS). Equal delivery performance can arise from decisions with different financial consequences, complicating evaluation through delivery metrics alone. Its process-oriented model connects procurement and production decisions to order fulfilment and factory finances. Seeded worlds and recorded actions support replay and branching from a shared history to examine alternative decisions and their downstream consequences. Linear and mixed-integer programming models generate profit-oriented reference decisions. Observation-limited comparators use disclosed information; full-foresight references use future events for diagnostic comparison. Controlled examples show equal delivery performance with different profit under the same information and distinguish delivery penalties from deferred payment. Verification includes material and financial balances, replay and forks, and a small allocation instance in which the tested optimisation-based controllers attain an analytically established optimum. An external three-role LLM-MAS client completed four illustrative runs comprising 159 periods, with reconciled balances and exact replay of recorded actions. Matched-world rolling comparisons contextualise the financial outcomes, while full-foresight results provide heuristic reference points rather than certified optima. The software enables researchers to investigate how agent organisation and information access affect business outcomes, including whether distributing procurement and production responsibilities changes profit relative to a centralised controller.


### arXiv 2611.29069 - Do LLM Agents Compromise Like Humans? Risk and Responsibility in Dyadic Decision-Making

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-21T22:16:04Z
- authors: Noah S. Okada, Ketika Garg, Wenning Deng, Dean Mobbs
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29069

Large language model (LLM)-based agents are increasingly deployed in settings where they must collaborate to make joint decisions. We investigate how this collaboration unfolds under risk, asking whether agents adjust their choices to accommodate their partners, how they assign responsibility for shared outcomes, and whether responsibility attribution shapes subsequent adjustment. We designed a series of agents with systematically varying risk preferences and paired them in a repeated joint decision-making task previously conducted with humans. Like humans, agents converged toward their partners' choices. However, similar aggregate behavior arose from different dynamics: agents showed little evidence of reciprocal adjustment, tended to weigh rewards more, and their responsibility attribution was decoupled from compromise. Similarly, while they showed bias in their responsibility attribution, the outcome's valence shaped the attribution differently than in humans. Our results reveal important differences between human--human and agent--agent joint decision-making, demonstrating that similar social outcomes can emerge from different social dynamics and reasoning.


### arXiv 2611.28755 - Who Speaks for the Water? Simulating Discursive Closure and Power Asymmetries in Hydrosocial Governance

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-21T11:59:06Z
- authors: Mario Lillo-Saavedra, Marcela Salgado-Vargas, Ángel García-Pedrero, Diego Rivera, Marcelo Somos-Valenzuela
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28755

Water governance conflicts stem not only from material scarcity, but from power asymmetries, institutional distrust, and the discursive frameworks through which actors assign meaning to water, infrastructure, and legitimate authority. Conventional simulation tools represent these dynamics via numerical opinion variables, missing the argumentative and narrative dimensions structuring real deliberation in hydrosocial territories. We present the Hydrosocial Agent-Based Model with Large Language Models (HSABM-LLM), a hybrid framework enabling heterogeneous agents to generate, evaluate, and adapt natural language arguments conditioned on institutional roles, interaction histories, and territorial context. Grounded in semi-structured interviews, a water-user survey, and 456 press and official documents from the Longaví River sub-basin (Maule Region, Chile), the model simulates deliberation over a contested large-scale reservoir proposal across four experimental scenarios. Opinion convergence toward unanimous opposition emerged within 7-14 months, operating through discursive closure driven by distributive equity and water security framings rather than argumentative dominance. Governance actors accelerated convergence by activating pre-existing institutional distrust rather than direct persuasion, while peer-based smallholder interactions drove 77-79% of all opinion changes. The formal authority of state institutions proved structurally decoupled from deliberative influence, revealing a legitimacy deficit that formal inclusion alone cannot resolve. These findings suggest participatory governance design must attend to the discursive conditions under which institutional actors enter deliberative spaces, rather than assuming inclusion is inherently consensus-building. HSABM-LLM offers a novel platform for integrating political ecology insights into computational social science.


### arXiv 2611.28671 - The Evaluation Substrate Matters: Benchmarking Multi-Agent Serving Substrates

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-21T08:50:10Z
- authors: Haoyuan Zheng, Dongting Li, Hancheng Ye, Qinsi Wang, Mingyuan Ma, Yuncheng Yao
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28671

Multi-agent systems (MAS) usually coordinate heterogeneous models and tools to solve complex tasks, creating a growing need for reliable evaluation.
