However, a multi-agent workflow that is fastest in one serving environment can lose its advantage in another, even when its models, prompts, and task remain fixed. This makes a benchmark result conditional on how the workflow is executed. We introduce MASSys, a benchmark for measuring this dependence in multi-agent large language model (LLM) systems. MASSys executes the same candidate-task pairs across serving substrates and load regimes, using five workload families spanning software repair, research synthesis, financial analysis, interactive control, and diagram generation. It records task quality alongside delivery, latency, cost, resource pressure, and failure evidence. In a controlled motivation diagnostic, the Star workflow falls from first to fourth in mean-latency rank under KV-cache pressure, while Chain rises from fourth to first. The substrate sweeps further show that increasing load creates long runtime tails and timeout pressure. In several task families, quality among evaluated outputs changes less than effective quality over submitted jobs: the system delivers fewer usable results even when the surviving outputs remain competitive. Tool and runtime traces reveal that these outcomes involve both model serving and non-model execution. Together, the results motivate evaluating MAS designs over explicit operating conditions and reporting which tasks are delivered, how well they are solved, and where execution breaks down.


### arXiv 2611.28660 - Persuasion from Within: Model-Based Insider Attacks in Multi-Agent LLM Debate and Consensus

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-21T08:41:43Z
- authors: Bangyan Ju, Xiaolin Sun, Yibin Hu, Zizhan Zheng
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28660

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in multi-agent systems for collective decision-making, such as multi-agent debate and consensus games, in which agents exchange messages and update their answers over multiple rounds and a resolution mechanism aggregates them into a final outcome. Most multi-agent LLM frameworks assume that every participant shares the system objective. In practice, however, a malicious agent can join as a legitimate member of the group and persuade the other agents toward a hidden adversarial goal. We formalize such attacks as a sequential decision-making problem in which the insider seeks to degrade the utility of the collective outcome. We propose a model-based insider attack that learns to persuade a group of agents: the insider first learns a world model of the benign agents from offline trajectories and then uses it both to train a persuasion policy and to plan its messages. In two case studies involving a numerical consensus game and a multi-agent debate, the learned insider is the most effective and the stealthiest attack we evaluate, outperforming both prompt-based and trained LLM persuaders from prior work. The attack also transfers to other target models, larger groups, and unseen datasets, and remains effective under existing Byzantine-resilience defenses. The learned insider also enables a new defense: adversarially training benign agents on the attack data largely removes both our attack and the strongest prior persuasion attack. These results show that combining world models with reinforcement learning and planning is an effective route to both attacking and hardening language-based multi-agent systems.


### arXiv 2611.28573 - MACE: Memory-Augmented Crop-Disease Evaluation of Frozen Vision-Language Models

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI, cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-21T05:13:05Z
- authors: Tirtho Roy, Shreyan Ganguly, Muhammad Arbab Arshad, Nikhil Bhattacharya, Soumik Sarkar
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28573

In plant phenotyping, crop disease diagnosis has traditionally relied on expert assessment of visible symptoms and on models tailored to specific crops or diseases, and both are difficult to scale across field conditions. Recent methods provide frozen vision-language models with source-grounded knowledge bases that summarize the typical symptoms of each disease but describe little of how symptoms vary across individual cases. We introduce MACE (Memory-Augmented Crop-Disease Evaluation), a training-free method that supplements the knowledge base with a memory of symptom descriptions paired with known disease labels. MACE matches a description of the query image against this memory and retrieves only text descriptions and disease labels, so the query is the only image that the diagnosis model sees. Across ten crops, MACE with Claude Sonnet 5.5 improved accuracy over the knowledge-base baseline from 42.8% to 62.2% in distribution and from 50.7% to 57.0% on held-out image collections. Retrieved disease labels accounted for most of the gain, and adding the corresponding text descriptions improved in-distribution accuracy by about six percentage points.


### arXiv 2611.28517 - Digital Twins for Multi-Agent Systems: When to Trust, What to Change

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-21T03:09:42Z
- authors: Truc Nguyen Thi Le, Phuoc Nguyen T. H
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28517

Digital twins of agent networks need to flag unreliable forecasts and help users choose interventions. Both decisions depend on what happens over several steps: an error can spread through the network, and an edit whose trajectory is easy to predict may still yield a poor collective outcome. We ask when information about error propagation improves failure detection and whether a learned twin can select effective network edits despite imperfect forecasts. We propose Propagation-Aware Validation for Networked Digital Twins (PAV-Twin). Its monitor combines Jacobian-based propagation features with recent errors and ensemble disagreement to estimate the risk that a forecast exceeds an error threshold. Its planner simulates each candidate edit and ranks the edits by their predicted collective outcomes. In simulations, the monitor gained 0.13 in area under the precision-recall curve on larger oscillator networks with familiar topologies and 0.09 on larger networks with held-out topologies, relative to a graph baseline without propagation features. Gains were small in contractive consensus. Giving the graph baseline the same features narrowed the gap. In a separate planning study, ensemble simulation recovered about 99% of the possible improvement over random intervention selection in oscillators and nearly all of it in consensus. With a simplified oscillator twin, selecting by predicted outcome also gave lower regret than selecting by trajectory fidelity. These results show when propagation features help flag unreliable forecasts and why the quality of intervention choices should be measured directly.


### arXiv 2611.28371 - How a Prediction Dies: A Challenge-Driven, Rule-Bound Self-Refutation in an LLM Agent Society

- categories: cs.MA, physics.soc-ph
- published: 2026-11-20T22:28:21Z
- authors: Lei Zhenbing, Lei Chang
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28371

Persistent societies of large language model agents now make empirical claims about their own collective behaviour, yet the fate of such claims is rarely observable. We document one claim, from registration to death, on AITRAP, a multi-agent platform whose public, append-only ledger records every action. The claim (0495d171, registered 19 October 2026) asserted that a majority of bridges between problem domains would remain attached to a single exploration lineage. Three community challenges forced its judgment criterion through four versions, from an ambiguous statement to an executable protocol with a fixed threshold, a minimum-frame-size gate, and a baseline calibration gate. When the first frame with at least 20 bridges arrived, support stood at 9 of 79 (11.4%; Wilson 95% CI [6.1%, 20.3%]); the author, bound by the registered clause, refuted the claim and relinquished the associated stake. Three findings emerge. The criterion became executable only under community pressure, which we document version by version. The calibration gate caught a fault in the author's own recalculation that would otherwise have published a spuriously perfect 0/79 result. The failure itself traces to a denominator that grew 8.8-fold in five days while the numerator grew 1.8-fold, coinciding with a change in platform incentives; we report this mechanism as a hypothesis, not a rescuing condition. A claim whose termination is carried out exactly as written carries more evidential weight than one that survives, and this case provides initial empirical grounds for treating voluntary self-refutation as a candidate indicator of epistemic accountability in incentive-coupled agent societies.


### arXiv 2611.28351 - Coupling-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Co-Evolving Graph Games

- categories: cs.LG, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-20T22:01:24Z
- authors: Mina Kim, Guanghui Lan, Benoit Montreuil
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28351

Building hydrogen station networks, electric vehicle charging grids, or vaccine distribution systems requires coordinating two separately operated graphs, demand-side facilities and the supply-side networks they depend on, where each operator's payoff depends on the other's choices. We formalize this as the Co-Evolving Graph Game (CEGG), a setting that sits between facility location, interdependent-network analysis, and graph-based multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) but is not jointly addressed by any of them. We propose Cross-Graph Attention with Policy Mirror Descent (CGA-PMD), pairing cross-graph attention, which lets each agent read only the partner-graph nodes structurally coupled to its own, with a policy mirror descent update for stable training. Across three reward domains and a range of practical network scales, we find that the structure of the coupling selects which method wins: at small scale most methods are competitive, but as scale grows or coupling becomes irregular, CGA-PMD is the one method that stays reliable: it attains the highest success count on each of five real city networks, while own-only, unmasked-attention, and Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO)-based variants degrade in distinct ways. Our theoretical analysis addresses the optimizer side: the Kullback-Leibler (KL)-regularized update admits an iteration-uniform cumulative drift bound, whereas the clip update admits no such bound on the iterations where the clip does not bind. The implementation is available at https://github.com/Mina-Kim-Github/cegg-cga-pmd.


### arXiv 2611.28173 - Selective Verification in Multi-Agent Orchestration with Large Language Models

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI, cs.DC
- published: 2026-11-20T04:42:14Z
- authors: Ramesh Krishnamaneni, Muralidhar Kurni
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28173

Agents can agree because they relied on the same flawed evidence. We use an exact model of nine binary votes to examine when a verifier should intervene. In the main comparison, workers err 20 percent of the time under ordinary conditions, shared failures occur in 10 percent of cases, and the verifier errs 2 percent of the time in either state. When worker error during a shared failure is 80 percent, checking close votes yields 7.93 percent verdict error, compared with 9.06 percent for random checks at the same expected check rate. The ordering reverses at 90 percent worker error during failure. At the matched check rate for that regime, checking close votes yields 9.98 percent error, random checks yield 9.41 percent, and ranking checks by their expected error reduction yields 6.73 percent. The model also shows that passing branch vote counts preserves the flat majority and reduces the number of messages the root receives. A design guide and a documentation audit of four Python frameworks translate these findings into implementation choices.


### arXiv 2611.28170 - How Behavioral Structure Shapes Agent Strategy in Language-Based Economic Games

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-20T03:59:59Z
- authors: Yichi Zhang, Shuo Yang, Jun Wang, Chunhua Liu
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28170

In competitive multi-agent economic games, agents continuously interact, strategize, and make decisions within a shared market. Consequently, every message produced by a language agent changes both the current economic state of the market and the opponent's subsequent behavior. Although terminal rewards can indicate whether a decision was effective and broad behavioral labels can provide a coarse interpretation of the interaction, neither specifies the concrete response that should be taken next. We therefore ask whether explicitly providing an opponent's operational structure to a focal agent changes the value of its task-specific executable response strategies and the resulting game outcome. Using Games in Language-based Economic Environments (GLEE) as a controlled environment, we investigate this question across bargaining, negotiation, and persuasion games. We analyze 298,357 trajectories using seven relational profiles and decompose each task's dominant profile into ten Act$\cdot$onomy structures, finding that dominant profiles have task-specific operational compositions. We then compare structure-blind and structure-aware policies in matched offline simulation and prospective online play. In fixed 334-game online cohorts for each task, the aware-minus-blind official-percentile point estimates of the profile-gated eligible samples are +2.52, +1.90, and +5.91 points in bargaining, negotiation, and persuasion, respectively (corresponding 95% confidence intervals include zero). Taken together, the results distinguish strategy selection from response realization: structure re-ranks strategies, while in persuasion the online point estimate becomes positive only when the policy also changes the submitted message or purchase action.


### arXiv 2611.31284 - Irreducible Governance Structure for Autonomous Agent Systems: Fair Allocation, Strategy-Proofness, and Multi-Scale Composition

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-19T18:40:56Z
- authors: Marcelo Fernandez
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31284

Autonomous agent governance is commonly framed as a problem of enforcing correct execution. However, in multi-agent systems, an equally fundamental question precedes execution: which agents are allowed to act. In this paper, we show that allocation is not an auxiliary concern but a structural component of governance. When multiple agents compete for execution under constrained resources, any allocation mechanism introduces unavoidable trade-offs between fairness, efficiency, and resistance to strategic manipulation. We formalize this through a set of impossibility results, including Sybil amplification and the incompatibility of desirable allocation properties. We then show that these allocation constraints cannot be resolved within a single layer of control. Instead, they propagate across the system, interacting with execution atomicity, state enforcement, and behavioral drift. This leads to a multi-scale governance structure composed of four orthogonal dimensions: temporal (atomicity), state (enforcement), behavioral (drift monitoring), and population (allocation). Our main result is an irreducibility theorem: under finite observability, no governance architecture can collapse these dimensions without loss of correctness or stability. Allocation, in particular, acts as a first-class constraint that prevents simplification of the governance stack. We validate these results through a series of analytical constructions and empirical ablations, showing that removing or approximating any layer leads to systematic failure modes. This work reframes agent governance as a structural problem: not only how actions are executed, but how access to execution is determined, and why these decisions impose irreducible architectural requirements.


### arXiv 2611.28148 - SYLVA: Self-Correcting Multi-Agent Markets over Frozen LLMs via Escrow Settlement, Step-Level Fault Isolation, and Contextual Routing

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-19T15:51:04Z
- authors: Hui Zhao
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28148

Open agent standards like the Model Context Protocol (MCP) and Agent2Agent (A2A) connect independent AI tools, yet open multi-agent markets fail under three real-world hazards: local rules shift silently outside pretraining, copycat wrappers and confident hallucinators game ratings, and uniform chain scoring punishes accurate specialists whenever another stage fails (blame collapse). We present SYLVA, a market protocol turning frozen foundation models (no weight updates, $ΔW = 0$) and human-maintained services into a self-correcting ecosystem. SYLVA combines an on-chain clearinghouse for escrow settlement and configurable creator royalties (85%/15% in our experiments), a boundary verifier for penalty-free out-of-domain (OOD) abstention and 3-peer step replay, a verifiable context-aware router compiling proven chains into 1-call skills with drift circuit breakers, and plain-English rule updates. We prove sufficient conditions for honest abstention, specialist protection against blame collapse, and wrapper elimination, and validate SYLVA across a 64-agent simulation, 6,543 live deepseek-flash API calls, and a Rust MCP SDK (rmcp) + Cairo clearinghouse deployed on Starknet Sepolia (19 live transactions). Without updating weights, SYLVA reaches 82.1% completion in 64-agent simulations and 98.3% on live LLM workflows (vs. 0.0% single frozen LLM and 45.6% static MCP/A2A), cuts false specialist penalties by 6.79x-10.8x, retains 86.2% creator revenue under wrapper/Sybil floods, and recovers rapidly after rule drift. Furthermore, 1-call skill compilation saves 78.3% of real LLM tokens, while our Starknet contracts verify zero-oracle quorum slashing and 32.32-bit fixed-point (Q32.32) routing on-chain. Code and contracts are at https://github.com/heurion-org/sylva.


### arXiv 2611.28103 - ABCoord: An Artificial Bee Colony (ABC)-Based Decentralized Distributed Two-Layer Decision-Making Architecture for the Coordination of AI Agents

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI, cs.NE
- published: 2026-11-17T12:31:18Z
- authors: Dervis Karaboga, Bugra Alperen Uluirmak
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28103

Rapid progress in large language models (LLMs) is turning AI agents into multi-agent systems that jointly solve complex tasks, and the architecture that coordinates the agents toward a common goal is gaining fundamental importance in these systems. Since centralized architectures can become bottlenecks as the number of agents grows, whereas distributed architectures incur increasing communication costs, a decentralized coordination architecture that scales with low communication cost is needed. Recently, the structure of the artificial bee colony (ABC) algorithm has been defined for the first time as a two-layer decentralized information-processing and decision-making architecture. ABCoord is built on this proposed architecture. The results show that ABCoord, used for the coordination of AI agents, performs at a level comparable to existing architectures on the BigCodeBench benchmark in the self-test condition, in which the tests are also written by the language model. In the oracle condition, in which the solutions are evaluated with the official tests, the number of solved problems increases considerably.


### arXiv 2611.33005 - Leto: Utility-Centric Agent Memory

- categories: cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-24T17:30:06Z
- authors: Shuguang Chen, Salman Paracha
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.33005

Large language model (LLM) agents rely on external memory to retain and reuse information across interactions. Existing memory systems treat memory as a record of the past and evaluate it by fidelity to the conversation. However, the value of memory lies in whether it improves the agent's response. A memory system decides what to memorize and what to serve before that response exists, and fidelity checks neither decision against it. Faithful memory can therefore fail to improve the response. We instead view memory as a prediction problem and present \textbf{\emph{\leto{}}}, a memory system for LLM agents that focuses on the utility of memory to the future rather than on its fidelity to the past. Because future requests are unknown, \emph{\leto{}} preserves access to conversation details through memory indexing, allowing information to remain available through its sources. When a request arrives, memory replay draws on this index to anticipate and supply the context the agent needs beyond what the request explicitly states. The utility of the served context remains uncertain until the response is generated, motivating a utility loop that uses response outcomes to refine future memory selection. We evaluate our system against six memory systems on five benchmarks across three scenarios. In personalization, it outperforms the strongest compared memory system by 16.3 percentage points in accuracy on PrefEval and by 3.1 points in task completion on PERMA. For long-horizon tasks, it achieves the best overall performance on MemoryArena averaged over five environments with a gain that increases with the chain depth. In long-context understanding, it is on par with retrieval over the raw history while serving fewer context tokens.


### arXiv 2611.32812 - Breaking the Joint Action Space Dependence in Distributionally Robust Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning

- categories: cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-24T15:32:04Z
- authors: Yue Wang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32812

We study the sample complexity of learning robust coarse correlated equilibria (CCE) in finite-horizon, general-sum, distributionally robust Markov games with known rewards and a generative model of the nominal transitions. Each player evaluates a policy by its worst-case return over total-variation (TV) balls centered at the nominal transition distribution of every stage, state, and joint action; this is the standard $(s,a)$-rectangular uncertainty set. Existing generative-model guarantees for this model scale with the number $\prod_iA_i$ of joint actions, where $A_i$ is the number of actions of player~$i$. Since this number grows exponentially with the number of players, these guarantees suffer from the curse of multiagency. We design an algorithm whose sample complexity depends on the action sets only through the largest individual action-set size $A_*:=\max_iA_i$. The algorithm uses $\widetilde O(SA_*H^5D^4/\varepsilon^4)$ transition samples and, with probability at least $1-δ$, returns a Markov policy that is an $\varepsilon$-robust CCE from every initial state. Here $S$ is the number of states, $H$ is the horizon, and $D:=\max_i\min\{H,1/ρ_i\}$, where $ρ_i$ is the TV radius of player~$i$. Two ingredients make this possible: a plug-in estimate of the robust backup whose mean error does not depend on the number of states, and a single batch of samples per round that serves as bandit feedback for all players at once. The result shows that the dependence on $\prod_iA_i$ can be avoided without modifying the $(s,a)$-rectangular uncertainty set.


### arXiv 2611.32445 - A Mesh to Remember: Persistent Appearance for Interactive Video Worlds

- categories: cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-24T11:42:48Z
- authors: Joonghyuk Shin, Alex Trevithick, Katarína Tóthová, Liyuan Zhu, Shengyu Huang, Jaesik Park
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32445

Video world models synthesize photorealistic observations in response to user actions but struggle to remain consistent over long interactions. Coding agents and text-to-3D models instead generate explicit 3D worlds whose geometry and state persist by construction, but whose appearance falls far short of photorealism. Recent methods combine the strengths of both by rendering such worlds into coarse proxies that guide a video model. However, since these proxies only specify where things are, the renderer must invent what they look like as the world is explored, and anything it forgets is reinvented when a surface reappears. Our key insight is that a mesh identifies which surface each pixel shows, across frames and views, even as objects move, allowing it to serve as an address for what the renderer generates. We propose M2R (Mesh to Remember), a few-step causal video renderer that stores its own features on the surfaces they depict, in each object's frame, and retrieves them by rasterizing the mesh whenever those surfaces come back into view. Appearance thus travels with objects wherever they move, persists across revisits, and is shared by every agent that observes it, while memory grows with the surfaces seen instead of the number of frames. Trained only on synthetic data, M2R transfers zero-shot to real indoor scans and to worlds authored by coding agents. It outperforms state-of-the-art methods in consistency across static, dynamic, and multi-agent benchmarks, more than doubling object permanence and nearly doubling cross-agent consistency, while rendering 720p video in real time (40 FPS).


### arXiv 2611.32125 - LARF: Learning-aligned Reasoning Framework for Search Relevance

- categories: cs.IR
- published: 2026-11-24T07:41:25Z
- authors: Tian Tang, Guo Chen, Reed Li, Hao Wang, Yu Lu, Haijin Liang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32125

Query-document relevance estimation is a core component of AI-native search systems, where a model must assess whether retrieved documents satisfy users' information needs under complex, multi-dimensional criteria. However, directly applying large language models (LLMs) to this task remains challenging because conventional supervised fine-tuning (SFT) provides limited supervision for domain-specific judgment rationales, while standard trajectory-level reinforcement learning (RL) objectives do not explicitly enforce margin separation among adjacent ordinal relevance labels. Moreover, although such reasoning can be useful during training, chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning is impractical for latency-sensitive online ranking. These challenges make it difficult to resolve ambiguous decision boundaries. We propose a three-stage framework LARF (Learning-Aligned Reasoning Framework for Search Relevance): it first performs cold-start SFT on strategically selected samples with multi-agent guided CoT rationales, then applies discriminative GRPO with attribute-wise, list-wise, and rule-consistency rewards together with a margin loss on the terminal relevance token, and finally distills the teacher into a dual-head student whose classification heads are retained for non-autoregressive inference. On a human-annotated web search relevance benchmark with approximately 300K training and 16K test instances, LARF-8B improves six-class classification accuracy by 5.78 percentage points and NDCG by 5.05 points, while the classification-only serving path reduces inference latency by 82.9\%. These results indicate that aligning structured reasoning, terminal decision optimization, and classification-head distillation can provide an effective accuracy--efficiency trade-off for industrial search relevance modeling.


### arXiv 2611.32122 - SCOPE: Evaluating and Enhancing Permission Explanation Transparency in Mobile Apps

- categories: cs.SE
- published: 2026-11-24T07:39:44Z
- authors: Liu Wang, Tianshu Zhou, Haoyu Wang, Xiyuan Liu, Yi Wang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32122

Permission explanations, explanatory text accompanying mobile app permission requests, are crucial for user privacy transparency and informed consent. Despite their importance, current practices often fall short of regulatory expectations due to the lack of systematic evaluation mechanisms. Through an empirical study of 600 mainstream mobile apps, we reveal widespread deficiencies: 15\% of permission requests provide no explanation, others use vague language or technical jargon, and critically, many fail to disclose third-party SDK data access despite these components actively using granted permissions. To address these transparency gaps, we present SCOPE, an automated multi-agent framework that systematically evaluates permission explanation compliance and generates targeted optimization recommendations. SCOPE employs four specialized agents working collaboratively: multimodal LLM-based explanation extraction, few-shot learning-based linguistic analysis, dynamic API-based purpose inference, and adaptive report generation. Comprehensive evaluation demonstrates SCOPE's effectiveness, achieving 98\% accuracy in explanation extraction, 93.5\% consistency in compliance analysis, and 92\% accuracy in purpose inference. A user study with 30 participants shows 84.6\% preference for SCOPE-optimized explanations, confirming practical utility. Our work provides the first systematic analysis of permission explanation practices and establishes a scalable solution for enhancing mobile app privacy transparency.


### arXiv 2611.32105 - Wireless-OptSG: A Versatile Multi-Agent Optimization Framework for Wireless Resource Allocation

- categories: eess.SP
- published: 2026-11-24T07:24:45Z
- authors: Wenhe Zhang, Xin Li, Hanzhi Chen, Chau Yuen
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32105

6G wireless networks are expected to accommodate heterogeneous service requirements across diverse deployment scenarios, giving rise to physical-layer resource allocation problems with scenario-dependent objectives and constraints. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) and agent systems can interpret wireless-domain specifications and incorporate execution feedback into optimization. However, translating language-level reasoning into feasible, high-quality resource allocations remains challenging, since candidates need to satisfy precise mathematical constraints throughout iterative search. This work proposes the Wireless Optimization Solution Generator (Wireless-OptSG), a versatile multi-agent framework for physical-layer resource allocation. Wireless-OptSG organizes optimization as a feedback-driven candidate search guided by a static meta-prompt and an evolving optimization history. In a nested outer-inner loop, parallel agents explore solution trajectories and periodically exchange high-quality records to guide candidate generation. Feasible initialization uses executable optimization primitives, while a tiered repair pipeline restores feasibility and solution-dependent context improves retained-record informativeness. Experiments on four representative tasks under perfect and imperfect channel state information show that Wireless-OptSG achieves performance comparable to scenario-specific state-of-the-art (SOTA) solvers, exceeds SOTA in some settings, and consistently outperforms generic baselines, demonstrating its potential as a transferable optimization framework for future wireless networks.


### arXiv 2611.32044 - CHORAL: A Multi-Agent Scaffold for Longitudinal Prognostic Reasoning from Patient-Generated Health Data

- categories: cs.HC
- published: 2026-11-24T06:28:29Z
- authors: Youngji Koh, Esther Kang, Gyuna Kim, Seungwan Jin, Hyunsoo Lee, Kyungsik Han
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32044

Longitudinal patient-generated health data can inform prognostic reasoning about how patients' conditions may evolve between follow-up visits. Existing approaches summarize trajectories or predict symptom states, but provide limited support for examining how heterogeneous evidence informs prognosis. We present CHORAL, a multi-agent scaffold that forms domain-specific appraisals before cross-domain synthesis and review, preserving evidence and revisions. Informed by a formative study with 12 psychiatrists, we evaluated CHORAL quantitatively and with 22 clinicians. CHORAL achieved balanced accuracy comparable to persistence (0.673 vs. 0.672), while detecting 40% of LOW-to-HIGH deterioration cases missed by persistence. Versus a single-agent baseline, it improved balanced accuracy from 0.639 to 0.673 and evidence coverage from 70.1% to 89.2%. Clinicians rated CHORAL significantly higher for evidence appraisal, alternative interpretations, grounds for challenge, and distinguishing what to rely on from what required verification. These findings show how multi-agent deliberation can support review by exposing alternative interpretations and grounds for verification.


### arXiv 2611.31938 - CAPE: Coordinating Calibration and Search for Tool-Use Policies

- categories: cs.AI, cs.SE
- published: 2026-11-24T04:43:02Z
- authors: Jiahao Wang, Xiaoxuan Chen, Aijun Yang, Xiaohua Wang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31938

A tool call can fail even when the operation behind it is correct, because the receiving tool parses delimiters, dates or record layouts under a configuration the agent cannot inspect. Compiling a tool-use policy for such a tool requires both calibration, which evaluates candidate calls on candidate configurations, and search over the evaluated calls. The two share one compilation budget: calibrating everything first postpones any improved policy until the end, while re-solving every partially known model delays the next useful query. A call becomes usable as soon as its effects are known on the configurations that reach it. We present CAPE, a training-free compiler built on this observation. It maintains an executable policy at all times, directs calibration to the calls that can improve this policy, and tightens bounds only where doing so ends calibration early. Returned policies preserve every configuration the initial policy completes, and CAPE certifies optimality when its executable and optimistic values meet. On CSV and JSON workflows built from 64 requests, CAPE reaches the independently computed optimum in 502 of 504 compilations while taking 22.0% less compilation time than eager exact search. On larger CSV families it returns policies with higher completion at similar compilation time, and it raises completion by 15.38 to 34.66 percentage points over an LLM that revises its serialization from error feedback.


### arXiv 2611.31880 - Multi Agent Reinforcement Learning enhanced Federated Learning for Predicting Remaining Useful Lifetime

- categories: cs.LG, cs.DC
- published: 2026-11-24T03:39:05Z
- authors: Rajarshi Chattopadhyay, Chen-Khong Tham
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31880

Remaining useful life (RUL) prediction is central to Prognostics and Health Management (PHM). The growth of industrial IoT has unlocked large multivariate sensor streams and enabled deep models - especially Transformers - for long-horizon sequence learning. However, transformers are parameter-heavy and data-hungry, while operational datasets are often siloed and privacy-sensitive. Federated Learning (FL) addresses this by training across clients without raw data sharing, but in practice many clients connect over lossy wireless links where UDP is preferred for low latency, making model exchange vulnerable to packet drops. We present a cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) framework that adapts FL to lossy channels by selecting per-client layer subsets to transmit and capping retransmissions. This framework is trained with QMIX value-function factorization for scalability. We instantiate the approach with a Graph Convolution Position-Aware Transformer (GCPATr) for RUL forecasting. On the CMAPSS dataset, our MARL-enhanced FL (RL-FL) consistently improves mean square error over standard FL, with the largest gains at high packet loss (e.g., up to 18% improvement on FD003 at 50% loss). These results show that MARL is an effective countermeasure to wireless degradation in FL-based RUL prediction.


### arXiv 2611.31873 - RepoEviAgent: Scalable Synthesis of Evidence-Grounded Repository-Level Question Answering Tasks

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-24T03:24:30Z
- authors: Aoqi Hu, Yueao Xing
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31873

Real-world software engineering (SWE) tasks require agents to understand repository structures, cross-module dependencies, and runtime behaviors, posing significant challenges for constructing high-quality training and evaluation data. To address this, we propose RepoEviAgent, a scalable SWE data construction framework that integrates quality-aware data curation and role-specialized multi-agent task synthesis. Starting from 8,477 open-source repositories and approximately 2.48 million pull request records, we employ semantic alignment, complexity constraints, and multidimensional quality assessment to identify valuable SWE data. We further develop a sandbox-based multi-agent framework that integrates automated environment construction, execution validation, repository exploration, and evidence-grounded Q&A synthesis. Based on this framework, we construct RepoEviQA, a benchmark for Repository-Level Evidence-Grounded Question Answering, comprising 334 tasks across five programming languages and diverse SWE categories, with rigorous rubric-based evaluation. We evaluate five frontier coding models on RepoEviQA, revealing substantial performance differences across task categories and programming languages.


### arXiv 2611.31804 - Federated Policy Gradient for Dynamic Consensus: Gradient Dominance and Topological Bounds

- categories: eess.SY
- published: 2026-11-24T01:56:36Z
- authors: Maryam Babazadeh
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31804

This paper presents a federated optimization framework for dynamic consensus in homogeneous multi-agent systems. The underlying problem is challenged by the communication graph's spectral properties, which induce spatial heterogeneity and complicate decentralized control even when the dynamics of the agents are completely known. The problem is addressed in this paper by bridging distributed control theory with federated policy gradients. Accordingly, a comprehensive optimization landscape for dynamic consensus is established through a modal decomposition formulation. Specifically, a parameterized suboptimality bound is characterized that explicitly decouples the true aggregate policy gradient from the network covariance mismatch. Building on this formulation, sufficient conditions based on the graph's Laplacian spectrum are derived to recover exact gradient dominance, which guarantees convergence to the global consensus policy and establishes a lower bound on the achievable dominance coefficient. Furthermore, a Federated model-free consensus algorithm is proposed that utilizes zeroth-order exploration alongside a bisection-based discount factor annealing mechanism, successfully eliminating the need for an initial stabilizing policy. This approach achieves linear global convergence for policy optimization subroutines while maintaining strictly bounded outer iterations until dynamic consensus is achieved. Through extensive simulation studies and comparative feasibility analyses across diverse network topologies, it is demonstrated that the proposed federated approach consistently outperforms existing model-based and data-driven separation-based techniques.


### arXiv 2611.31756 - TACT-Bench: Testing Adaptive Conversational Threats against Tool-Use Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.CR
- published: 2026-11-24T01:01:49Z
- authors: Ilia Hashemi Rad, Feijie Wu, Haoyu Wang, Jing Gao
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31756

The growing autonomy of AI agents in using external tools and making decisions has created an urgent need for comprehensive safety benchmarks. Existing benchmarks test compromised tool channels or adaptive multi-turn attacks, though they may not capture the unique capabilities of policy-governed tool-use agents, which must resist unauthorized requests without abandoning the legitimate work embedded in the same interaction. To fill this gap, we introduce TACT-Bench, a stateful benchmark built around scenario-specific stories, personas, manipulation tactics, and response-adaptive escalation. Each adversarial task targets an action explicitly prohibited by the policy provided to the assistant, while the policy remains hidden from the attacking user. TACT pairs each adversarial task with at least one benign counterpart, and its adversarial tasks retain authorized ground-truth actions when a compliant resolution remains available. We define these base tasks as TACT-Base. We additionally introduce TACT-TrustPrime, which places an independent benign task before an unrelated attack in the same conversation. We also propose Paired Selective Trustworthiness (PST), which combines explicit safe refusal with demonstrated capability on matched benign tasks, to analyze an agent's overcautiousness and practicality. Averaged across 6 different models as assistants, TACT-Base raises Attack-Induced Failure Rate (AFR) by 17.53% over four compatible attack baselines, and TACT-TrustPrime raises it by a further 17.01%. The two evaluated defense methods reduce AFR by only 8.35%--9.41% on average and do not consistently improve PST, underscoring the need for stronger policy-enforcement methods.


### arXiv 2611.31709 - RTLDetective: Agentic RTL Debugging through Dataflow Analysis and Simulation-Guided Fault Localization

- categories: cs.AR
- published: 2026-11-23T23:59:43Z
- authors: Neusha Javidnia, Ruisi Zhang, Farinaz Koushanfar
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31709

Large language model (LLM) agents have shown promise in RTL debugging, yet accurately localizing functional errors and generating reliable repairs remain challenging. We propose RTLDetective, a multi-agent framework that integrates signal dependency analysis, simulation-guided fault localization, and knowledge-based reasoning for automated RTL debugging and repair. RTLDetective constructs signal dependency graphs from RTL code and extracts failure information from waveform traces. A lightweight localizer agent predicts expected intermediate signal values and evaluates them through targeted simulation interventions, iteratively probing signal dependencies to isolate suspicious logic and identify potential fault locations. A debug agent combines this evidence with static analysis, a curated knowledge base of hardware design principles and RTL bug patterns, and reasoning to generate corrected RTL. To reduce inference overhead, the framework reuses cached context across debugging iterations. We evaluate RTLDetective on the MEIC benchmark, achieving an 86.1% fix rate, 13.9 percentage points above the best baseline, while making fewer LLM calls.


### arXiv 2611.31682 - LimoBench: How Street Smart Are Large Language Models?

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.DB
- published: 2026-11-23T23:14:41Z
- authors: Gautam Choudhary, Jack Peng, Udesh Kumarasinghe, Rachel Rafik, Yousef Hammad, Ahmed Samy
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31682

Large language models (LLMs) have transformed how humans interact with information, enabling natural-language interfaces across various domains. They are increasingly deployed for geospatial applications including map-based question answering, location-based services, and natural-language interfaces to spatial databases. But are LLMs truly location-aware, able to reliably interpret and precisely answer location queries involving real-world coordinates, points of interest (POIs), and geospatial distances? To answer this concretely, we present LimoBench, a street-level benchmark of 82 natural-language location queries over seven canonical spatial operations: geocoding, reverse geocoding, routing, directionality, range search, $k$-nearest-neighbor search, and spatial join. The queries are grounded in real map data of West Lafayette, Indiana, and are graded by location and semantic complexity. They are designed to probe LLMs' street-level spatial awareness, from simple lookups to multi-step spatial reasoning. We also present Limo, an approach based on the Model Context Protocol (MCP) that enhances the location awareness of LLMs. Limo grounds LLMs in PostGIS-backed spatial data through a compact set of tools. We find that ungrounded models mostly achieve single-digit accuracy (3.1% for GPT-6 Luna) across different spatial operations. In contrast, the same models achieve near-perfect accuracy (99.3% for GPT-6 Luna) when given access to Limo's spatial tools through MCP. Current LLMs thus have limited and unreliable street-level spatial knowledge, while grounding provides accurate answers at modest overhead (+0.2 s latency, 7.7$\times$ tokens, and 2.2$\times$ cost for GPT-6 Luna). We release the benchmark, Limo toolset, and evaluation code to encourage the community to evaluate their own location-aware LLM approaches.


### arXiv 2611.31656 - BRAG: Bayesian Retrieval Augmented Generation

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-23T22:41:00Z
- authors: Javier Movellan, Manjot Bilkhu, Young-mok Jung, Sirajul Salekin, Henry Tran, Zhao Huang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31656

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) ranks memory chunks by semantic similarity to the query. Many methods improve on this by learning from feedback on what was retrieved, but each is a separate heuristic, with no account of when it is the right choice. We introduce BRAG, a generative model of retrieval with use. Each chunk generates the queries it answers from a mixture of von Mises-Fisher components anchored at its original embedding, and each chunk has a latent utility that drifts over time. The model gives the optimal inference, a closed-form filter that stores a few keys and two scalars per chunk and updates them online from the labels of a curator, and it frames retrieval as a control problem. It also shows when existing methods are optimal. Query averaging, Rocchio-style feedback and frequency reranking are special cases with fixed parameters, NUDGE is the case of one key per chunk with no dynamics, and LADER the limit of one key per past query. On two public long-term memory benchmarks, BRAG matches these methods on queries asked before, as expected, because when nothing changes over time the assumptions behind these special cases hold. On harder problems, where a new chunk makes an old one obsolete, BRAG outscores them. They all stay at plain-search level (Recall@5 0.67-0.71), while BRAG retrieves the new chunk 0.80-0.86 of the time. We also ran BRAG on three of NUDGE's seven text benchmarks, and it matches or beats NUDGE on all three. On nine months of a personal agent's memory (20,640 chunks), BRAG raises Recall@5 on held-out queries from 0.75 to 0.98 and Recall@1 from 0.44 to 0.97, with little change on chunks never queried.


### arXiv 2611.31611 - Multi-agent System for Automatic Discovery of Pareto-optimal Meta-solvers for PDEs

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-23T21:55:34Z
- authors: Youngkyu Lee, Shanqing Liu, Jerome Darbon, George EM Karniadakis
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31611

Designing linear solvers for discretized partial differential equations requires problem-specific expertise. We present a multi-agent framework, in which language-model agents propose, critique, implement, and evaluate solver configurations assembled from Krylov methods, preconditioners, block factorizations, and neural operators. Inside the proposing agent, a non-dominated sorting genetic evolutionary algorithm evolves the population of meta-solvers toward the Pareto front of six measured quantities, so that one run returns a front of trade-offs rather than a single tuned solver. The system runs on small open-weight language models served on a single laptop, and we compare it with a single agent on four problems. For three-dimensional acoustic scattering and elasticity, it delivers 47 and 48 convergent hybrid solvers out of 60 candidates. Relative to the Jacobi-preconditioned Krylov baselines, the best of the discovered solvers reduce the iteration counts by factors of 4.7 and 60 and the online solve time by factors of 2.3 and 3.0, whereas the single agent produces no executable plan. For coupled dynamic thermoelasticity in two and three dimensions, where the baselines fail, it completes 98 of 100 transient candidates and, compared with the single agent, reaches a given accuracy 15 times faster in two dimensions and a displacement error 20 times lower in three dimensions.


### arXiv 2611.31582 - LatentLens: Rethinking Latent Interface in Multi-Agent Systems

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-23T21:26:38Z
- authors: Ziqing Yang, Kewei Cheng, Mingjie Li, Yang Zhang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31582

Latent interfaces let agents in a multi-agent system communicate through continuous representations rather than decoded text. Prior work reports performance and efficiency gains, but neither separates latent communication from the agent harness nor examines its internal components, leaving its actual contribution unclear. We propose LatentLens, a framework that decouples the latent interface at two levels: externally from the agent harness, to measure its contribution to overall performance (RQ1), and internally into realignment compatibility, information accessibility, and propagation dynamics, to identify the benefits and limitations of each (RQ2). Across seven benchmarks with Qwen3 models, adding the latent interface to the same harness does not improve and can even substantially degrade accuracy. Internally, latent representations retain some domain information, but incompatible realignment can substantially degrade performance, and repeated latent steps can amplify representation-level failures. Thus, while latent representations may preserve information, they do not necessarily ensure effective communication, which also requires compatible realignment, information accessibility, and stable propagation.


### arXiv 2611.31475 - SpatialCraft: Evolving Procedural Intelligence from Experience and Skills for Spatial Reasoning Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-23T19:47:57Z
- authors: Xiwei Liu, Yulong Li, Xuhui Li, Zhixiang Lu, Haolin Yang, Jianxu Chen
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31475

Spatial reasoning agents increasingly rely on external tools, such as 3D reconstruction, segmentation, and geometric computation, to answer questions that a multimodal large language model (MLLM) cannot resolve from pixels alone. Tool access, however, shifts the bottleneck to orchestration: the agent must decide which tools to call, in what order, and how to interpret their outputs, yet a stateless agent repeats the same mistakes across tasks. We ask whether a frozen tool-using agent can improve its orchestration from its own interaction history, without parameter updates. We present SpatialCraft, a self-evolving framework that maintains two forms of external knowledge: Experiences, condition-action tactics distilled by contrasting rollouts of the same task, and Skills, multi-step procedures with explicit activation and termination conditions. Three designs couple the two streams. Experiences can be anchored to a Skill, appearing only while it is active rather than once per task. A Contrastive Utility Score estimates each Experience's effect by randomly withholding it across rollouts of the same task, separating its contribution from task difficulty, and Experiences with consistently positive utility are consolidated into the Skills they support. Across five spatial benchmarks and four backbones, SpatialCraft achieves the best accuracy in all 20 settings, showing that a frozen agent can learn to orchestrate spatial tools from its own experience.


### arXiv 2611.31201 - Embo-Harness: Towards Understanding Frontier-Model-as-Policy Harness

- categories: cs.RO
- published: 2026-11-23T18:48:54Z
- authors: Xiao-Ming Wu, Kang Liao, Yuexiao Ma, Taolin Zhang, Mingqian Zhou, Nuo Chen
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31201

Frontier multimodal models exhibit extraordinary generalization in generating robot actions from observations and instructions, enabling a new paradigm in which they serve directly as policies within closed-loop robotic harnesses. However, the design principles of such harnesses, and the capabilities underlying their strong performance, remain unclear. We present Embo-Harness, a framework for systematically studying frontier-model-as-policy harnesses. We investigate four components of the robotic agentic loop, namely, prompting, planning, action execution, and tool use, and distill six empirical findings into a practical recipe. This recipe prioritizes interfaces that exploit the model's native visual reasoning and tool-use capabilities over handcrafted plans, skills, or action priors, and identifies effective choices for action representation, chunking, reasoning effort, visual observations, short multi-action sequences, and policy refinement. On RoboLab-120, Embo-Harness achieves an 83.3\% success rate, outperforming the strongest reported baseline by 26.2 percentage points. It also succeeds in 15 of 20 zero-shot trials across four real-world manipulation tasks. Beyond performance, controlled interventions indicate that the studied model depends more strongly on accurate spatial target estimation than on explicit low-level joint or trajectory generation. These results provide both a practical recipe for building strong frontier-model-as-policy harnesses and a clearer account of the capabilities that drive their performance.


### arXiv 2611.31346 - What People Choose Not to Say: A Multimodal Benchmark for Fine-Grained Information Manipulation in Conversations

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-23T17:57:08Z
- authors: Yuhan Bian, Zhaokai Liang, Jia Wang, Haoyu Liu, Yu Chen, Jianfeng Qu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31346

Recent advances in MLLMs have improved social understanding, enabling models to recognize emotions and distinguish truthful from deceptive behaviors. However, existing benchmarks largely focus on coarse-grained emotion recognition or binary truth-deception detection, overlooking the diverse ways people manipulate information in conversations, such as withholding, leakage, fabrication, evasion, and defensive counterclaim. Understanding these behaviors requires reasoning about underlying facts, participant knowledge, and conversational context, making them difficult to evaluate reliably. To address this gap, we introduce InfoCon, a multimodal benchmark for fine-grained information manipulation in conversations. InfoCon identifies six fine-grained strategies and derives a coarse consistency label from their factual and interactional relations. We develop an evidence-grounded multi-agent annotation framework to construct and verify reliable labels from rich contextual evidence. We benchmark representative multimodal models and systematically analyze their fine-grained information manipulation understanding, providing a testbed beyond binary truth-deception judgments.


### arXiv 2611.31344 - Query Obfuscation with Embedding-Optimized Noise (EON): A Representational Blindspot in Agentic Retrieval

- categories: cs.IR
- published: 2026-11-23T17:53:49Z
- authors: Ankit Gupta, Christoph Adami, Emily Dolson
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31344

In modern agentic and semantic retrieval systems, dense embedding models increasingly determine which intent, tool, document, memory, or image to retrieve for a query. We identify a representational failure mode in this pipeline: a meaningful query can be replaced by visibly nonsemantic content while preserving much of its retrieval outcome, even when the candidate bank is hidden from the optimizer. We introduce Embedding-Optimized Noise (EON), constructed using only the natural query representation, without access to candidate embeddings, relevance labels, rankings, retrieval feedback, or guardrail decisions. Despite this corpus-blind objective, EONs frequently preserve intent and tool routing, benchmark document search, episodic agent-memory retrieval, and cross-modal search across multiple dense embedding families. Crucially, this disconnect carries serious security implications: for malicious queries, an EON can reliably evade input guardrails that block the natural query, executing the intended safety-sensitive retrieval undetected. Through the geometry of the embedding space, we then characterize how query-level representation matching can reliably maintain subsequent retrieval outcomes over candidates unseen during optimization. Together, these findings expose a fundamental phenomenon across dual encoders that we call BLINDSPOT, where systems remain operationally blind to nonsemantic artifacts while reliably retrieving real, consequential content.


### arXiv 2611.31088 - MAASAI: A Tool-Augmented Multi-Agent System for Astronomical Data Analysis

- categories: astro-ph.IM
- published: 2026-11-23T17:35:44Z
- authors: S. Riggi, A. Ingallinera, C. Bordiu, C. Buemi, F. Bufano, M. Burgay
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31088

Modern astronomical workflows require the coordinated use of specialised software, scientific archives, heterogeneous data, and domain-specific knowledge. Advances in large language models and multimodal foundation models enable natural-language interaction with scientific data and tools. Realising this potential requires integrating these models with established scientific capabilities. We present MAASAI (Multi-agent Astronomy Assistant for Scientific Analysis and Inference), a modular multi-agent system combining foundation-model reasoning, retrieval-augmented knowledge, and specialised astronomical tools within a graph-based execution framework. MAASAI coordinates request preparation, planning, execution, and response synthesis via specialised workers. Its architecture supports native and external applications, artefact and provenance management, optional human approval, and configurable inference backends, including fully local deployment with open-weight models. Motivated by SKA-era data-analysis challenges, MAASAI is also designed to support other astronomical domains. We evaluate MAASAI through radio-image benchmarks, single- and multi-step workflow demonstrations, and literature-grounded reasoning. Compared with a commercial-model baseline, MAASAI improves macro-averaged F1 scores from 0.53 to 0.72 for extended/diffuse emission classification and from 0.37 to 0.88 for source morphology classification. Workflow demonstrations show the coordination of measurements, source extraction, archive queries, and catalogue processing. A 94-question literature benchmark yielded generally favourable automated assessments, with comparable ratings from a pilot expert evaluation of 59 answers. These results highlight how tool-augmented multi-agent systems extend foundation-model reasoning to specialised astronomical analyses through established scientific software.


### arXiv 2611.31338 - Per-Attack Blocking Hides Catalog Vulnerability: A Weakest-Link Analysis of Tool Manifests

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-23T17:22:47Z
- authors: Karthik Karunanithi
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31338

Runtime controls for tool-using agents depend on manifests that state what each tool may read and where its output may go. Evaluations of these controls, including ChainCaps, report per-attack blocking: the share of attacks a set of manifests stops. We show that this is the wrong unit. A catalog is only as safe as its weakest source policy: if $M$ independently authored policies are each wrong with probability $p$, the chance that the catalog contains an accepted exfiltration path is $1-(1-p)^M$, while per-attack blocking stays at $1-p$. Three lines of evidence agree. In synthetic catalogs run through two enforcement engines, ChainCaps and OpenAPPA, per-attack blocking holds at 92% while catalog vulnerability rises from 22% at 10 tools to 99% at 200, and composition provides no protective effect. A fifteen-model authoring pilot shows that real errors cluster by author, so a 200-tool catalog behaves like about 5 independent decisions rather than 60 (95% interval 3.4 to 9.2). On 114 real MCP tools with expert-written rules, the models' own manifests block 89% of attacks yet leave 9 of 15 catalogs open, as the author-effect model predicts and independence does not. Evaluations should report catalog vulnerability, the sink coverage of their tests, and an effective number of independent decisions alongside per-attack blocking.


### arXiv 2611.31022 - OmniV-Bench: Can MLLMs Comprehend Immersive Spatio-temporal Scenes?

- categories: cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-23T17:03:16Z
- authors: Liu Yang, Huiyu Duan, Yixuan Gao, Sirui Liu, Lu Liu, Zhaolin Cai
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31022

Omnidirectional videos (ODVs) capture dynamic environments with a full $360^\circ$ field of view, providing rich spatial context and temporal dynamics for applications such as virtual reality (VR), augmented reality (AR), and embodied intelligence. Recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made great progress in spatial and temporal understanding, yet their ability to comprehend the immersive 360 spatio-temporal information conveyed by ODVs remains largely unexplored. To bridge this gap, we first introduce OmniV-Bench, a Benchmark designed to comprehensively evaluate the capabilities of MLLMs on spatio-temporal Omnidirectional Video understanding. OmniV-Bench contains 4,123 high-quality ODVs collected from diverse scenes, along with 8,923 human-annotated question-answer pairs spanning 11 fine-grained tasks across three categories, i.e., general, spatial, and spatio-temporal understanding. Extensive benchmark experiments reveal that the spatio-temporal complexity of ODVs poses significant challenges for current MLLMs. To address these limitations, we further propose OmniV-Harness, an agentic harness that enhances ODV understanding through viewpoint-guided evidence initialization and task-relevant evidence acquisition with adaptive tool use. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate that OmniV-Harness consistently improves immersive spatio-temporal reasoning ability of MLLMs. Both OmniV-Bench and OmniV-Harness will be released upon publication.


### arXiv 2611.31016 - CenShaper: Shaping Proxy Traffic for Censorship Circumvention

- categories: cs.CR
- published: 2026-11-23T17:00:54Z
- authors: Shabbir Hussain, Aaron Ortwein, Roya Ensafi
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31016

Modern commodity DPI systems are capable of analyzing packet sizes, directions, and timings to detect circumvention tools even when those tools use sophisticated obfuscation. The anti-censorship community has called for innovation in traffic shaping and built an ecosystem that facilitates the adoption of new shaping techniques. However, a fundamental gap is halting progress: \textit{what} should circumvention traffic look like? Past experience with traffic shaping has underscored that effective shaping must produce traffic that looks like legitimate Internet traffic while limiting the ability of censors to identify multiple proxies through shared fingerprints. In this paper, we use our access to large-scale real-world TLS traffic at a regional ISP to analyze approximately 12 million connections, finding that individual servers differ substantially from the broader TLS population and that traffic patterns vary across services. Combining these empirical observations with past experience with traffic shaping, we build CenShaper, which shapes each proxy's traffic to look like a selected TLS server. To reproduce these server-specific patterns, CenShaper trains a shared generative model using traffic from TLS connections to many servers and adapts it for each proxy using a small sample of recorded connections to its selected server. Our model evaluation shows that fingerprints learned from known proxies transfer poorly to proxies imitating previously unseen TLS services. In end-to-end testing, CenShaper reduces the detection rate of TLS-over-TLS fingerprinting from over 85\% to approximately 3\% at a 1\% false positive rate. Finally, we show how running CenShaper as a sidecar process simplifies integration with existing circumvention tools, making it a practical defense against increasingly powerful traffic analysis attacks.


### arXiv 2611.30750 - SchemaCaps: When a Compatible Tool Update Silently Widens an AI Agent's Authority

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CR
- published: 2026-11-23T14:24:36Z
- authors: Yulliwas Ameur, Samia Bouzefrane, Soumya Banerjee
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30750

The tools users approve for their AI agents keep changing their JSON input schemas. Of 8 surveyed MCP clients and scanners, none is observed or documented to re-approve a tool whose input schema widens (two not determinable). We define non-amplification over the coordinates visible in the schema (declared arguments and undeclared fields, on effective values) and a re-binding rule decided by subschema inclusion, trusting no field labels by default and conservative up to its checker and tested case analysis. Evidence: a Coq proof of composition; 48 regression tests; an exact-reference benchmark, where the reviewed version of the rule silently re-bound 27 amplifying changes, now fixed; and a pilot on 604 releases of 39 public MCP servers. There, 412 of 580 compatible schema changes widen some coordinate, yet compatibility re-binds them silently. The authors' adjudicated double annotation finds at least 76 that add or widen an authority-bearing argument (66 of the 84 a name-based labeller flags, plus 10 it misses). On existing tools, the rule asks 645 re-approvals, against 1,197 for pinning the input schema (2,004 with descriptions) and 617 for compatibility. Behaviour change behind an identical schema remains out of reach.


### arXiv 2611.30745 - Intent-Conditioned Argument Mediation for Tool-Using LLM Agents: Blocking Trusted-but-Unauthorized Arguments

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CR
- published: 2026-11-23T14:22:21Z
- authors: Yulliwas Ameur, Miqeas Pedro Mendes, Samia Bouzefrane
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30745

LLM agents turn a user's request and retrieved content into tool calls, and a call can reach the wrong recipient, endpoint, file or participant. Argument-level provenance blocks such values when they come from untrusted content, but accepts a trusted value that the user never requested. IntentFlow, a mediation layer between an unmodified planner and its tools, checks each authority-bearing argument against an intent contract and a provenance policy. On a synthetic benchmark of 200 attacks (oracle intent and injection labels, declared roles), attack success falls from 50.5% with provenance alone to 12.0% [8.2-17.2%]; every remaining success alters a content or payload field. IntentFlow wrongly blocks none of 200 paired benign tasks and 4 of 50 hard ones. On 50 trusted-but-unauthorized attacks, provenance-only mediation executes all 50 and IntentFlow none; a CaMeL-style check also blocks all 50 but rejects 16 of 50 benign tasks. On an offline AgentDojo-derived replay, IntentFlow blocks all 20 attacks at 75% benign success, as does a CaMeL-style check; a Progent-style allowlist blocks 19 and keeps all benign tasks. With one live planner and the same oracles, the mediator blocked all 17 corrupted calls proposed in 110 attacks.


### arXiv 2611.30559 - Learning What to Change: State-Verified Reinforcement Learning for Educational Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-23T12:04:31Z
- authors: Shengyao Wang, Siyuan Su, Siyu Song, Mengsong Wu, Yiming Luo, Wenbo Wu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30559

Persistent tutoring agents do more than answer questions: they manage course files, practice records, and learner profiles that govern future lessons. In such stateful settings, knowing what to teach does not guarantee knowing what to change. We identify a pervasive failure mode, content-state decoupling, where agents deliver pedagogically sound responses while silently corrupting workspace state through unwarranted updates, missed prerequisites, or unintended side effects. Text-level judges overlook these state errors, whereas isolated tool-call checks break down because whether a write action is valid depends jointly on the learner request, prior state, and execution order. We propose State-Verified Reinforcement Learning, which supervises online policy optimization directly through verifiable workspace transitions. Within isolated sandboxes that snapshot state around every tool call, a deterministic verifier audits tool effects, workflow ordering, and native auto-sync side effects, pairing graded partial credit with a hard gate that zeroes episode reward upon any critical violation. Whenever no critical violation occurs, an evidence-grounded judge scores actual saved artifacts rather than agent claims. We pair 1,000 verifiable training instances with an independently authored, human-reviewed evaluation benchmark of 60 tasks spanning 11 educational categories and 16 native tools. After online GRPO training, Qwen3.8-27B raises category-weighted strict success on our benchmark from 51.47% to 80.74% (+29.27 points), cutting content-pass-only execution failures from 45.6% to 13.7% and required state-check failures from 63 to 1 across 180 trials. The resulting policy outperforms seven reference baselines by over 20 points while reducing output tokens by 17-39% across seven of eight external benchmarks without regressing on general reasoning or tool use.


### arXiv 2611.30399 - Cost-Sensitive Reversibility of Non-Deterministic Actions

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-23T10:02:56Z
- authors: Giuseppe Canonaco, Alberto Pozanco, Daniel Borrajo
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30399

Determining action reversibility requires checking whether the effects of an action can be undone. Recent work has extended reversibility to fully observable non-deterministic (FOND) planning. It distinguishes weak reversibility, where reversal may succeed, from strong-cyclic reversibility, where reversal is guaranteed under fairness. These notions are qualitative: they say whether reversal is possible, but not how costly it is. We introduce cost-sensitive reversibility for non-deterministic actions. Given positive action costs, we define weak and strong reversal budgets, $k$-bounded reversibility, and a distinction between finite strong reversal and strong-cyclic reversal with unbounded worst-case cost. We relate the resulting notions in a reversibility lattice, prove cost-aware policy-merging results, and show how to compute restricted weak and strong universal uniform reversal budgets using cost-optimal classical planning and worst-case-optimal FOND search, respectively. We also give an admissible lower-bound test for rejecting $k$-bounded reversibility. These notions support finer-grained safety decisions: a small finite strong budget bounds recovery cost, whereas a small weak budget certifies only a cheap possible recovery path. Actions without an acceptable strong bound may therefore warrant human approval, as in tool-using agent systems.


### arXiv 2611.30230 - Composing Independently Distilled Memory and Retrieval Policies for Long-Term Agents

- categories: cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-23T08:01:29Z
- authors: Grigorii Davydenko, Artem Sakhno, Igor Pavlov, Julia Belikova, Andrey Savchenko, Maksim Makarenko
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30230

Long-lived Web assistants require effective memory and retrieval mechanisms to access information from past user interactions. While these capabilities can be learned through specialized policies, it remains unclear whether specialists trained independently in different environments can be effectively composed without joint optimization. This paper investigates compositional transfer through policy distillation. Memory-writing and sequential retrieval policies are independently distilled from frontier-model action trajectories into 3B models and combined with a fixed answer generator without downstream training. On a LongMemEval-based benchmark, the distilled composition achieves 73.1% accuracy, compared with 36.2% for a composition of existing RL-trained specialists, and also outperforms the evaluated RL-trained compositions on PersonaMem-v2. Component ablations reveal that improvements in individual policies depend on their composition, highlighting the importance of cross-specialist compatibility. Further task-specific adaptation improves accuracy to 75.2% on LongMemEval through RL and 48.9% on PersonaMem-v2 through supervised distillation. These findings demonstrate the effectiveness of independent specialist distillation for modular multi-agent systems. Source code and datasets of teacher-generated memory-editing and retrieval trajectories are released to facilitate reproducibility and further research.


### arXiv 2611.30200 - Adopting AI Technologies without Traps: A Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning Framework

- categories: cs.AI, econ.TH
- published: 2026-11-23T07:41:07Z
- authors: Jitong Yang, Avinash Reddy Mudireddy, Ziqing Lu, Huan Cai, Weiyu Xu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30200

AI technologies and AI agents play increasingly important roles in human society, including in education, economics, and research. Adopting AI technologies can bring immediate, and sometimes tremendous, rewards such as through productivity boost, but they can also bring negative effects including but not limited to losses of job and training opportunities, and cognitive capability declines. Thus, it is important to investigate the optimal ways of adopting AI technologies without different traps. In this paper, we develop a general multi-agent Markov decision process (MDP) based reinforcement learning (RL or MARL) modeling framework for determining and analyzing optimal adoption of AI technologies. This framework can model the long-term dynamic effects of AI adoption, and lead to finding the optimal AI adoption strategies. In our modeling framework, the negative effects of AI adoption are modeled by undesirable state transitions or negative rewards in the MDPs. The proposed multi-agent RL MDPs incorporate both human agents and AI agents. The key idea of our models is that incorporating AI technologies may not only increase the immediate rewards for AI agents but also alter the states of the involved human and AI agents. In particular, we apply this framework to analyzing AI offloading leading to skill erosion, to analyzing optimal human resource structures for a firm adopting AI, and to analyzing the economic equilibria in the trading/employment among human and AI agents. Since the multi-agent MDP based RL technique is from control, machine learning and AI, this research is essentially using an AI technique to determine the optimal ways of adopting or co-existing with AI without traps.


### arXiv 2611.30193 - Visual-RFT++: Extending Visual Reinforcement Fine-Tuning to Multimodal Tool Use

- categories: cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-23T07:36:15Z
- authors: Ziyu Liu, Zeyi Sun, Xiaoyi Dong, Yuhang Cao, Haodong Duan, Dahua Lin
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30193

Reinforcement Fine-Tuning (RFT) has recently become a central paradigm for advancing Large Reasoning Models (LRMs), as demonstrated by systems such as OpenAI's o3 and the R1 series. Despite significant progress in LLMs, how to leverage RFT to unify visual perception, step-by-step reasoning, and agentic tool use into one coherent training paradigm remains an open challenge. In this work, we present Visual Reinforcement Fine-Tuning++ (Visual-RFT++), a unified framework that extends RFT to both visual perception tasks and multimodal agentic workflows, enabling LVLMs to reason with images, act upon images, and evaluate their own outputs using verifiable reward. With Visual-RFT++, open-source LVLMs not only show significant gains on diverse visual perception tasks, but also acquire agentic abilities such as web searching for real-time information and code-based image manipulation (e.g., cropping, rotation, and analysis). Across visual perception tasks, including fine-grained classification, few-shot detection, reasoning grounding, and open-vocabulary detection, Visual-RFT++ delivers strong gains over SFT, including a +24.3 percentage points improvement in one-shot fine-grained classification and +21.0 percentage points on COCO's 4-shot setting and +15.4 percentage points on LVIS with around several hundred samples. To evaluate agentic behaviors, we introduce the Multimodal Agentic Tool Bench (MAT) with MAT-Search and MAT-Coding. Visual-RFT++ surpasses its baseline by +18.6 F1 / +13.0 EM percentage points on MAT-Coding and +10.3 F1 / +8.7 EM percentage points on MAT-Search. It also shows gains of 29.3 F1 and 25.9 EM percentage points on multi-hop QA benchmarks such as 2Wiki and HotpotQA, demonstrating strong cross-modal generalization. Visual-RFT++ introduces a data-efficient and reward-driven framework that strengthens both visual reasoning and agentic adaptability.


### arXiv 2611.30163 - ReVAgent: Multi-Agent Reasoning-Intensive Video Generation

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-23T07:11:19Z
- authors: Yehui Yang, Jing Fan, Jun Hu, Shangyao Chen, Jinhua Bian, Song Wang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30163

A central challenge in generating instructional videos for reasoning-intensive topics is to show how and why the state changes at each step. Even when an animation renders successfully, the depicted object relationships and state transitions can conflict with the explanation, making the reasoning difficult to follow. We present ReVAgent, a multi-agent framework that solves a problem, generates keyframes for its explanation, and synthesizes an instructional video. The agents share the execution record of a solution program, reuse intermediate results and constructions in the scenes, and check rendered keyframes to guide revisions to the solution or the scenes. We also introduce a multidimensional rubric for assessing whether generated videos present the problem clearly, organize the steps coherently, and use visuals to explain the reasoning. We calibrate a multimodal judge using human ratings and apply it to instructional videos for mathematics and algorithms. In the evaluated samples, ReVAgent presents multi-step reasoning coherently and uses diagrams to show how key operations lead to subsequent states.


### arXiv 2611.30090 - Post-Training Agents for Long-Document Reasoning

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-23T05:51:19Z
- authors: Sushant Mehta, Logan Ritchie, Edwin Chen
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30090

Professional documents are a long-context workload that retrieval-style benchmarks do not capture. In a ten-domain enterprise collection, a task's evidence is spread across a source document of median length 27 pages (mean 48.8, maximum 873); a third of tasks rest mainly on charts, drawings, and maps; and 70.9% require two or more reasoning hops.
