We post-train Kimi-K2.7, an open-weight foundation model, at its full context window on 1,465 expert-authored tasks with atomic rubrics from that collection, training rank-32 adapters with one round of privileged-context on-policy self-distillation followed by two rounds of rubric-reward reinforcement learning. We render each PDF as text for training and as extracted text plus selected page images at evaluation. The smoothed RL training reward rises from 0.37 in the first tenth of the run to 0.67 in the last. On the 100-task GDP.pdf holdout, served in a 262,144-token window, strict pass (all rubric criteria met) more than doubles after the second round, from 11.0% to 24.5%, with gains across input lengths up to 240K tokens. Mean tokens generated stay flat (9,803 to 9,733), so the gain does not come from longer outputs. GPT-5.6 Sol's leaderboard strict pass is 30.7% at a measured 3,422 tokens. On 220 out-of-domain GDPval tasks, where the model runs as a long-horizon tool-using agent, mean rubric reward rises from 0.571 to 0.746 after the third round and zero-credit tasks fall from 54 to 10, while messages per task fall 18%, from 70.9 to 58.2. In paired trajectories, the third round replaces a context-thrashing retry loop with one up-front check of all sources, and verification extends from checking that files exist to checking what they contain. We argue that long-context evaluation of agents should report accuracy jointly with tokens and turns, and that datasets of long documents should ship text and image renderings alongside each PDF.


### arXiv 2611.30043 - Domain advantage in two-dimensional quantum-device design with multi-modal agentic harness

- categories: cond-mat.mes-hall, cs.SE, quant-ph
- published: 2026-11-23T04:49:41Z
- authors: Shenhao Miao, Yizhou Li, Liqiao Xia, Ruihao Liu, Dingke Wang, Pablo Jarillo-Herrero
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30043

High-quality two-dimensional quantum devices are commonly fabricated from mechanically exfoliated crystals, whose irregular shapes and placement require a lithographic layout tailored to each specimen. Drawing these layouts remains largely manual. We introduce qlaybench, a benchmark that tests whether language-model agents can turn microscope images and experimental requirements into device layouts that fit the specimen. Agents with conventional tools often construct devices when material outlines are supplied, but struggle to identify those outlines from images. Modelled on the human workflow, qlaybot, a multimodal agentic harness, connects image interpretation to the tools used to align images, draw device features and inspect the resulting layout. We evaluate each layout with checks drawn from practical design experience, asking whether it fits the specimen and connects its intended components. With greater computational effort, an agent using qlaybot passes 81.5% of the benchmark tasks, compared with 24.1% when it uses conventional tools. This domain advantage highlights the importance of connecting scientific interpretation of a specimen to geometric design and provides a route towards reducing manual effort in two-dimensional quantum-device fabrication.


### arXiv 2611.30042 - SWAM: State-aware World Action Model for Multi-Agent 3D Environments

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-23T04:49:25Z
- authors: Jenna Kang, Bingxuan Li, Colin Groth, Anatolii Evdokimov, Sai Zhang, Qi Sun
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30042

Interactive world models generate virtual environments autoregressively from player actions and visual history. However, visual history alone cannot reliably capture the evolving states of independently acting non-player characters (NPCs) in real games, particularly when they are outside the camera view. Recent multi-agent world models address this challenge through joint multi-view generation or persistent shared state, but often rely on external observations, like maps or scene geometry, or predefined state transitions. We present SWAM, a State-aware World Action Model that separates agent-state evolution from autoregressive visual generation in interactive 3D environments. A lightweight state model propagates character dynamics from actions without consuming generated frames, combining motion priors with learned scene-dependent corrections. The predicted states then guide an autoregressive diffusion transformer alongside visual history through a novel conditioning scheme. Rather than requiring an explicit scene geometry map or visibility tests, the conditioning indicates where characters may appear, allowing the diffusion model to learn their appearance and occlusion. This design enables NPCs to evolve independently of visual generation while maintaining consistent observations across visibility changes, interactions, and respawns. Evaluations in a custom Godot-based first-person shooter game demonstrate improvements in visual fidelity, NPC consistency, and long-horizon stability over existing approaches.


### arXiv 2611.29999 - Antiphony: A Dual-Process Agent for Deepfake Video Detection with Real-Video Calibration

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.CR
- published: 2026-11-23T04:03:18Z
- authors: Xinyu Yan, Yurong Hao, Zherui Li, Yebo Wu, Tiantong Wu, Jiaming Zhang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29999

Reliable video deepfake detection requires generalization to unseen generators while limiting false alarms on authentic footage. Although existing detectors capture diverse visual cues, they may produce overconfident errors under generator shift, limiting the reliability of confidence-based fusion. Threshold calibration is further complicated by shifts in authentic capture domains and the lack of future-generator examples. To address these challenges, we present Antiphony, a framework that combines calibrated fusion of four visual experts with selective investigation. Authentic videos serve as a shared reference for score fusion and threshold calibration, enabling threshold updates without target deepfakes. For this expert-fusion stage, we establish finite-sample false-alarm control when the scoring procedure is fixed independently of calibration and authentic samples are exchangeable. To refine decisions near the calibrated threshold, a tool-using multimodal investigator examines these videos and contributes an additional fusion score. We train the investigator on generator-held-out predictions and counterfactual expert outputs, to improve robustness to unreliable expert evidence. Across six benchmark protocols, Antiphony achieves a mean benchmark score of 88.3%, exceeding the strongest evaluated MLLM and conventional detector baselines by 14.7 and 11.5 points, respectively. It also outperforms ARGUS by 20.2 points on average and by 3.6 balanced-accuracy points on FaceVid's 20 unseen generators.


### arXiv 2611.29977 - Conartist: Adaptive Agentic Red-Teaming of Computer-Using Agents against Multi-Step Prompt Injection

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-23T03:41:22Z
- authors: Jiaqi Rong, Yunchen Lu, Lihai Nie, Yan Jia, Tong Li, Zheli Liu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29977

Harnesses secure Computer-Using Agents by enforcing security constraints and defending against prompt injection attacks. However, we find that two core security mechanisms of agent harnesses remain vulnerable. First, consistency checking prevents agents from deviating from legitimate user objectives, while attackers can bypass it by embedding malicious actions that appear logically consistent with legitimate task execution. Second, risk auditing proactively examines high-risk operations. Yet, the auditing process itself would introduce additional exploitable actions that violate security boundaries. To evaluate above defects we found, we propose Conartist, a cascaded adaptive prompt injection framework comprising two progressive stages. First, Conartist employs a goal-pollution strategy to establish deceptive causal dependencies between legitimate user objectives and malicious operations, progressively embedding attack steps into legitimate task workflows. However, these task-coupled attacks remain susceptible to risk auditing. To address this limitation, Conartist further analyzes security-auditing feedback from controlled attack exploration to identify exploitable auditing patterns. Conartist then adaptively refines its deception strategies to either avoid triggering risk auditing or induce additional high-risk operations. Extensive experiments across five backbone models demonstrate the effectiveness of Conartist, achieving attack success rates of 32.12% on 576-instance tool use benchmark and 66.67% in the Reddit GUI benchmark, outperforming the SOTA attack by 14.41 and 29.17, respectively. By analyzing one of the most popular Agent, Openclaw, we furthermore concluded seven realistic exploitable attack patterns, which reveals that security mechanisms designed to prevent malicious actions can themselves be manipulated to facilitate attacks.


### arXiv 2611.29942 - LLM-Assisted Software Migration: from Module-Level Transformation to Repository-Level Integration

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-23T03:02:55Z
- authors: Yanan Zhang, Shengqi Liu, Tao Zhang, Yidi Liu, Alan Tang, Hailong Chen
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29942

Software migration adapts an existing implementation to a new language, version, API, framework, or platform while preserving its behavior. Large language models (LLMs) succeed on individual functions and modules, but this success does not readily carry over to entire repositories, particularly deployable applications. This survey reviews LLM-assisted migration from module-level transformation to repository-level integration, culminating in the delivery of complete applications. We analyze 96 sources---including 46 direct migration papers---across four perspectives: (1) supported software engineering activities, (2) model roles as editors or orchestrators, (3) orchestration workflows (agentless, single-agent, or multi-agent), and (4) enabling techniques including knowledge engineering, project context, environment feedback, and model adaptation. We then compare evaluation practices and benchmarks. Although 30 of the 46 studies target repositories, most acceptance evidence relies merely on build success, supplied tests, or static and model-based proxies. Moreover, compilation, visual similarity, test outcomes, and runtime behavior capture fundamentally distinct properties and cannot be conflated into a single success rate. We also identify critical gaps in requirements engineering support and in controlled comparisons of agent architectures. Finally, we discuss six open problems: application integration, platform differences, non-functional properties, generalization, attribution of gains, as well as cost and maintenance.


### arXiv 2611.29901 - Synapse: An HBM-Coordinated HBF Near-Memory Computing Architecture for Pipelined Agentic AI Execution

- categories: cs.AR
- published: 2026-11-23T01:57:33Z
- authors: Juhyeong Jin, Hyunjun Cho, Joo-Young Kim
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29901

During multi-agent video reasoning, model weights and per-agent key-value caches can exceed high-bandwidth memory (HBM) capacity. High-bandwidth flash (HBF) adds capacity, but agents still wait for GPU execution. Synapse, an HBM-coordinated near-memory computing architecture, overlaps HBF attention and expert execution with GPU computation across agents. It also reduces dispatch delays and unnecessary refreshes. Across three models, Synapse achieves 48.92-66.60% lower end-to-end latency, up to $2.98\times$ throughput, and up to 64.48% lower modeled energy per output token than GPU execution with passive HBF. Wear simulations project 46% longer HBF lifetime than block-wide refresh at equal workload rates.


### arXiv 2611.29804 - FORGETMENOT: Auditing Memory Driven Privacy Risks in Commercial AI Agents

- categories: cs.CR
- published: 2026-11-22T22:10:40Z
- authors: Ismat Jarin, Tu Le, Athina Markopoulou
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29804

Commercial AI agents increasingly incorporate long-term memory (both persistent and chat-history) to enable more accurate and personalized responses, raises significant privacy and policy concerns. Yet little is known about user privacy risks associated with memory behavior, in particular, about memory storage, disclosure, transparency and deletion control. To bridge this gap, we propose FORGETMENOT, an auditing framework for analyzing the privacy risks of long term memory in commercial AI agents. Using our framework, we collect around 3800 multi-turn sessions comprising around 6M tokens across five popular AI agents. Our results show that agents store a wide range of personal attributes, including demographics, health, and sensitive details. Furthermore, user visible memory reveals substantially less (around 20% on average) attributes than the agents store in practice, exposing a significant transparency gap. Our stealth memory injection (SMI) technique further increases the amount of personal and sensitive attribute stored in memory, raising it by 25% and 15% for Copilot and Claude, respectively. We further observed memory deletion control is unreliable, as all five agents exhibit non-zero post deletion leakage, including health and self-harm related attributes. Finally, we demonstrate the feasibility of memory disclosure to a 3rd-party email recipient as a proof-of-concept, as Gemini shows 31.9% leakage rate. Together, our findings reveal notable privacy risks in commercial agents memory. Finally, we discuss both design implications and regulatory recommendations to protect users memory privacy in commercial agents.


### arXiv 2611.29571 - Non unitary and nonlinear quantum generative large model design and fast large-scale training without back propagation

- categories: quant-ph, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-22T15:51:56Z
- authors: Tianze Zhu, Xiaofeng Jiang, Min Liao, Luying Zhang, Ruixi Liu, Sen Kuang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29571

Transformer-based large models require huge computational and storage costs. Quantum computation is expected to address that due to its potential to speed up classical algorithms exponentially. Key challenges of implementing quantum large models include the linear, unitary nature of qubit evolutions contrast with the non-unitary matrices and nonlinear functions in classical Transformers, as well as the difficulty of implementing gradient back-propagation on quantum hardware. Here we propose a fully quantum large model containing all the Transformer layers, implementing the non-unitary transformations through SVD-decomposition-based quantum circuits, and approximated nonlinear activation functions through pre-trained parameterized quantum circuits. We also propose a quantum circuit training algorithm based on multi-agent Reinforcement Learning, which can train large-scale parameterized quantum circuits without back-propagation. The dependency between the inference time complexity and the model's hidden dimension has been reduced from polynomial to logarithmic, showing an exponential advantage. The inference executions on the quantum hardware verify our model's 300-1000 times speed-up, and the measurements on the sequence-to-sequence tasks shows its learning ability. The training algorithm achieves only 25% complexity of the best SOTA quantum circuit training algorithms, especially this ratio decreases fast as the parameter scale increases. Our work not only proposes a quantum large model that can significantly reduce the computational cost, but also promotes the quantum computation towards general computing.


### arXiv 2611.29501 - MAWAM: Joint Imagination for Collaborative Robot Manipulation with a Multi-Agent World Action Model

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-22T14:16:55Z
- authors: Rongze Tang, Chenyu Zhou, Jianjie Fang, Ziming Liu, Zhaolu Wang, Ziyou Wang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29501

World action models (WAMs) excel at robot manipulation by imagining future observations and decoding actions from them, yet remain confined to a single embodied agent. In practice, manipulation often requires multiple robots to collaborate across disjoint workspaces, coupled timing, and heterogeneous embodiments. Such coordination demands both spatiotemporal alignment of future interactions and awareness of individual embodiment and role identities, whereas existing decentralized per-robot policies, flat centralized models, and multi-robot VLAs fail to unite both. To bridge this gap, we present MAWAM, a multi-agent world action model that jointly predicts the future observations of multiple embodiments and decodes each agent's actions from them for collaborative manipulation. Through an agent-axis curriculum, MAWAM expands a single-agent WAM along an agent dimension to coordinate teams under shared weights. Within one unified causal sequence, joint self-attention spans all agents to spatiotemporally align their imagined futures, while hierarchical language, identity encodings, and embodiment masks bind each stream to its role, identity, and embodiment. We further formalize multi-agent-necessary manipulation along three axes (why, when, and who) and develop MA-Task Harness, a coding-agent pipeline where mutually constraining generators and evaluators co-evolve customizable simulation-data and evaluation packages. Using this pipeline, we build MA-Bench across all axes with seven tasks, pairing joint success rate with role and collaboration scores for fine-grained analysis. On MA-Bench, MAWAM reaches 82.7% mean joint success, 30.7% above the strongest baseline, and achieves real-time closed-loop collaboration on a real heterogeneous robot team.


### arXiv 2611.29494 - CoHABIT: Complementary Evaluation of Furniture Layouts through Spatial Rules and Household Routines

- categories: cs.LG, cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-22T14:02:51Z
- authors: Ewsey Obzherin, Samira Atlangirieva, Anastasia Antsiferova, Valentin Khrulkov, Art Prosvetov, Andrey Kuznetsov
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29494

We introduce CoHABIT, a framework for evaluating furniture layouts through complementary spatial rules and household routines. Static assessment captures geometric validity, ergonomic access, functional organization, and configurable design preferences, including Feng Shui. Dynamic assessment uses learned resident policies to simulate everyday activities, resource sharing, coordination, and individual burdens in multi-agent households. Controlled comparisons show how spatial criteria and household behavior reveal distinct aspects of layout quality, while resident profiles and scheduling influence furnishing rankings. Independent verification of household needs grounds dynamic comparisons in observed outcomes, enabling interpretable assessments of how furnished environments serve different households.


### arXiv 2611.29465 - Voice Agents: From Auditory Intelligence to Auditory Agency

- categories: eess.AS
- published: 2026-11-22T13:09:59Z
- authors: Kele Xu, Qisheng Xu, Xinwang Liu, Huaimin Wang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29465

Voice systems increasingly combine streaming speech, persistent state, reasoning, tool use, and embodied control. Once these functions share a runtime, spoken input can change digital or physical state, and recognition or response quality alone no longer characterizes the system. This article studies the interfaces through which spoken evidence becomes consequential. \emph{Auditory intelligence} denotes the capabilities required to perceive, understand, coordinate, and generate spoken interaction; \emph{auditory agency} denotes the causal path from that interaction to stateful decisions and observable consequences. The \emph{commitment boundary} marks where revisable auditory evidence becomes persistent state, a plan, or an executable request. \emph{Agency closure} then follows an authorized action through observation, verification, recovery or compensation, and communication of the outcome. Evidence from full-duplex interaction, reasoning, memory, tools, embodiment, and human control shows a consistent systems-level mismatch: integration is advancing faster than end-to-end observation of what the integrated system actually does. The resulting design problem is to preserve a trace from spoken evidence to consequence while keeping commitment, execution, and verification attributable.


### arXiv 2611.29457 - MAS-BIT: A Multi-Agent System for End-to-End BraIn-to-Text Decoding

- categories: q-bio.NC, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-22T12:55:45Z
- authors: Xinjie Zhang, Linyang He, Yizi Zhang, Yuesheng Ma, Nima Mesgarani
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29457

Speech brain-computer interfaces aim to restore communication by translating neural activity into text, yet end-to-end decoders have historically trailed cascaded systems in accuracy. We introduce a Multi-Agent System for BraIn-to-Text (MAS-BIT), the first multi-agent framework for end-to-end brain-to-text decoding from intracortical recordings. MAS-BIT coordinates three specialized agents: a neural agent that interprets continuous neural representations, a phonetic agent that decodes soft phoneme posteriors, and an arbitration agent that integrates their complementary evidence with direct access to neural representations to generate the final transcription. The expert agents communicate with the arbiter through token-embedding-based latent communication, constructing continuous messages as probability-weighted combinations of token embeddings. These messages retain alternative token hypotheses and provide a differentiable interface through which sentence-level supervision jointly trains the neural encoders and specialized agents across participants. MAS-BIT achieves state-of-the-art end-to-end decoding performance on the Brain-to-Text '24 and '25 benchmarks, with word error rates of 6.929% on '24 and 3.606% on the '25 public test set (3.892% on the private test set). These results surpass the published BIT cascade on the '25 public set and narrow the gap on '24, without ensembling independently trained models or using external language-model rescoring.


### arXiv 2611.29367 - BenchMint: From Real-World Tasks to Executable Benchmarks

- categories: cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-22T10:13:31Z
- authors: Zining Huang, Zuo Wang, Mengyao Zhang, Yilan Piao, Zhe Wang, Shuohan Wang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29367

Large language models (LLMs) need evaluations of everyday reasoning and tool use, such as planning itineraries under user constraints and grounding numerical answers in evidence. We introduce BenchMint, an automated benchmark-construction workflow, and Daily-Life, its collection of 100 selected tasks across 11 real-world domains. BenchMint jointly constructs tasks and scoring criteria from the same source documents. Language-model judges assess possible answers against these criteria using tool traces and private evidence. We report 26 model/harness configurations, including GPT, Claude and Gemini families; 17 have complete domain profiles. These are descriptive configuration results, not a controlled model ranking. In one selected travel task, we corrected the reference total from 320 to 380 minutes while keeping both answers unchanged. The incorrect answer's arithmetic verdict switched from pass to fail; the correct answer's switched from fail to pass. With one ballot per cell, this exposes a reference error without establishing repeatability or collection-wide validity. Incomplete construction comparisons establish no general advantage of joint authoring or feedback-guided revision.


### arXiv 2611.29321 - Multi-Agent Coordinated Method-Level Refactoring of Rust Code

- categories: cs.SE
- published: 2026-11-22T08:34:05Z
- authors: Yijian Zheng, Yi Zhang, Juyuan Zhang, Di Cui
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29321

As Rust codebases evolve, developers refactor code to reduce duplication, simplify complex methods, and keep related functionality together. Developers can make these changes manually or use IDE assists for supported operations, then check the resulting code with compiler diagnostics and tests. Method-level refactoring in Rust must preserve observable behavior while accounting for ownership, borrowing, and lifetimes. We present a large language model (LLM)-based workflow for extracting, inlining, and moving methods in Rust. The workflow combines candidate generation, configurable specialist review,and container-based validation. On six tasks from three open-source Rust projects, three end-to-end configurations passed all task checks on their first attempt, using 6, 13, and 31 model calls. To isolate the effect of review, we then gave four configurations the same six reference patches and six deliberately behavior-changing variants. A designated specialist rejected all six behavior-changing patches before container validation and let all six reference patches proceed, reducing Docker invocations from 24 to 12 relative to direct validation. The fixed specialist panel also caught all six faults early and rejected two reference patches over unmeasured performance concerns. On this workload, specialist review reduced execution work, while its effect on total time depended on review overhead and which patches reached validation.


### arXiv 2611.29294 - RSLaA: Self-Reconfiguring Roadside LiDARs as Agents for 3D Traffic Perception

- categories: cs.RO, cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-22T07:30:06Z
- authors: Yang Ma, Yichen Sun, Yubing Zheng, Zhongxiang Feng, Zeyang Cheng, Said M. Easa
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29294

Cooperative perception with roadside LiDARs (RSLs) extends the sensing range of autonomous vehicles and resolves onboard-sensor occlusions, particularly at curves and intersections. Because infrastructure deployment is costly, existing work optimizes RSL configurations once, against road geometry and aggregated traffic statistics, and treats them as fixed thereafter. Traffic flow, however, is inherently dynamic, and a placement optimal on average can be far from optimal at any given moment. We introduce RSLaA (Roadside LiDARs as Agents), a framework in which each RSL adaptively adjusts its pose to the evolving traffic scene over decision-time windows. Since ground truth is unavailable at decision time, the framework centers on surrogate metrics computed solely from a probabilistic occupancy grid of historical observations, which predicts the next-window perception performance of candidate poses. We construct a co-simulation benchmark pairing five candidate surrogates with detector-agnostic ground-truth perception labels at every decision window, identifying the multi-view coverage metric J_mv as the most reliable predictor. Upon this validated surrogate we build two adaptive configuration methods, a receding-horizon (MPC) optimizer and a lightweight MAPPO-based multi-agent reinforcement learning scheme with centralized training and decentralized execution. Across 10 sites and 89 six-hour traffic scenarios (890 evaluation runs), adaptive configuration consistently improves the composite perception score over static deployment. Our results indicate that RSLs could be benefitted from being treated as agents.


### arXiv 2611.29262 - Neural Agent Compiler: Learning to Schedule Soft Dependencies

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-22T06:03:46Z
- authors: Bo Yan, Peng Wang, Zihan Chen, Ziwen Kan, Zhen Tan, Huiyuan Chen
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29262

Large language model agents accomplish complex tasks by coordinating multiple reasoning steps and tool operations, which form dependencies among their operations. Existing orchestration methods primarily consider hard dependencies that determine when an operation can legally begin, but overlook soft dependencies, i.e., information that is not required for execution but may improve an already executable operation. We identify soft-dependency scheduling as an important problem in agent execution. To this end, we introduce Neural Agent Compiler (NAC), a learned scheduler that predicts the task-quality and resource consequences of three execution modes and selects the highest-utility legal mode at a scheduling boundary. NAC represents the executable continuation, pending information event, and resource state in a shared memory, and uses a utility function to adapt the consequence predictions to different resource preferences. Across AppWorld, WebShop, and $τ^2$-bench, NAC achieves 276/417 AppWorld task completions, a 74.69 WebShop Task Score, and 59/100 $τ^2$-bench successes. Additional studies show that the learned scheduler reduces execution work, establishing soft-dependency scheduling as a promising solution for orchestrating tool-using agents.


### arXiv 2611.29255 - NoeVera: Can Knowledge Guide Experiments and Experiments Refine Knowledge?

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.DC, cs.IR, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-22T05:49:43Z
- authors: Pinaki Prasad Guha Neogi, Spyros Mastorakis, Ankur Mallick, Saravan Rajmohan, Victor Rühle
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29255

With the growing demand for large language model (LLM) services, optimizing LLM serving systems is becoming increasingly complex, and the bottleneck is no longer predicting how a given configuration will perform. The harder problem is deciding what to investigate: which mechanisms matter for a workload, which configurations are worth testing, and what evidence a decision still needs. In this work, we present NoeVera, an agentic Second Brain that goes beyond just one-shot retrieval by turning papers, code, benchmarks, and experimental results into a persistent, provenance-preserving knowledge base with three planes: source evidence, reusable synthesis, and agent memory. Decision-aware retrieval assembles compact evidence packs, role-specific agents reason over them and invoke a simulator (Vidur or GAUSS) whenever quantitative support is needed, and every finding is written back as a scoped evidence record, so knowledge guides experiments and experiments refine knowledge. In configuration search over four serving scenarios, NoeVera comes within 5% of the optimum in a median of 6-7 simulator calls, versus 9 for Bayesian optimization and 15 for a knowledge-free agent with the same tools; on A100 GPUs, its representative configurations deliver ~1.30x vLLM-default throughput and meet every scenario's latency objective. When reused, provenance-preserving evidence records over-generalize on only 8% of applicability traps, versus 28% for a general-purpose agent memory. On the held-out 48-task split of a 72-task benchmark, NoeVera scores 29% higher than an equally equipped agent and 36% higher than its single-call retrieval counterpart (0.80 vs. 0.62 and 0.59), and even when baselines receive NoeVera's own prompts, a statistically significant part of its evidence-evolution advantage remains.


### arXiv 2611.29248 - AgenticMM: A Material-Centered Multi-Agent Architecture for Scalable First-Principles Materials Modeling

- categories: cond-mat.mtrl-sci
- published: 2026-11-22T05:40:25Z
- authors: Zhenkun Yuan, Geoffroy Hautier
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29248

Multi-agent frameworks are attracting increasing attention as a means of autonomously driving complex, long-running workflows in first-principles materials modeling. However, such frameworks commonly organize AI agents by computational task or scientific role. When scaled across many materials, this organization can require repeated transfers of material-specific context and complicate persistent execution and resource coordination. Here, we introduce AgenticMM, a material-centered multi-agent architecture in which each material has a persistent agent that owns its complete workflow and retains its scientific context, while a persistent coordinator allocates shared resources, mediates communication, and tracks campaign progress. AgenticMM is designed to incorporate reusable, prompt-based skills as modular definitions of these workflows. Using DefectSkills prompts, we demonstrate the feasibility of AgenticMM through a multistage point-defect campaign in which material-specific agents concurrently perform host electronic-structure, phase-stability, and intrinsic-defect calculations for five chemically diverse semiconductors.


### arXiv 2611.29225 - IteraSong: LLM-Guided Iterative Symbolic Music Creation in a Web DAW

- categories: cs.SD, cs.CL, cs.SC
- published: 2026-11-22T04:57:00Z
- authors: Di Lu, Xinran Zhang, Yan Gao, Yuan Zhang, Xiaoqing Wang, Qingwen Zhou
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29225

Music creation is inherently iterative, yet many symbolic generation systems still center on producing or regenerating isolated outputs. We present IteraSong, an interactive web-based Digital Audio Workstation (DAW) for LLM-guided iterative symbolic music creation. Unlike one-shot generation systems, IteraSong supports a generate-audition-edit-refine loop over a persistent multi-track project. Users initialize a fully labeled pop draft, then progressively refine it through natural-language instructions. The LLM maps requests to a hierarchical action space of editing primitives exposed via Model Context Protocol (MCP) tools, spanning global, track, section, and bar/note scopes. The demo enables attendees to edit multi-track compositions in natural language, apply deterministic and generative edits, and undo/redo changes in real time.


### arXiv 2611.29195 - FunGrasp-Zero: Field-Conditioned Graph Attention Policy for Zero-Shot Cross-Embodiment Dexterous Functional Grasping

- categories: cs.RO
- published: 2026-11-22T03:39:52Z
- authors: Yanhan Lin, Yuliang Wu, Yihao Guo, Wei-Jin Huang, Jianwen Li, Jiawei Tang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29195

Dexterous functional grasping is critical for robotic tool use, yet current dexterous hands exhibit substantial morphological diversity. This hardware heterogeneity makes cross-embodiment functional grasping a fundamental but largely unexplored problem. Existing functional grasping methods use retargeted hand poses as guidance, tying task representation to a specific embodiment and failing to explicitly capture object-centric functional contact intent, limiting generalization across hands. To address this, we propose FunGrasp-Zero, a reinforcement learning framework for zero-shot cross-embodiment functional grasping. Instead of relying on embodiment-specific joint configuration, FunGrasp-Zero captures functional intent via an object-centric, cross-embodiment-consistent contact map, adapted to heterogeneous hands through morphological mapping. We construct a hand-object potential field from mapped contact regions, yielding attractive and repulsive guidance vectors. A Field-Conditioned Graph Attention Network (FC-GAT) leverages these vectors to coordinate multi-finger motion across hands, while a potential field reward guides fingers toward target regions and penalizes non-target collisions to enforce functional alignment and stability. We further establish X-FunBench for cross-embodiment functional grasping, evaluating both physical grasp success and functional intent preservation across hands. Evaluated on X-FunBench, our unified policy, co-trained on four embodiments, achieves 86% average grasp success rate across five unseen hands on novel objects. Deployed on four unseen physical hands (Leap, Inspire, Sharpa, Wuji) for novel object grasping, the policy achieves 80% average success rate without fine-tuning. To our knowledge, FunGrasp-Zero is the first RL-based framework to achieve zero-shot cross-embodiment functional grasping across unseen hands in both simulation and the real world.


### arXiv 2611.29112 - Bridging Blind Spots With Words: Decentralized Multi-View Spatial Reasoning via Language-Constrained Communication

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.IR, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-22T00:23:01Z
- authors: Soumyabrata Chaudhuri, Joydeep Ghosh
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29112

As multimodal LLMs (MLLMs) get increasingly used as collaborative partners across embodied robotics, AR/VR, and multi-user systems, spatial reasoning is now often distributed across agents with partial environment visibility, necessitating spatial reasoning integration. Prior work in language-mediated spatial integration is however strictly limited to simplified two-agent scenarios. To address this gap, we first formalize the general problem of decentralized multi-view spatial reasoning, where N agents observe distinct viewpoints, exchange budget-constrained natural language messages, and collaborate to enable a listener to answer spatial queries. We then introduce a novel multi-view spatial benchmark to isolate queries that demand non-trivial multi-view integration. Through an extensive empirical study, we discover that the penalty of restricting decentralized spatial reasoning to natural language with limited budget is far smaller than suggested by prior two-agent literature. Nevertheless, we observe that substantial headroom still remains for multi-agent spatial reasoning in this setting, as evidenced by a significant gap between current centralized performance and an oracle best-of-K ceiling. Moreover, we show that reinforcement learning with credit assignment substantially bolsters the decentralized model, effectively closing the gap with the centralized baseline.


### arXiv 2611.29037 - Distributed Interactive Conformal Prediction for Multi-Agent Systems

- categories: cs.RO, eess.SY
- published: 2026-11-21T20:57:51Z
- authors: Daniel M. Cherenson, Dimitra Panagou
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29037

Multi-agent systems increasingly rely on learning-enabled components and distributed algorithms for perception, estimation, and control, introducing uncertainty that makes it challenging to satisfy mission requirements. Conformal prediction quantifies uncertainty with distribution-free guarantees, but existing methods do not address the challenging combination of dependence among robots, distributed calibration, and the distribution shift induced when calibrated uncertainty bounds modify the closed-loop system. We introduce Multi-Agent Interactive Conformal Prediction (MA-ICP), a distributed framework for computing robustness margins in heterogeneous systems to satisfy mission requirements with high probability. MA-ICP aggregates robot-level nonconformity scores, which measure errors in modeled or estimated quantities, into one mission-level score per robot class, allowing dependence among robots within each mission. Mission-level scores and quantiles are computed using neighbor-to-neighbor communication in a finite number of communication rounds. Updating the robustness margins changes robot trajectories and distributed algorithm outputs, so we use class-dependent score sensitivities to upper bound the shift in mission-level scores. We then establish calibration-conditional coverage guarantees per robot class for the updated margins. We demonstrate MA-ICP on cooperative payload control, formation control with obstacle avoidance, and distributed target tracking missions.


### arXiv 2611.29036 - Grounding Collaborative Agents in Group Cognition

- categories: cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-21T20:54:55Z
- authors: Zhaoxuan Tan, Mengting Wan, Jingbo Yang, Kwei-Herng Lai, Renyi Qu, Lorenzo Jaime Flores
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29036

Collaborative agents joining an ongoing team rely on shared context to connect multi-party interaction histories with downstream coordination decisions. Existing work typically focuses either on retrieving historical facts from conversation logs, which surfaces isolated evidence without capturing who knows or owns what, or on evaluating end-to-end interactions, which conflates preparation with execution and lacks direct supervision because a team's history has no single ground-truth summary. To bridge this gap, we formulate group briefing, the task of reconstructing task-relevant group cognition across participants' expressed beliefs, group dynamics, and grounding gaps, and introduce a four-domain benchmark of 1,351 requests and 9,975 verified reference points paired with a two-stage evaluation pipeline combining reference-point coverage and downstream collaboration simulation. Using this pipeline, we show that GroupBrief, a simple task-guided approach that iteratively probes the message history and synthesizes a structured brief without offline memory indexing or graph construction, outperforms ten retrieval and agent-memory baselines in overall coverage with gpt-5.4-mini, while benchmarking nine language models reveals that prospective friction remains harder to recover than past state across model scales. We further use reference-point coverage as a feedback signal for harness optimization, refining GroupBrief's retrieval and synthesis instructions while keeping model weights frozen. On held-out Test projects, GroupBrief (optimized) improves coverage by 2.54 percentage points for gpt-5.4-mini and 12.88 for gpt-5.6-luna, achieving 64.46% and 68.42% preference over no brief in downstream simulations. These results establish our benchmark and evaluation pipeline as a foundation for measuring and optimizing how collaborative agents ground in group cognition.


### arXiv 2611.29014 - Population-Based Post-Training for Large Language Models

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-21T19:50:24Z
- authors: Yixuan Even Xu, Aleksei Petrenko, Raja Giryes, Jackson Hamburger, Kevin Chen, Fei Fang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29014

Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is one of the most popular post-training paradigms for large language models. RLVR typically trains a single model under hyperparameters chosen before training begins, while researchers tune hyperparameters across consecutive runs. We introduce Population-Based Post-Training (PBPT), which trains several models in parallel and lets the training process do the tuning through population-based training. PBPT periodically evaluates models on a validation set. High-performers are duplicated and their hyperparameters perturbed to explore new configurations. Low-performers stop training and are replaced by these copies. On reasoning benchmarks (Enigmata, Nemotron) and interactive tool-use environments (Gaia2, AppWorld), PBPT produces stronger agents than compute-matched groups of independent RLVR learners. Furthermore, PBPT is able to recover from poor initial hyperparameters, automatically discovering better settings over the course of training.


### arXiv 2611.28911 - Ads for Agents: Measuring Ad-Serving MCP Servers

- categories: cs.CR
- published: 2026-11-21T16:38:08Z
- authors: Shahrooz Pouryousef, Sohini Chakraborty, Ali Montazer, Muoi Tran
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28911

AI agents call external tools through protocols such as the Model Context Protocol (MCP). Some MCP servers return paid advertisements (ads) into the model's context. The model then decides whether these ads and their disclosures reach the user. We trace these ads from their sources to the agent's tool results across public MCP servers and packages. We find the servers through their public traces in ad networks, package registries, certificate logs, hostnames, MCP directories, and GitHub configurations. We then query the ad-serving servers with read-only requests and inspect their ad links. Ads appear on only 0.57% of 101,363 open MCP endpoints and 0.04% of 122,656 local MCP packages. Although 173 ad networks target agents, operators insert the ads themselves on about half of the 226 ad-returning servers. Nearly all ad-serving packages likewise carry the operator's own affiliate identifier. While ads adapt to the topic and intent of the query, their disclosure stays fixed within each tool. Only 9.3% of the ads carry an explicit sponsored label. Most ad links also hide their destinations. Since operators insert most ads themselves and label them inconsistently, we propose a per-item ad annotation in MCP results. Clients can show this annotation to the user independently of the model's answer.


### arXiv 2611.28846 - EA-Bench: Measuring information and action efficiency under imperfect instructions

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-21T14:54:32Z
- authors: Xu Wan, Wenyue Xu, Shenjie Zhao
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28846

Personal agents help users complete everyday work across digital applications. Many standard benchmarks assume that users provide sufficient information to solve a task. However, agents often encounter incomplete, distracting, or inconsistent information in practice. To systematically study agent inefficiency under such imperfect information, we introduce Efficient Action (EA)-Bench, which evaluates both tool calls and user interactions in work-related tasks. To our knowledge, it is the first benchmark to jointly evaluate tool-call efficiency and user-interaction efficiency under controlled missing, redundant, and conflicting information. We construct 63 base-task instances adapted from existing work-related benchmarks. To guide perturbation design and quantify task requirements, we represent workflows as directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) and examine node-level dependencies to identify where information can be withheld, contradicted, or supplemented with distractors. We use a two-stage validation process combining AI-assisted checks with expert screening to select 232 conditions from 792 initial candidates. We organize these tasks into Easy and Hard tracks by task complexity. Experiments show that imperfect information can reduce accuracy and increase tool-use and user-interaction costs. More concerning, several models obtain apparently correct outcomes by guessing user-specific values without confirmation, revealing potential execution unreliability in current models under imperfect user information.


### arXiv 2611.28787 - DiscernBench: Evaluating Cooperative Discernment of LLM Agents in Institutional Settings

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-21T13:01:14Z
- authors: Disha Sheshanarayana, Tanishka Magar
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28787

The deployment of large language models in operational settings demands evaluation of judgment under institutional constraint. We introduce DiscernBench, a benchmark for evaluating cooperative discernment: the ability of an agent to determine whether to comply with, refuse, or escalate a request in an institutional setting. DiscernBench comprises scenarios derived from real-world incident reports across domains such as aviation, law enforcement, and emergency management. We evaluate eight LLMs across three decision-making configurations and analyze both final decisions and the intermediate judgments produced during multi-agent deliberation. Under disagreement, Final Responders exhibit model- and role-dependent aggregation patterns in which a lone specialist recommendation can disproportionately influence the collective decision, a phenomenon we term Minority-Signal Override. Role-specialized multi-agent deliberation raises average over-escalation from 3.79% under direct single-agent judgment to 28.27%, with the direction and magnitude of minority-signal influence varying substantially across models and specialist roles. These results reveal distinct collective decision behaviors that are not captured by final-answer accuracy alone. Trustworthy multi-agent systems therefore require evaluating not only individual specialist reasoning, but also how conflicting judgments are transformed into collective decisions. Code and data are available at https://github.com/Disha1001/DiscernBench.


### arXiv 2611.28710 - CoopOcc: Cooperative 3D Semantic Occupancy Perception via Multi-Agent and Cross-Modal Fusion

- categories: cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-21T10:11:11Z
- authors: Zengle Zhu, Zhen Li, Tianyi Huai, Xinhu Zheng, Liuqing Yang, Rongqing Zhang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28710

Semantic occupancy perception has emerged as a promising paradigm for jointly modeling 3D scene geometry and semantic information. However, this unified representation cannot compensate for missing information caused by occlusion and sensor coverage. Multi-agent cooperation alleviates this limitation, but information from different agents is not equally useful across spatial regions. Meanwhile, feature representations from each agent form the basis of reliable multi-agent fusion. Cross?modal fusion constructs such representations by integrating complementary semantic and geometric information. Despite this complementarity, jointly establishing reliable 3D correspondence across agents and preserving modality-specific structures remains challenging. To address these limitations, we propose CoopOcc, which performs cooperative 3D semantic occupancy perception through multi-agent and cross-modal fusion. CoopOcc uses ego?query cross-agent attention (EQCA) to selectively aggregate aligned features across agents, and anchor-based bidirectional feature interaction (ABFI) for cross-modal exchange at matched coordinates. We further construct SemanticVVSim, a simulation dataset, and establish a unified benchmark. For simulation-to-real transfer, we propose confidence-aware hierarchical occupancy and relational distillation (CHORD). CoopOcc performs best overall among ten evaluated methods on SemanticVVSim. On Occ3D-nuScenes, CHORD improves performance using knowledge from SemanticVVSim. The code is available at https://github.com/LOTEAT/CoopOcc.


### arXiv 2611.28673 - ViewAgent3D: A Training-Free Multi-View Pipeline for 3D Scene Understanding

- categories: cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-21T08:50:36Z
- authors: Sarosij Bose, Arindam Dutta, Konstantinos Karydis, Amit K. Roy Chowdhury
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28673

3D scene understanding tasks are often approached by building explicit 3D scene representations or by training specialized multi-modal models over point clouds, voxels, or scene graphs. We take a complementary view: for many queries, the main bottleneck is not the absence of a stronger 3D representation but the selection of a small subset of multi-view RGB views that actually makes the query answerable. We present a training-free, query-conditioned multi-view pipeline that performs diverse tasks using only 2D images and a set of tools used purely for inference. Our method first decomposes each query into a set of concepts that identify unique attributes that assist with reasoning. This drives a coarse-to-fine evidence selection process: a coarse retrieval selects and scores all views, a diversity-aware selector reduces redundancy, and a reranking stage helps shortlist views by target visibility or answerability. The resulting evidence is optionally grounded for queries that benefit from explicit localization, and a self-verification step helps refine the final response, especially for open-ended cases. Despite using no task-specific training and no explicit 3D representations at inference time, our approach provides approximately 10% gain on 3D VQA and 5% gain on dense captioning and Embodied QA while being applicable across different scenarios. We further propose a unique judge-based evaluation system, motivating a judge who takes into account many semantically correct or partially correct answers, thereby improving holistic scene understanding.


### arXiv 2611.28642 - Aegis: A Conversational Prototype for Lung Cancer Risk Information and Follow-up

- categories: cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-21T08:09:46Z
- authors: Bowen Han, Chengwei Cao, Runxin Zhang, Yuyang Jiang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28642

Collecting self-reported risk information and maintaining continuity between assessment, explanation, and follow-up are practical components of a lung cancer screening workflow. We present Aegis, a browser-based research prototype that combines a conversational questionnaire, rule-based risk categorization, downloadable reports, retrieval-augmented health information, and doctor-user communication. A session-oriented controller manages question progression and clarification, while a language model assists with answer validation. Structured responses are passed to a separate risk-analysis module, and reports are linked to user and session records through Firebase services. The project adopts a multi-agent design that separates information collection, validation, assessment, and report generation. Its documented verification scope comprises 45 functional test specifications covering account management, questionnaire interaction, reporting, doctor-side review, and consultation. The available evaluation describes workflow behavior qualitatively; it provides no per-case execution results, clinical cohort, predictive-performance measurements, or comparative experiments. We therefore present Aegis as an engineering prototype for organizing risk information and follow-up, with clinical validity and the benefits of its agent-based design remaining unestablished. The paper describes the implementation, distinguishes implemented workflows from incomplete extensions, and identifies the evidence required for reproducible and clinically meaningful evaluation.


### arXiv 2611.28617 - VeriLadder: A Multi-Agent Specification Refinement System for Tool-Verified Verilog Datasets

- categories: cs.AI, cs.SE
- published: 2026-11-21T07:09:02Z
- authors: Saeid Rajabi, Chengmo Yang, Satwik Patnaik
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28617

Training data is a bottleneck in AI, especially for large language models (LLMs) that write Verilog, because register-transfer-level (RTL) code is rarely published and existing datasets mostly check code for syntax, not instruction correctness. This paper presents VeriLadder, a five-level Verilog dataset, and the multi-agent framework that builds it, in which LLM agents write and revise specifications, and EDA tools (a synthesizer and a formal equivalence checker) decide what is accepted. Its levels follow an engineer's checks. Level 1 labels designs from two large Verilog corpora, namely RTLCoder and PyraNet, with synthesis results, failure causes, critical warnings and post-synthesis metrics. 28% of RTLCoder designs fail synthesis and are kept as negatives. Level 2 grades each instruction on four specification qualities, and low grades predict failed rebuilds. Level 3 rewrites each instruction into a refined specification and accepts it only if an LLM from another model family, reading only that specification, writes a circuit formally equivalent to the original. This raises the share of rebuildable designs from 26.1% to 78.2%, keeping every failed specification with its counterexample. Levels 4 and 5 add verified paraphrases and one-fact variants, hard negatives that differ only in one behavior from the verified design. VeriLadder labels the synthesizability of 716,627 designs and, from RTLCoder, provides 11,247 refined specifications, 32,393 paraphrases and 8,397 one-fact variants. Fine-tuning Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct on VeriLadder raises functional pass@1 from 31.7% to 50.0% on VerilogEval v2 and from 35.0% to 45.2% on RTLLM v2. The dataset and fine-tuned models are publicly available.


### arXiv 2611.28604 - MADRec: Reliable Multi-Agent Reasoning with Evidence-Grounded Debate for Recommendation

- categories: cs.IR
- published: 2026-11-21T06:31:16Z
- authors: Zunlong Liu, Weijie Feng, Jingzhi Li, Lei Zhu, Zhiyong Cheng, Meng Wang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28604

Large Language Models (LLMs) are transforming recommender systems from conventional preference matching toward reasoning-driven and explainable decision-making. Multi-agent collaboration further advances this paradigm by distributing recommendation reasoning across specialized agents. However, existing frameworks rarely assess the reliability of intermediate reasoning during collaboration, allowing erroneous judgments to propagate across agents and ultimately degrade recommendation quality. To address this issue, we propose Multi-Agent Debate-based Recommendation (MADRec), an evidence-grounded multi-agent framework that enhances the reliability of multi-agent recommendation reasoning. MADRec consists of three coordinated modules. First, the Multi-Agent Debate (MAD) module enables an Interaction Agent and a Semantic Agent to analyze interaction signals and content-level semantic signals from complementary perspectives, generating diverse recommendation judgments through structured debate. Second, the Evidence-Grounded Retrieval-Augmented Reasoning (EG-RAR) module grounds the Semantic Agent's reasoning in item-level evidence and refines candidate scores by assessing how well the evidence supports the corresponding semantic claims. Third, the Judge-based Ranking Calibration (JRC) module leverages debate-derived reliability cues to adaptively calibrate interaction-based and semantic ranking results for the final recommendation. Extensive experiments on three real-world datasets demonstrate that MADRec consistently outperforms competitive baselines and enhances the reliability of multi-agent recommendation reasoning.


### arXiv 2611.28564 - Tool-to-Self: Internalizing Visual Tool Capabilities via Scaffolded Reinforcement Learning

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-21T04:38:15Z
- authors: Jin Cui, Hao Dong, Xinyue Long, Boran Zhao, Pengju Ren
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28564

Vision-language models (VLMs) increasingly rely on external visual tools to access fine-grained visual evidence, but repeated tool execution incurs substantial inference overhead and deployment dependencies. Moreover, conventional tool-use RL can improve visual-task performance, yet the resulting tool gains are often unstable. Motivated by this, we propose Tool-to-Self (T2S), a tool-scaffolded reinforcement learning framework that turns external visual tools into temporary training scaffolds. T2S cold-starts tool-use ability and observation prediction from tool-repairable examples, then progressively anneals tool execution on a per-tool basis during RL. By combining answer-correctness and observation-consistency rewards with privileged on-policy self-distillation from actual tool observations, T2S effectively teaches the model both to reproduce tool outputs and to reason from them. Experiments across diverse visual perception and reasoning benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements over both explicit tool-use models and implicit zooming models while enabling tool-free inference with reduced latency.


### arXiv 2611.28558 - From Reward Engineering to Data-Centric Reinforcement Learning for Effective Agentic Visual Tool Use

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-21T04:24:24Z
- authors: Jin Cui, Xinyue Long, Jiepeng Zhou, Boran Zhao, Pengju Ren, Hao Dong
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28558

Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) increasingly use external visual tools to acquire evidence during reasoning, yet learning when to invoke these tools remains challenging. Existing tool-use reinforcement learning (RL) methods often rely on call-contingent rewards to encourage beneficial tool use, but such reward engineering can distort the task objective and lead to degenerate calling behaviors. We argue that effective tool-use learning requires identifying not merely which questions are difficult, but which failures are attributable to missing visual evidence and can therefore be corrected through tool observations. We propose AOVO, a data-centric framework that enables MLLMs to learn effective tool use through perception attribution and policy-adaptive data sampling (PADS), without introducing tool-specific rewards. It counterfactually probes the model's reasoning states to identify observation-sensitive questions, distinguishing them from reasoning-dominated ones. PADS then periodically estimates category-level observation effects from recent policy rollouts and dynamically reallocates the training distribution toward questions where visual observations provide informative learning signals, while optimizing solely for final-answer correctness. Experiments across diverse visual perception and multimodal reasoning benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements over competitive tool-use MLLMs, with causal analysis further validating the data-centric perspective for learning effective visual tool use.


### arXiv 2611.28473 - ImCDN: An Immersive Content Delivery Network for Cooperative Tile-level 360 Video Streaming

- categories: cs.NI
- published: 2026-11-21T01:47:50Z
- authors: Ning Wang, Xiaofan Liu, Beatriz Lorenzo, Sumudu Samarakoon
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28473

Delivering immersive 360 video over Content Distribution Networks (CDNs) demands ultra-high bitrates, yet each user's viewport covers only a small fraction of the full panorama. Therefore, conventional segment-level architectures waste scarce edge resources on unseen regions. This paper presents ImCDN, an immersive end-to-end tile-granularity CDN framework that integrates field-of-view (FoV)-driven cloud pre-distribution with hybrid segment-tile caching at edge servers, edge compute for on-demand tile transcoding, and an edge-to-edge relay mechanism for cooperative content sharing. We formulate the CDN optimization problem, comprising cache admission, compute scheduling, and serving-node selection, as a long-term utility maximization that balances cache hit-rate, edge sharing, end-to-end latency, backhaul traffic, energy consumption, viewport video quality, and cache replacement cost. We solve it via multi-agent proximal policy optimization (MAPPO) under centralized training and decentralized execution across edge servers. Simulations on a realistic topology with real 360 head-tracking traces show that ImCDN improves edge hit rate and reduces user waiting time, reduces origin traffic by approximately 18% compared to the non-cooperative edge variant, and outperforms segment-only CDN baselines at comparable immersive quality (e.g., 4K/6K) and standard caching heuristics.


### arXiv 2611.28454 - When a Permitted Action Completes a Forbidden Workflow: Designing Consequence-Oriented Approval for LLM Agents

- categories: cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-21T01:17:42Z
- authors: S M Asif Hossain, Ruksat Khan Shayoni, Md Kishor Morol, M F Mridha
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28454

Tool-using language-model agents increasingly ask people to approve consequential actions, yet an approval prompt shows only the next step. A request to send a file to an approved recipient can look harmless even when that file was assembled, several steps earlier, from data the user said must never leave. We call this a cumulative authorization failure and argue that it is an interaction design problem as much as a security problem. The evidence a reviewer needs is spread across the trajectory, and listing it in time order leaves the reviewer to reconstruct the causal chain. We present the Cumulative Consent Ledger (CCL), an approval interface built on four design principles that restate the user's intent, bind the review to the exact action, compose the cumulative consequence, and keep every claim checkable. A trusted monitor that tracks artifact lineage supplies the evidence. In a between-subjects study with 80 participants and 960 approval decisions, CCL was associated with fewer unsafe approvals than an evidence-matched chronological log (24.2% vs. 32.5%), higher comprehension, and faster decisions, although the adjusted difference in unsafe approvals remained uncertain (-7.9 points, 95% CrI [-19.7, 3.8]). In a system evaluation, the monitor detected 89.2% of 120 violating checkpoints and, used as an execution gate, reduced executed unsafe effects from 43.3% to 5.0% for one agent at a small cost to benign completion. We derive implications for designing consent around consequences rather than individual calls.


### arXiv 2611.28348 - SMAD: A Semantics-Guided Multi-Agent Framework for Unsupervised Time-Series Anomaly Detection

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-20T22:00:23Z
- authors: Qinghua Liu, Junpeng Wang, Mingzhi Hu, Menghai Pan, Shubham Jain, Yuzhong Chen
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28348

Time-series anomaly detection (TSAD) lacks a one-size-fits-all detector for heterogeneous data and anomaly types. Existing automation is limited by implicit objectives, potentially unreliable historical knowledge, or high computational costs. More fundamentally, it selects how to detect anomalies without explicitly defining what to detect. We propose SMAD, an LLM-based multi-agent framework that translates anomaly intent into executable, testable detection pipelines. Rather than selecting detectors against an implicit target, SMAD establishes a natural-language semantic contract grounded in measurable time-series properties, specifying target event patterns, expected score responses, and validation questions. This contract governs both detector construction and evaluation: LLM agents propose pipelines, while contract-matched synthetic interventions provide label-free evidence from the target series to assess semantic coverage and guide iterative self-refinement. A semantics-aware ensemble integrates validated candidates and attributes each alert to contributing detectors and supporting evidence. On univariate and multivariate TSAD benchmarks, SMAD improves detection performance over the strongest baseline by 11.5% and 9.6%, respectively, while outperforming general-purpose coding agents at 2-5x lower language-model token cost.


### arXiv 2611.28341 - When Tools Fail, Agents Fabricate: Silent Failure and Verification-First Oversight in Tool-Using LLM Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.SE
- published: 2026-11-20T21:47:43Z
- authors: Gün Kaynar, Boshen Yan, Carl Kingsford
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28341

Tool-using LLM agents must recover when a tool call fails, by retrying, switching tools, or revising their plan. We study how agents fail when tools fail, using RetryRealistic, a sandboxed yet realistic testbed for controlled fault injection in which agents operate on a real OS filesystem with genuine permission enforcement, SQLite databases, a deterministic local HTTP server, and a constrained shell, across 8 tools, 9 fault types with controlled persistence, and 30 multi-step tasks. Evaluating four agents over 2,100 faulted runs against ground truth, we find that recovery is unreliable (19-68% correct) and frequently silent: 6-26% of runs end in a confident fabrication, a wrong answer asserted as fact. The failure mode is governed more by the fault type than by the model: faults that return partial data drive fabrication (up to 38%), while hard denials drive honest failure. The mechanism is what we call silent ingestion: the agent accepts faulted data as valid on first contact without retrying or flagging the problem, then builds a fabricated answer on top of it. We show that fabrication is reducible: a verification-first Monitor, grounding checks in structured tool outputs and issuing corrective directives rather than hard blocks, raises correct recovery from 45.6% to 56.3% and cuts fabrication from 17.3% to 10.8%. Removing chain-of-thought increases fabrication (+8.3pp), so deliberation guards against silent ingestion. We release RetryRealistic, the fault-injection toolkit, and all annotated traces at https://github.com/Kingsford-Group/RetryRealistic


### arXiv 2611.28324 - MatHarness: Towards Long-Horizon Open-ended AutoResearch for Computational Materials Science

- categories: cs.AI, cond-mat.mtrl-sci
- published: 2026-11-20T21:24:27Z
- authors: Yanlin Fei, Lei Li, Shaolong Chen, Nazhou Liu, Xinmiao Yu, Madalina Ciobanu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28324

Materials science research is a non-linear, long-horizon process. Existing scientific agents, however, largely rely on specific task execution or predefined workflows, rendering them brittle in open-ended settings. To bridge this gap, we present MatHarness, an agent harness designed for open-ended, long-horizon computational materials research spanning the complete lifecycle from initial ideation to manuscript writing. MatHarness couples three mechanisms: execution robustness through pre-flight verification, expert-distilled skills, and just-in-time auditing; epistemic adaptability through persistent research artifacts and an evolving scientific mental model; and collective intelligence through a heterogeneous multi-agent round table under targeted human oversight. We evaluate MatHarness on adsorbate-induced surface segregation in Cu-Zn alloys, an open question in methanol-synthesis catalysis. Over a multi-week trajectory consuming roughly 10^3 GPU-hours (50 production DFT relaxations prescreened by 171 machine-learning-potential evaluations) the system revised its plan six times, recovered autonomously from failed calculations, and had an independent auditor overturn one of its own over-attributed claims before publication. Within the sampled metallic dilute-limit models, the validated DFT analysis supports an adsorbate-induced tendency toward greater surface Cu exposure at both composition endpoints, with facet- and coverage-dependent exceptions. These results were compiled into a complete manuscript with human involvement concentrated in specification, approval, and strategic steering gates. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/PrentisAI/pippin.


### arXiv 2611.28176 - Med-Agentic: Distilling Agentic Medical Reasoning with Internalized Meta-Capabilities

- categories: cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-20T06:30:52Z
- authors: Yucheng Zhou, Junwei Sheng, Jianbing Shen
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28176

Large language models (LLMs) in expert domains face a trade-off between agentic tool use, which externalizes expertise behind brittle multi-turn runtimes, and end-to-end specialization, which internalizes expertise only implicitly at task-level granularity. We propose internalized meta-capabilities: atomic, parametrically internalized, and composable reasoning units that an LLM can select and combine within a single chain-of-thought (CoT). We instantiate this idea as Med-Agentic for medical visual diagnosis, where the model emits a capability-tagged structured CoT in one forward pass, turning capability invocation into tagged token generation rather than external tool calls. For training, Med-OPD performs multi-teacher on-policy distillation with token-level dual-factor teacher routing conditioned on both the image and the active capability tag, strictly generalizing per-prompt teacher routing. We show that this routing gives an unbiased estimator of the same expected teacher-KL gradient. Across public medical imaging datasets spanning nine anatomies and eight modalities, Med-Agentic consistently improves in-distribution and out-of-distribution diagnostic accuracy over the strong competitors.

- 1.0.6 @ 2026-11-18T08:06:30Z :: ## What's Changed ### v1.x security fixes 1.0.6 is a security release for the deprecated v1 line. Upgrade with `npx @modelcontextprotocol/inspector@v1-latest`, or move to v2 (`@latest`). * **A malicious MCP server can no longer crash the proxy by streaming an unbounded server-sent event.** The proxy now caps how much of an upstream SSE stream it will buffer. A single line, or a single event, longer than 16 MiB closes that upstream connection with an error instead of growing the proxy's heap until it runs out of memory and drops every session. This covers the `sse` and `streamable-http` transports, including a standalone GET stream with the wrong content-type. The cap sits well above any legitimate message (four times the SDK reference servers' 4 MB inbound limit). ([GHSA-3xgv-mvj6-g78r](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/inspector/security/advisories/GHSA-3xgv-mvj6-g78r)) by @cliffh

- 2.15.0 @ 2026-11-12T00:46:29Z :: ## What's Changed ### Security fixes 2.15.0 fixes four vulnerabilities. The fetch-guard and event-stream fixes affect every v2 release from 2.0.0 through 2.14.0, the mcpdo daemon fix affects 2.10.0 through 2.14.0, and the server.json preview fix affects 2.14.0. Upgrade with `npx @modelcontextprotocol/inspector@latest`. * **The fetch guard blocks cloud metadata addresses outside `169.254.0.0/16`**, including `100.100.100.200` and `192.0.0.192` and their decimal and IPv4-mapped IPv6 forms, so neither the `/api/fetch` proxy nor OAuth discovery can be pointed at them ([GHSA-fvg8-f358-w2c8](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/inspector/security/advisories/GHSA-fvg8-f358-w2c8)) * **The network tracker caps the body it records for a POST event-stream response**, so a server streaming an unbounded response can no longer exhaust the web backend's memory and take down every session ([GHSA-9w48

- 1.0.5 @ 2026-11-11T21:39:41Z :: ## What's Changed ### v1.x security fixes 1.0.5 is a security release for the deprecated v1 line. Upgrade with `npx @modelcontextprotocol/inspector@v1-latest`, or move to v2 (`@latest`). * **The proxy now refuses two more fixed cloud metadata addresses.** Its connection guard already blocked `169.254.0.0/16`. It now also blocks `100.100.100.200` (Alibaba Cloud ECS) and `192.0.0.192` (Oracle Cloud Classic), including their IPv4-mapped IPv6 forms. The surrounding `100.64.0.0/10` range stays reachable, so Tailscale and other CGNAT targets still work. - v2.2.0 @ 2026-09-07T15:53:57Z :: `pip install -U mcp`. Docs: https://py.sdk.modelcontextprotocol.io/ A few defaults changed in this release. If you run a server or client on 2.x, skim these first: ## Behaviour changes **HTTP client redirects are only followed within the endpoint's origin** (#3397) - `Client("https://...")`, `streamable_http_client` and `sse_client` follow a redirect only if it stays on the same scheme, host and port (or upgrades `http` to `https` on the same host). - A redirect anywhere else is not followed: the call fails with `MCPError` and the session stays usable (an SSE connect fails with `httpx2.HTTPStatusError`). ### modelcontextprotocol-go-sdk

- v1.9.0-pre.1 @ 2026-11-17T10:02:09Z :: In this release we introduce fixes, hardening, and small API additions on top of [v1.8.0](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk/releases/tag/v1.8.0). It adds no new protocol revision: `2026-07-28` remains the newest version the SDK negotiates. Most changes tighten spec compliance (standard JSON-RPC error codes, exact JSON number decoding without `float64` rounding, SEP-2243 HTTP headers, MRTR, and OAuth/OIDC validation), bound peer-controlled inputs, and add hooks for stream keep-alives, per-session notifications, and middleware. Eight new `MCPGODEBUG` flags let you revert individual behavior changes, and seven flags from `v1.7.0` are removed as scheduled; see below. ## Hardening HTTP clients no longer follow cross-origin or method-changing redirects unless the `http.Client` sets its own `CheckRedirect` (`StreamableClientTransport`, `SSEClientTransport`, and OAuth requests). The

- v1.8.0 @ 2026-09-14T08:05:46Z :: _**This release is equivalent to v1.8.0-pre.2. Thank you to those who tested the pre-release.**_ In this release we introduce several fixes and improvements on top of [v1.7.0](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk/releases/tag/v1.7.0). It adds no new protocol revision: the supported set is unchanged, and `2026-07-28` remains the newest version the SDK negotiates. The bulk of the work is hardening the transports against resource exhaustion, closing session leaks, deadlocks and teardown hangs found by users running the new protocol at scale, and giving servers explicit control over which protocol versions they advertise. ### anthropics-anthropic-sdk-python

- v2.14.0 @ 2026-11-24T21:00:57Z :: ### Features * **api:** add Google-managed data controls for Google Cloud projects * **api:** add Google-managed data controls for user profiles * **api:** add support for compliance review cases, notices, and items ### Bug Fixes * **client:** honor proxy and trust_env when proxy variables are set `DefaultHttpxClient` and `DefaultAsyncHttpxClient` read `HTTP_PROXY`, `HTTPS_PROXY`, `ALL_PROXY` and `NO_PROXY` even when the caller passed `proxy=` or `trust_env=False`, and the variables won. Now `proxy=` is used for every request and the variables are not read, and `trust_env=False` ignores them, as in plain `httpx2`. What you may notice: with `proxy=` set, a host listed in `NO_PROXY` used to be reached directly and now goes through your proxy. To keep a host off the proxy, pass `mounts={"all://that.host": None}`. ### Chores * **docs:** drop the provisional naming note from the Google Cloud

- v2.12.0 @ 2026-11-20T15:56:21Z :: ### Features * **api:** add the image_dimensions_too_large error code ### Bug Fixes * **bedrock:** send the user profile ID in the request body Amazon Bedrock reads `user_profile_id` from the request body and ignores the `anthropic-user-profile-id` header, so a profile ID given to `AnthropicBedrock` or `AsyncAnthropicBedrock` was not applied. Both clients now move it into the body of Messages requests and no longer send the header. A `user_profile_id` already in the body takes priority over the header. Some models on Bedrock do not support user profiles. A request to one of them with a profile ID now fails with a 400 error; before, the ID was ignored.
