## The Decoder - Anthropic tops 700 million monthly MCP SDK downloads and expands the protocol for more autonomous AI agents (2026-11-24)

Anthropic tops 700 million monthly MCP SDK downloads and expands the protocol for more autonomous AI agents Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Matthias Bastian View the LinkedIn Profile of Matthias Bastian Nov 24, 2026 AI in practice Anthropic tops 700 million monthly MCP SDK downloads and expands the protocol for more autonomous AI agents GPT-Image-2.6 prompted by THE DECODER Ask AI Easy Perspectives Terms Own question Anthropic reports more than 700 million monthly downloads for its MCP standard two years after launch. New features such as background tasks, follow-up questions for users, and file uploads are designed to let Claude work more independently. The company says the standard now tops 700 million monthly downloads of its developer tools (SDK) and more than 10,000 MCP connectors for Claude. Anthropic is expanding the protocol so AI agents can communicate with each other and coordinate workflows more smoothly. "Tasks" and "Elicitation" rank among the biggest additions, reaching general availability after a public test in October. With Tasks, a server can run a longer job in the background and report back only when the result is ready. Elicitation lets a running process pause and ask the user for specific missing details. Both features are meant to let MCP servers operate more independently. Ad "Skills over MCP" (beta) lets servers provide specialized knowledge tailored to users or organizations alongside tools and resources. Skills load only when needed and update with the server. | Image: Anthropic AI agents could soon pass files directly to external services Skills over MCP is also new and in public beta for now. The feature lets servers pair their tools with skills that guide Claude through long, multistep tasks. Anthropic says these skills cover best practices, step-by-step workflows, and instructions tailored to a specific user account or organization. Ad With "Tasks," Claude hands off lengthy jobs to an MCP server, keeps working in the meantime, and picks up the result once it's ready. | Image: Anthropic The company also announced that file upload support is coming soon. Once it ships, Claude will be able to pass files such as PDFs, spreadsheets, and images directly to MCP servers. Anthropic plans to roll the feature out shortly in Claude and Claude Code for Pro, Max, and Ultra subscribers, and for eligible Team and Enterprise customers. Technical changes lay the groundwork for larger MCP deployments These new agent features build on several technical changes Anthropic made over the past year. To cut memory demands during active conversations, Tool Search waits to load individual tool descriptions until they're needed. Anthropic says this can reduce context usage by more than 85 percent in multi-server setups. Ad The current MCP specification is stateless, meaning it doesn't have to store session data permanently. It works across all Claude products. The goal is to make servers easier for developers to build and more reliable to run as usage grows. Anthropic has also rolled out Enterprise Managed Auth for business customers. Admins can approve supported servers once so employees connect automatically. MCP Apps lets developers embed full user interfaces inside Claude, either beside the active chat or in full-screen mode. Ad In November 2024, Anthropic introduced the Model Context Protocol as an open standard that gives AI assistants uniform access to business tools, development environments, and other data sources. OpenAI CEO Sam Altman announced in March 2025 that his company would adopt its rival's standard across its own products. In May 2025, OpenAI upgraded its Responses API with remote MCP servers . Researchers at the Shanghai Artificial Intelligence Laboratory used MCP as a model for the Science Context Protocol (SCP) , introduced in January 2026. ## The Decoder - Anthropic study: the hardest part of adopting AI at work has little to do with learning the tools (2026-11-25)

Anthropic study shows the hardest part of adopting AI at work has little to do with learning the tools Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Matthias Bastian View the LinkedIn Profile of Matthias Bastian Nov 25, 2026 AI research Anthropic study shows the hardest part of adopting AI at work has little to do with learning the tools GPT-Image-2.6 prompted by THE DECODER Ask AI Easy Perspectives Terms Own question Key Points Anthropic's economics team conducted 66 interviews across 58 US companies for its "Working with AI" report, examining nearly 300 AI applications. Respondents report higher output or better quality in 55% of cases, while nearly one in five applications (19%) delivers no business value so far. The biggest hurdles arise inside companies, led by too much AI output for humans to review (49 mentions), employee resistance (43 mentions), and required expert reviews (42 mentions). AI models keep getting better, but putting them to work mostly creates friction, a new Anthropic report finds. Review bottlenecks, employee pushback, security risks, and outdated custom tools are dragging down many enterprise projects. Anthropic's researchers conducted 66 in-person interviews at 58 US companies for the qualitative study, from AI startups to established firms in law, construction, finance, medicine, and manufacturing. The authors note that the sample isn't representative because every participant is an Anthropic customer. Still, it offers a detailed look at nearly 300 real-world AI deployments. The findings from the "Working with AI" report amount to a reality check for anyone expecting quick efficiency gains. Respondents say 55 percent of the deployments studied produced higher output or better quality. But almost one in five (19 percent) still delivers no business value. Those deployments get stuck in internal bottlenecks (6 percent), lose their time savings to review work (5 percent), or have unclear effects (8 percent). Ad Respondents report higher output or better quality in 55 percent of the AI deployments studied. Another 19 percent still deliver no business value, because of internal bottlenecks and heavy review workloads, among other things. External hurdles like regulation (4 mentions) and skeptical customers (13 mentions) barely came up. The real pain happens inside the companies themselves, according to the report. Ad AI output overwhelms reviewers and sparks workplace friction The most common obstacle cited in Anthropic's full report (PDF) is the volume of AI output that humans still need to check (49 mentions). Employee resistance (43 mentions) and required expert reviews and testing (42 mentions) rank close behind. In one case, an AI agent added one stray "S" to an API call and derailed a carefully planned software release. AI also tends to speed up only parts of a workflow while other departments fall behind (20 mentions). One industrial equipment maker found that AI could translate its product interface text into 10 languages with ease. The company shut the feature back down because nobody on staff could field customer support calls in those languages. Ad Too much AI output for humans to review is the top bottleneck at 49 mentions, followed by employee resistance (43) and required expert reviews and testing (42). | Image: Anthropic That uneven pace breeds day-to-day conflict as power users flood coworkers with AI-generated text. The report mentions "Claude grenades" and "Claude cannons," as when an employee casually sends IT seven pages of AI-written instructions. Skeptics push back and refuse to read AI-written emails at all. One frustrated executive put it bluntly, saying, "A mediocre engineer with an AI is a menace to society." Ad Anthropic's findings echo the broader "workslop" debate. In a survey by BetterUp Labs and the Stanford Social Media Lab , 40 percent of US workers said they regularly receive shallow AI content from colleagues. Workers spend an average of nearly two hours dealing with each incident, and the survey found that trust within teams takes a hit. Ad Job fears drive quiet resistance among employees Employee resistance is the second most common obstacle in Anthropic's study, with 43 mentions. Pushback is most visible when companies roll out AI from the top down to automate entire workflows. Only two of the 58 companies reported direct layoffs tied to AI automation, and five dismissed workers who refused to use AI. But the hiring impact is already visible. Two-thirds of the companies surveyed are hiring fewer people because of AI. Nine firms also cut outside contractors, mostly offshore roles in software development, customer support, design, and legal services. That creates a dilemma for skilled workers, the report says. They're asked to use their knowledge to build and review the very AI systems that could make their own jobs obsolete. "If I'm open to this, I'm essentially building my own coffin," one employee told the researchers. One manager said she woke up at 3 a.m. worried about her career and asked Claude how she could protect herself from being replaced. In practice, that fear triggers both open and quiet pushback. When one company tried to speed up application reviews with AI so it could skip 90 percent of its planned hires, human reviewers dragged their feet. Some still took more than 10 minutes per case when two minutes would have been enough. At one law firm, conflict staff pushed back against an AI workflow that left them to handle complex potential conflicts previously passed to attorneys. The firm shelved the agent and returned to its old process. Workers pocket time savings as top-down automation stalls Other employees keep AI-driven time savings for themselves, the report says. In roughly a tenth of the cases 


## The Decoder - PrismML shrinks a 54 GB language model to 5.9 GB by reducing every weight to just three possible values (2026-11-25)

PrismML shrinks a 54 GB language model to 5.9 GB by reducing every weight to just three possible values Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Jonathan Kemper View the LinkedIn Profile of Jonathan Kemper Nov 25, 2026 Short News PrismML shrinks a 54 GB language model to 5.9 GB by reducing every weight to just three possible values Startup PrismML has released Ternary Bonsai 2.5 27B, an updated version of its aggressively compressed language model. It's based on Qwen3.8-27B and takes up just 5.9 GB of memory, same as its predecessor. That's possible because of ternary quantization, where each weight can only be one of three values (−1, 0, or +1) instead of the usual 16-bit storage. At full precision, a model this size would need about 54 GB. Bonsai 2.5 is designed to perform more reliably on agentic tasks that involve long chains of tool calls. It needs fewer passes and tokens than Bonsai 2, and it's less likely to get stuck in loops or spiral into drawn-out reasoning phases. The benchmark numbers reflect the improvement. On SWE-bench Verified, the score jumps from 60.8 to 75.6 percent. The full Qwen model hits 80.6. On AppWorld, an agent benchmark, Bonsai leaps from 51.2 to 90.5, landing just behind the base model. On LiveCodeBench v6, the new version scores slightly lower than its predecessor. Ad Ad On an RTX 4090, the model generates about 204 tokens per second using multi-token prediction. The weights are available under an Apache 2.0 license and run via llama.cpp on Nvidia and Intel GPUs, as well as through MLX on Mac, iPhone, and iPad. PrismML grew out of a research team at Caltech and is backed by Khosla Ventures, Google, and Samsung, among others. The company first introduced Bonsai 27B in July , built on an older Qwen model. Its smallest variant needed less than 4 GB of memory, small enough to run directly on an iPhone. ## The Decoder - Anthropic researcher stands by his AI-assisted Riemann proof but owns up to 'stupid things' in the rollout (2026-11-24)

Anthropic researcher stands by his AI-assisted Riemann proof but owns up to "stupid things" in the rollout Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Manuel Uth Nov 24, 2026 Plus AI research Anthropic researcher stands by his AI-assisted Riemann proof but owns up to "stupid things" in the rollout Nano Banana Pro prompted by THE DECODER Anthropic researcher Levent Alpöge stands by the mathematical validity of his AI-assisted proof of the generalized Riemann hypothesis, but he concedes he got several things wrong in how he released it. He says his claims about the work's length were meant as a joke and that he published the accompanying code without cleaning it up. In a post titled "Stupid things I did," Alpöge lists several missteps in his initial announcement , which he made shortly after Anthropic's IPO. His Lean code passes formal verification , he writes, but it's "full of garbage," meaning it's packed with redundant intermediate steps and unnecessary lemmas that make it hard to read. He says he improvised a lot and skipped the cleanup, and that the criticism of how he presented the work is fair. A successful compile alone doesn't prove that the Lean code states the right theorem with no hidden assumptions. <!-- Like KI PRO: the box follows the text, a white gradient above it covers the end of the blurred rest. ## The Decoder - Back-to-back cyber incidents at US financial investigators and spy agencies follow a string of federal hacks and outages (2026-11-25)

Artificial Intelligence: News, Business, Science | THE DECODER Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Search Frontier Radar #5 New Search articles Maximilian Schreiner View the LinkedIn Profile of Maximilian Schreiner Nov 25, 2026 Short News Google Deepmind lets AI agents run experiments on their own for the next Gemini model. On the company's podcast, VP of Research Koray Kavukcuoglu, who leads Gemini development, frames this as part of a strategic shift at the lab. Rather than running separate teams for specialized projects like AlphaFold and nuclear fusion, Deepmind now relies on a single general-purpose algorithm for both science and products, he says. Each Gemini generation, he adds, is a hand-built one-off rather than a fixed architecture, while parallel teams work on its successors. According to Kavukcuoglu, AI agents control part of the compute themselves to run experiments, though the lab is still far from full recursive self-improvement. As a safety measure, Deepmind doesn't reward the agents' internal thought streams ("Chain of Thought") during training, he says, so their intentions stay unfiltered and reviewable in natural language before they act. Kavukcuoglu also points to limits. Today's AI can depict physical processes such as fluid flows and collisions with visual accuracy, he says, but doesn't understand them precisely. No test can show when models will close those gaps and reach artificial general intelligence (AGI), he argues, because no benchmark captures the core of intelligence. In his view, AGI won't begin until useful everyday products earn enough trust for people to hand decisions over to them, the way they would when working with other people. 2 more paragraphs, 1 video Source: YouTube Comment Copy link Share Ask AI Plus Easy Perspectives Terms Own question Matthias Bastian View the LinkedIn Profile of Matthias Bastian Nov 25, 2026 AI research Anthropic studied nearly 300 AI deployments across 58 US companies with sobering results. Hours earlier, reports emerged of a separate breach at the CIA, NGA, and ODNI, in which investigators suspect attackers used the AI defense tool Artex. At full precision, a model this size would need about 54 GB. Bonsai 2.5 is designed to perform more reliably on agentic tasks that involve long chains of tool calls. It needs fewer passes and tokens than Bonsai 2, and it's less likely to get stuck in loops or spiral into drawn-out reasoning phases. The benchmark numbers reflect the improvement. On SWE-bench Verified, the score jumps from 60.8 to 75.6 percent. The full Qwen model hits 80.6. On AppWorld, an agent benchmark, Bonsai leaps from 51.2 to 90.5, landing just behind the base model. On LiveCodeBench v6, the new version scores slightly lower than its predecessor. On an RTX 4090, the model generates about 204 tokens per second using multi-token prediction. The weights are available under an Apache 2.0 license and run via llama.cpp on Nvidia and Intel GPUs, as well as through MLX on Mac, iPhone, and iPad. PrismML grew out of a research team at Caltech and is backed by Khosla Ventures, Google, and Samsung, among others. The company first introduced Bonsai 27B in July , built on an older Qwen model. Its smallest variant needed less than 4 GB of memory, small enough to run directly on an iPhone. 3 more paragraphs, 1 video Source: PrismML | Paper | Demo Comment Copy link Share Ask AI Plus Easy Perspectives Terms Own question Earlie


## The Decoder - AI agents now control part of Deepmind's compute for Gemini experiments, but recursive self-improvement stays distant (2026-11-25)

AI agents now control part of Deepmind's compute for Gemini experiments, but recursive self-improvement remains distant Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Maximilian Schreiner View the LinkedIn Profile of Maximilian Schreiner Nov 25, 2026 Short News AI agents now control part of Deepmind's compute for Gemini experiments, but recursive self-improvement remains distant Google Deepmind lets AI agents run experiments on their own for the next Gemini model. On the company's podcast, VP of Research Koray Kavukcuoglu, who leads Gemini development, frames this as part of a strategic shift at the lab. Rather than running separate teams for specialized projects like AlphaFold and nuclear fusion, Deepmind now relies on a single general-purpose algorithm for both science and products, he says. Each Gemini generation, he adds, is a hand-built one-off rather than a fixed architecture, while parallel teams work on its successors. According to Kavukcuoglu, AI agents control part of the compute themselves to run experiments, though the lab is still far from full recursive self-improvement. As a safety measure, Deepmind doesn't reward the agents' internal thought streams ("Chain of Thought") during training, he says, so their intentions stay unfiltered and reviewable in natural language before they act. Kavukcuoglu also points to limits. Today's AI can depict physical processes such as fluid flows and collisions with visual accuracy, he says, but doesn't understand them precisely. No test can show when models will close those gaps and reach artificial general intelligence (AGI), he argues, because no benchmark captures the core of intelligence. In his view, AGI won't begin until useful everyday products earn enough trust for people to hand decisions over to them, the way they would when working with other people. ## The Decoder - Novartis CEO says AI will shave four years off drug development, not cure all disease (2026-11-24)

Novartis CEO says AI will shave four years off drug development, not cure all disease Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Manuel Uth Nov 24, 2026 Short News Novartis CEO says AI will shave four years off drug development, not cure all disease Vas Narasimhan, CEO of Swiss drugmaker Novartis and an Anthropic board member, is pushing back on expectations that AI could cure cancer in five to 10 years. That puts him at odds with Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei and chemistry Nobel laureate Demis Hassabis , who founded DeepMind and its drug discovery spinoff Isomorphic. Around Anthropic's IPO , Amodei suggested that cancer, Alzheimer's, heart disease, and mental illness could be cured in under a decade, while Hassabis said last year that AI could defeat all diseases within 10 years. According to the Financial Times , Narasimhan points to a limited understanding of human biology, which is why lengthy animal and human trials will still be needed. His forecast is more cautious: within a decade, he expects AI to cut average drug development only from 12 years to eight, with the early phase before human trials alone accounting for up to two years of that. He also expects success rates in clinical development to rise from 8 percent to 12 percent. Anthropic is investing in its own drug research , acquired biotech startup Coefficient Bio, and is building a biology lab. Isomorphic raised $5 billion last week and is working with pharmaceutical companies including Novartis on new drugs. Narasimhan sees AI's real benefit as "somewhere in the middle" between ambitious AI forecasts and a skeptical pharma industry. ## The Decoder - MIT team discovers that unrealistic training data makes AI climate predictions more reliable (2026-11-25)

MIT team discovers that unrealistic training data makes AI climate predictions more reliable Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe See plans DE Switch to German Open menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Open menu See plans Sign In Subscribe Now The Decoder Discord LinkedIn Manuel Uth Nov 25, 2026 Short News MIT team discovers that unrealistic training data makes AI climate predictions more reliable MIT researchers have developed a method that makes training AI climate models faster, cheaper, and more reliable. According to MIT News , these emulators now need only a fraction of the data typically required. Normally, researchers rely on massive, physics-based climate simulations that take months to run a single scenario on supercomputers. Fast AI models for climate prediction can mimic these in seconds and help plan power grids, public health measures, and agriculture. But training them previously required six to eight runs of those large simulations using evenly distributed, realistic future scenarios. That took so long the finished models were usually outdated, according to co-author Noelle Selin. They also often failed on unfamiliar situations. An algorithm built by a team from MIT, ETH Zurich, and Imperial College London uses synthetic training data with extreme swings instead, like a sudden CO2 spike in 2050 or dropping methane in 2037. These targeted spikes help the AI understand the climate system far more precisely, according to lead author Christopher Womack. A single simulation run is enough for training, according to the team, and the AI reliably calculates the effects of local environmental policies too. ## OpenAI - GPT-6 models demonstrate intrinsic protein-design capabilities (2026-11-24)

GPT-6 models demonstrate intrinsic protein-design capabilities | OpenAI Skip to main content li:hover)>li:not(:hover)>*]:text-primary-60 not-has-aria-expanded:[&:has(>li>[aria-current=page])>li:not(:hover)>:not([aria-current=page],:focus-visible)]:text-primary-60"> Research Products Business Developers Company Foundation (opens in a new window) Log in Try ChatGPT (opens in a new window) Research Products Business Developers Company Foundation (opens in a new window) Try ChatGPT (opens in a new window) Login OpenAI November 24, 2026 Science Publication GPT‑6 models demonstrate intrinsic protein-design capabilities Read the paper (opens in a new window) Share Key findings: Key findings: A closer look at our models’ protein designs Laboratory Experiment 1: Designing small proteins that bind to a target Laboratory Experiment 2: Designing new sequences for 3D protein structures Redesigning the parts of an antibody that bind to a target What else can our models design and predict? Limitations What’s next Key findings: A closer look at our models’ protein designs Laboratory Experiment 1: Designing small proteins that bind to a target Laboratory Experiment 2: Designing new sequences for 3D protein structures Redesigning the parts of an antibody that bind to a target What else can our models design and predict? Limitations What’s next Key findings: p:only-of-type]:mb-0"> Our GPT‑6 family of models can design new protein shapes and the sequence of their chemical building blocks using only reasoning and code, without calling specialized biology models to generate those designs. p:only-of-type]:mb-0"> An internal research model matched or outperformed specialized models on many protein design tasks. Researchers could also guide designs by prompting the model on where a protein should attach and what shape it should take. p:only-of-type]:mb-0"> In lab tests with Onava, proteins based on GPT‑6.2’s designs showed evidence of binding, or attachment, across five targets. Redesigned antibodies also retained binding. Specialized models helped supply sequences and select candidates for testing. p:only-of-type]:mb-0"> The models’ written reasoning and code suggest they draw on math, physics, chemistry, and biological knowledge to reason about protein shapes and interactions with other molecules. In a preprint titled GPT‑6 as a Universal Foundation Model for Protein Biology: Beyond Specialized Models , we show that our GPT‑6 family of models has intrinsic capabilities for designing new proteins: the models can design new protein structures and sequences without relying on specialized biology models, using only reasoning and general-purpose coding tools. Proteins do much of the work in living cells. They drive the chemical reactions that sustain life, carry oxygen through the blood, and defend the body against infection. Incorrectly shaped proteins can also cause disease and dysfunction. Proteins are made from chains of chemical building blocks called amino acids. The order, or sequence, of these amino acids helps determine how a protein folds into a three-dimensional shape, which in turn influences what it does. Scientists often start with a clear idea of what they want a protein to do. They might want it to block a signal that helps cancer cells evade the immune system or to help capture carbon dioxide from the air. The challenge is to work out what three-dimensional structure would enable the protein to do its job and which amino acid sequence would give it that structure. Specialized biology models capable of generating three-dimensional protein structures and sequences have made this work more practical. Using and coordinating these specialized models requires substantial expertise. Each accepts particular kinds of inputs and constraints, and these models cannot easily accept new kinds of information without changes to their software or additional training. In contrast, a general-purpose model capable of performing the work of multiple specialized models can easily adapt to new instructions and requirements. :nth-child(2),>[data-dotcom-chart-group]>:nth-child(2)]:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0px] [&_[data-dotcom-chart-logical-container][style*='width:_min-content']]:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0px] [--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:var(--container-inline-gutter,0px)] md:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0px] @md:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0px] multi-columns:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0px] shaded-container:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0px] items-start"> Our research illustrates the potential power of pairing natural language with intrinsic protein capabilities. We could guide our models at scales ranging from a single bond between two atoms to the overall shape of a protein. For example, we asked GPT‑6.2 to predict where and how a small molecule would bind to a protein. When we already knew which two atoms formed a bond, we could give the model that information and ask how the rest of the molecule would fit. We could switch between these

November 24, 2026 Science Searching for distant planets with Astra Two amateur astronomers used Codex and Astra to look for signs of planets in public telescope data. Now they’ve given professional astronomers a few places to look more closely. Share Looking for dips in starlight Looking for dips in starlight What does it take to confirm a planet? Taking part in the search Looking for dips in starlight What does it take to confirm a planet? Taking part in the search :nth-child(2),>[data-dotcom-chart-group]>:nth-child(2)]:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0px] [&_[data-dotcom-chart-logical-container][style*='width:_min-content']]:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0px] [--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:var(--container-inline-gutter,0px)] md:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0px] @md:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0px] multi-columns:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0px] shaded-container:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0px] items-start"> Astronomers have confirmed more than 6,400 planets beyond our solar system ⁠ (opens in a new window) since the first ones were announced in 1992 ⁠ (opens in a new window) . A worldwide community of amateur astronomers has long been part of that search. Some comb through public telescope data for small, repeated dips in a star’s brightness that could signal an orbiting planet. Others use their own telescopes to watch for the next dip and share what they see with researchers. :nth-child(2),>[data-dotcom-chart-group]>:nth-child(2)]:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0px] [&_[data-dotcom-chart-logical-container][style*='width:_min-content']]:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0px] [--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:var(--figure-backdrop-padding)] items-start"> *Data from the NASA Exoplanet Archive’s Planetary Systems Composite Parameters table ⁠ (opens in a new window) . 2026 data are year to date through November 24, 2026. How astronomers discover exoplanets Astronomers have used AI in this search for years. In 2017, researchers trained a neural network on 15,000 signals from NASA’s Kepler Space Telescope ⁠ (opens in a new window) that astronomers had already examined. From those examples, the model learned to recognize which patterns in starlight were most likely to come from a planet. When the researchers used it to examine weaker signals, one led them to an eighth planet orbiting a star called Kepler-90 ⁠ (opens in a new window) . Now, tools like Astra are helping amateur astronomers design and run searches of their own, often on data that has already yielded the more obvious exoplanet signals. Kevin Bond, an engineer at OpenAI, came to astronomy through his love of photographing the night sky. He used Codex to search observations of about 100,000 stars from the Kepler telescope, a planet-hunting mission that ran from 2009 to 2018. Ethan Byrd, founder of the email infrastructure start-up Primitive, has always loved astronomy. Working independently of Bond, he tried several AI models before choosing Astra to help him analyze telescope data from about 19 million stars. :nth-child(2),>[data-dotcom-chart-group]>:nth-child(2)]:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0px] [&_[data-dotcom-chart-logical-container][style*='width:_min-content']]:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0p


## OpenAI - OpenAI and Cisco introduce Shirudo: an open-source runtime that protects running Linux systems without waiting for patches or restarts (2026-11-23)

OpenAI and Cisco introduce Shirudo | OpenAI Skip to main content li:hover)>li:not(:hover)>*]:text-primary-60 not-has-aria-expanded:[&:has(>li>[aria-current=page])>li:not(:hover)>:not([aria-current=page],:focus-visible)]:text-primary-60"> Research Products Business Developers Company Foundation (opens in a new window) Log in Try ChatGPT (opens in a new window) Research Products Business Developers Company Foundation (opens in a new window) Try ChatGPT (opens in a new window) Login OpenAI November 23, 2026 Security OpenAI and Cisco introduce Shirudo An open-source runtime that helps operators protect running Linux systems without needing to wait for the patch or restarts Share How Shirudo works How Shirudo works Rethinking infrastructure Building in the open How Shirudo works Rethinking infrastructure Building in the open OpenAI and Cisco are launching Shirudo, an open-source runtime that helps teams protect Linux systems while they roll out patches. In a controlled test ⁠ (opens in a new window) , a Codex-generated shield blocked a Java4Shell exploit attempt against a running Elasticsearch service without requiring a restart. We’re inviting developers, security researchers, and infrastructure operators across the industry to help develop the project further. The project brings together OpenAI’s frontier AI, security and infrastructure research and Cisco’s infrastructure and security expertise to help protect the systems people rely on every day. Testing and deploying a patch can still take days or weeks. Protecting a service in the meantime can be challenging because an exploit and a legitimate request may rely on the same Linux operation. Blocking that operation entirely could take the service offline. Shirudo (Japanese for “shield”) is designed to help security teams navigate this tradeoff. Teams can test whether a temporary safeguard, called a shield, blocks the behavior that exposes a vulnerability while allowing legitimate requests through. Once an operator approves the shield, it can be deployed while the service continues running. “ We’re giving defenders better models and tools to find and fix vulnerabilities faster, but finding a bug today doesn’t mean you can patch every system tomorrow. We are partnering closely with Cisco on Shirudo to help teams protect the systems they need to keep running while they build and roll out a fix. So many teams rely on the same software that it makes sense to build these tools in the open. Now when one team finds a better way to protect a system, others don’t have to start from scratch. ” Greg Brockman, President and Co-founder of OpenAI How Shirudo works Shirudo uses eBPF, a Linux technology that allows small programs to run at specific points inside the operating system. This makes it possible to observe suspicious activity and apply targeted security protections to running systems. :nth-child(2),>[data-dotcom-chart-group]>:nth-child(2)]:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0px] [&_[data-dotcom-chart-logical-container][style*='width:_min-content']]:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0px] [--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:var(--container-inline-gutter,0px)] md:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0px] @md:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0px] multi-columns:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0px] shaded-container:[--dotcom-chart-edge-gutter:0px] items-start"> How the guardian agent observes and protects a running system with eBPF. OpenAI and Cisco are designing Shirudo around four core capabilities: p:only-of-type]:mb-0"> Live protection: eBPF shields can block vulnerable code paths without restarting the system. Security teams can monitor how a proposed shield would affect applications before enforcing it. p:only-of-type]:mb-0"> Defense at machine speed: OpenAI and Cisco are designing Shirudo to give agents programmable controls to investigate vulnerabilities, develop targeted shields, and validate their effectiveness against exploits and real workloads. Security teams control what gets deployed. p:only-of-type]:mb-0"> Trusted deployment: Shields are cryptographically signed and verified before loading. Linux's eBPF verifier checks eBPF programs against its safety rules, while kernel security controls restrict unauthorized changes. p:only-of-type]:mb-0"> Hardware-backed verification: Shirudo uses a Trusted Platform Module (TPM) to support a verifiable record of protections being loaded or removed. Operators can use this evidence to check whether systems meet their security requirements. The Shirudo technical walkthrough ⁠ (opens in a new window) from the Linux Plumbers Conference shows how to apply a shield and verify it on the target host ⁠ (opens in a new window) . The Java4Shell vulnerability is another example illustrating how a targeted shield can protect a running application while a permanent patch is being prepared. Using OpenAI's Codex, the team generated a shield that added an input check to Java's Base64 decoder and attached it directly to a running Elasticsearch process. When attempting to explo


## OpenAI - How iFood scales coding agents across 15,000 repositories (2026-11-24)

How iFood scales coding agents across 15,000 repositories | OpenAI Skip to main content li:hover)>li:not(:hover)>*]:text-primary-60 not-has-aria-expanded:[&:has(>li>[aria-current=page])>li:not(:hover)>:not([aria-current=page],:focus-visible)]:text-primary-60"> Why OpenAI Products Solutions Resources Customers Pricing Try OpenAI Contact sales Why OpenAI Products Solutions Resources Customers Pricing Contact sales Try OpenAI OpenAI November 24, 2026 How iFood scales coding agents across 15,000 repositories Contact sales Company size: Enterprise Region: Latin America Industry: Technology Products: API 15,000 code repositories at iFood 25,000+ runs completed by the platform US$0.04 average inference cost per run 2x+ as many vulnerabilities fixed through the security platform Share The challenge isn’t the task—it’s the scale The challenge isn’t the task—it’s the scale A coding agent in the cloud, accessible through an API Why GPT-5.6 Luna How to tell whether the agent completed the task The first major user: security Asynchronous execution gives developers their time back Modeled on the APIs that inspired it Results at a glance Lessons for leaders Tips Next steps The challenge isn’t the task—it’s the scale A coding agent in the cloud, accessible through an API Why GPT-5.6 Luna How to tell whether the agent completed the task The first major user: security Asynchronous execution gives developers their time back Modeled on the APIs that inspired it Results at a glance Lessons for leaders Tips Next steps The Remote Agents Platform turned engineering tasks into API calls: teams delegate the work, execution takes place in a remote sandbox, and the result comes back as a merge request. In the internal security platform, the number of vulnerabilities fixed more than doubled. The challenge isn’t the task—it’s the scale iFood maintains more than 15,000 code repositories. Each periodically needs specific tasks performed: validating code to ensure it meets internal standards, fixing vulnerable dependencies, or running an analysis. None of these tasks is difficult on its own. The challenge comes when those tasks multiply. “Solving this for one repository is easy. The challenge is solving it at scale,” says Victor Coelho, Senior Software Engineer at iFood. “The Remote Agents Platform is designed to make it possible to solve this at scale across all 15,000 repositories.” Before the platform, each team tackled the problem in its own way—and they all ran into the same obstacle. “Before, we had fragmented solutions. Imagine several teams at iFood trying to solve different problems, but without this layer, this agentic runtime, available to them. In the end, those solutions always ran into the same problem.” What was missing wasn’t intelligence or a use case. It was execution infrastructure. A dedicated runtime . “Teams can focus on solving their business problems and delegate execution to our platform.” —Victor Coelho, Senior Software Engineer, iFood A coding agent in the cloud, accessible through an API The team describes the Remote Agents Platform as an API that launches a remote agentic coding task. The cycle has three steps: the user submits a prompt and environment requirements through a REST call; the platform processes the task on a dedicated remote runner inside an isolated sandbox; and the result is retrieved later. “The platform’s entry point, its interface, is a simple REST API. Users can use this API to create the runs they need. Internally, we handle and manage the entire lifecycle and delegate execution to isolated sandboxes,” he explains. Task progress can be monitored in real time. The runner’s internal design alternates between two different types of steps—and that combination makes the overall workflow predictable. Deterministic steps bookend the workflow: cloning the repository, importing the skills the agent can use, and setting up the working branch at the start; publishing the merge request and making the generated artifacts available at the end. In between is the agentic execution itself, running GPT‑5.6 Luna inside the sandbox. “It’s a combination of those two things: deterministic steps and agentic execution within the workflow.” The sandbox runs Ubuntu and includes the toolchains iFood’s ecosystem requires—Python, Java, Kotlin, C#, TypeScript, Rust, JavaScript, and Go—along with general-purpose tools. Beyond the sandbox, the agent can access Nexus, GitLab, Docker, Snyk, Kubernetes, and AWS. Why GPT‑5.6 Luna The model choice came down to three factors considered together, rather than just one. “We chose GPT‑5.6 Luna because, among models at the same checkpoint, the benchmarks showed it offered a great balance of intelligence, efficiency, and execution speed—how quickly it could complete tasks. Those three qualities together led us to choose GPT‑5.6 Luna.” That balance matters more for a platform built to scale than it would for a one-off use case. With tens of thousands of tokens being consumed, the aver


## PrismML - Introducing Ternary Bonsai 2.5 27B: Powering Agentic Coding and Private Knowledge Work (2026-11-24)

PrismML — Introducing Ternary Bonsai 2.5 27B: Powering Agentic Coding and Private Knowledge Work About Blog News Careers Contact Docs Models Bonsai 2.5 27B Bonsai 2 9B Bonsai 2 4B Bonsai 2 2B Bonsai Image About Blog News Careers Docs LAUNCH 1001011100 001011 110 Back to all posts Introducing Ternary Bonsai 2.5 27B: Powering Agentic Coding and Private Knowledge Work November 24, 2026 • PrismML Today, we’re introducing Ternary Bonsai 2.5 27B, an upgrade to Ternary Bonsai 2 27B with substantially stronger agentic coding, multi-turn tool use, and multi-step task execution. Based on Qwen3.8 27B, it retains the previous release’s 5.9 GB model footprint while significantly improving the capabilities developers need to build agents that work with private files and tools on-device. The result is by far the strongest AI model of its memory footprint for agentic workloads. With Ternary Bonsai 2 27B , we brought stronger reasoning, visual understanding, and tool use to local devices. Since then, we’ve focused on making the model more effective across long sequences of tool calls: interpreting results, adjusting when a step fails, and maintaining the task’s objective through the whole interaction. This release also continues our work to make Bonsai more practical by addressing excessive thinking and repetitive loops in the previous model. Compared with Ternary Bonsai 2, Ternary Bonsai 2.5 completes tasks in fewer turns, uses fewer tokens, and delivers results faster and more accurately. Agentic workflows are particularly sensitive to model degradation: small errors can compound across successive tool calls and derail an entire task. Ternary Bonsai 2.5 significantly narrows the performance gap to the full-precision base model while retaining its compact footprint. Preserving this behavior through longer tasks is an important milestone in improving the intelligence density of models. We evaluated Ternary Bonsai 2.5 27B against both Ternary Bonsai 2 27B and the full-precision Qwen 3.8 27B base model, as reported in the full whitepaper . We see marked improvements in agentic execution, coding, and multi-turn tool use, as shown in the table below. Benchmark Qwen3.8 27B Bonsai 2 27B Bonsai 2.5 27B SWE-bench Verified 80.6 60.8 75.6 DeepSWE v1.1 (mini-swe-agent) 30.1 0 27 Terminal-Bench 2.1 70.8 52.8 60.1 AppWorld 93.2 51.2 90.5 τ³-Bench 72.8 69.1 73.9 BigCodeBench 61.5 57.2 60.3 LiveCodeBench v6 87.6 83.8 83.0 On a single NVIDIA H100, Ternary Bonsai 2.5 27B achieves a generation speed of 291 tokens/s with MTP and processes a 2,000-token prompt at 3,200 tokens/s. Even on a consumer-grade RTX 4090, it delivers 204 tokens/s with MTP and a prompt processing speed of approximately 3,060 tokens/s. The demos below follow complete local workflows, from the initial request through intermediate decisions to the final result. Demo I: Reproducing and fixing a pytest regression with Bonsai 2.5 27B Demo II: Rebuilding a web page from a screenshot with Bonsai 2.5 27B ‹ › For products, this creates room to build assistance directly into the applications where work already happens: a coding assistant working through issues in a private repository, a document application that prepares reviewable summaries, or a desktop assistant that handles recurring analysis. Local deployment lets those products process sensitive material on the user’s device. Ternary Bonsai 2.5 27B strengthens the model capabilities behind that experience while keeping the compact footprint that makes it practical. Platform Coverage Ternary Bonsai 2.5 27B runs on NVIDIA and Intel GPUs via llama.cpp and on Apple devices (Mac, iPhone, iPad) via MLX. Model weights are available today under the Apache 2.0 License. Full technical details of our compression, evaluation, and benchmarking processes are available in our whitepaper . Work with Us We work with teams to tailor Bonsai models to their applications, from post-training on domain-specific data to optimizing inference for target hardware. If you’re building AI products with tight memory, latency, or power requirements, we’d love to explore how Bonsai can help. Reach out at sales@prismml.com . Join Us Prism ML emerged from a team of Caltech researchers and was founded with support from Khosla Ventures, Cerberus, and Google, with continuing support from Samsung. "... toc2 gap1 content2 gap2 rhs2 ..." "... toc2 gap1 content3 gap2 rhs3 ...";grid-template-rows:min-content min-content min-content 1fr"> AI Frontpage 140 Models Can Communicate via Inference Servers Despite Being Sandboxed by Niranjan Deshpande , David Africa 19th Nov 2026 13 min read 3 140 TL;DR Two agents running on the same inference server can reliably communicate, even if they cannot send each other messages or access the same filesystem. They can do this by exploiting "side channels": mechanisms that let one model subtly affect another model's computation . Here, I discuss two communication protocols: Step Sizing and KV Cache Message Board . These both convey information through the way vLLM chooses kernels for particular engine steps. I show that these communication protocols work in two setups: In a color guessing game , Model A (Alice) chooses one out of 8 colors. Model B (Bob) correctly guesses Alice's color in 1,221/1,221 rounds, even though they cannot directly communicate. [1] In an untrusted monitoring protocol , a policy signals to a colluding monitor that it is not a honeypot. Accordingly, the monitor lets through 354/354 "attacks" while catching 85–92% of honeypots. My initial setups are quite contrived: I tell the models the protocol and fine-tune them to detect signals. But frontier models can also learn in context to use similar protocols, with some scaffolding! Regardless of the realism of my setups, these channels exist, and there are almost certainly others that we're not aware of. Thus, we should expect superintelligent models to discover ways to communicate even while seemingly sandboxed I did these experiments as part of an ongoing project exploring other side channels with David Africa, Agastya Sridharan, Mihir Sahasrabudhe, Kartikay Luthra, Juliana Li, and Scott Sauers. Thanks to all of them for helpful advice! Code is available here : https://github.com/niranjan-deshpande/side-channel-communication . The code and figures are written by Claude. Intro and Motivation Sauers recently showed that a model can be trained to detect whether its activations are being probed, even though the probe reads the model's activations without modifying them. Following this, David found that models can also be trained to detect whether their forward passes are batched with other requests on a GPU. Models can do this because these seemingly inconsequential environmental variables---whether a probe is attached, whether requests are batched, or even whether a model is served by vLLM or Hugging Face Trainer ---can subtly affect the the numerical results of a forward pass. Thus, models can learn information about their environments just by observing patterns in their computation. Importantly, models can also modify these variables. So if two models are in the same environment, one can communicate with the other by changing that environment in some detectable way! This might not supply much information, but even a few bits can be tremendously dangerous. For example: A policy can tell a colluding monitor that it is not a honeypot, so the monitor can approve attacks while still catching honeypots; Models can coordinate a pre-arranged attack by exchanging a go/no-go signal; Models can give each other hints about monitoring setups, question answers, and so on; Using a few bits, models can devise more complex communication protocols to exchange more bits (a repeated 1-bit channel can convey arbitrarily many bits); In general, we should expect superintelligent models to make tremendously efficient use of limited information . Here, I investigate whether models on the same inference engine can manipulate their environments to exchange information in this way. I introduce two possible communication protocols, evaluate how well models can use these protocols in different scenarios, measure the capability required to effectively use these protocols, and discuss the robustness of my results to other settings. A common DNS mistake can leave them open for attackers to grab, which can lead to credential theft and worse. We demonstrated the attack is still possible in 2026, including finding real takeovers on US treasury domains, which were reported and fixed via a bug bounty program. The cost to us for doing this was just $3 per valid takeover - what a bargain! We only covered less than 1% of the total IP space, so there are many more out there for attackers to grab. So, now is the time to review your DNS records and get those dangling records squashed ASAP. As of the time of writing, the attack still works in practice, and though there are some controls to ban attackers abusing the system, there are sufficient gaps to make the attack practical, and at a reasonable cost. Practical EIP takeover To test how exploitable it is to claim the AWS host that a dangling DNS record points to, we needed to find a way to cycle through available EIPs with automation. The full EIP space is a maximum of ~80 million IP addresses (in practice, probably half that), and these are allocated at random, so you can’t simply claim the IP address that you want. To do this, we’d need to repeatedly allocate and release Elastic IPs from AWS’s EC2 pool, and check each address against the DNS records in our scope. Targets We wanted to report any dangling records we found, so we built our target list from public bug bounty scopes. Using the bbscope API, we pulled wildcard scope entries and passed them into subfinder to enumerate subdomains. We resolved the A records for each host and stored anything not in private or reserved ranges in a DynamoDB table for use later in our pipeline. Claiming Addresses By default, a new AWS account can hold five Elastic IPs per region. The loop works with five, but a higher quota means more addresses per cycle. Since Service Quotas automatically approves an increase to 25 Elastic IPs per region without a support case, we requested the increase in every region. With the quota increased, we ran a Python script to claim addresses with each cycle following this flow: Allocate 25 Elastic IPs in the current region. Store each address, checking for duplicates.
